is a monoid where the multiplication is the composition of operators and the unit is the identity operator
1
.)

The
pre-references

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
are the weak*-continuous bounded linear maps
from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
.
(The
weak*-topology
on the space of bounded operators is the coarsest topology where
x\mapsto\lvert{\operatorname{tr}ax}\rvert
is continuous for all trace-class operators
a
(§20 in
[
Con00
]
). In other words, a net
x_{i}
weak*-converges to
x
iff for all trace class
a
,
\operatorname{tr}ax_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}ax
.
Weak*-continuous bounded linear maps are also known as
normal
maps
[
Tak79
, Def. III.2.15]
.)

The tensor product on
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
is the

tensor product of von Neumann algebras
, that is,
\mathbf{A}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\otimes$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\otimes$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\otimes$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\otimes$}}}\mathbf{B}
is the set
B(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
of
bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. (In
[
Tak79
]
,
this tensor product is defined in Definition IV.1.3 as
“
\overline{\otimes}
”.
The fact that
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}=B(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
is given by (10) in the same section.)
For any
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
b\in\mathbf{B}
,
a\otimes b
is defined as the
unique operator such that

(a\otimes b)(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes b\phi
for all

\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A},\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
. (The tensor product on the rhs of
this equation is the Hilbert space tensor product
[
Tak79
, Definition IV.1.2]
.
Existence of
a\otimes b
is shown in the discussion after that definition.)

The
references
are
the weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphisms. (Unital
means
F(1)=1
,
*
-homomorphism means linear,
F(ab)=F(a)F(b)
and
F(a^{\dagger})=F(a)^{\dagger}
.)

We give the proofs that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
is a reference category in
Appendix C
.

Note that for finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, the definitions here coincide with those from
Section 4
since over finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, all operators are bounded, and all linear maps are bounded and weak*-continuous, and the tensor product of von-Neumann algebras coincides with the algebraic tensor product.

Example, specialized

The explanations from
Section 4.1
how to instantiate the example from
Section 3
in the quantum case also apply in the infinite-dimensional settings, with very minor differences:
The Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{T}
corresponding to type
T
is the space
\ell_{2}(T)
of square-summable sequences
T\to\mathbb{C}

(instead of
\mathcal{H}_{T}:=\mathbb{C}^{T}
).
The update monoids are the bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{T}

(instead of the linear operators).
Program semantics in the pure state semantics are bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{T}

(instead of the linear operators).

\mathbf{Sem}_{T}
consists of the completely positive
bounded-linear
maps
T(\mathcal{H}_{T})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{T})
where
T(\mathcal{H}_{T})
are the trace-class operators over
\mathcal{H}_{T}

(instead of the linear operators).
Everything else applies unchanged.

(Since for finite
T
,
\mathcal{H}_{T}=\mathbb{C}^{T}
, and the bounded and trace-class operators coincide with the linear operators, and all linear maps are bounded-linear, the instantiation from
Section 4.1
is a special case of this instantiation.)

4.3
Discussion

Using other tensor products (in the infinite-dimensional case).

In
Section 4.2
, we chose the tensor product of von Neumann algebras as the tensor product in our category.
As a consequence, the morphisms we considered were
weak*-continuous
bounded linear maps. (This is because the von Neumann tensor product is the weak*-closure of the algebraic tensor product.)
However, this is not the only possible tensor product on spaces of bounded operators. Other tensor products exist that are the closure of the algebraic tensor product with respect to other topologies. For example, the projective C*-tensor product
[
Tak79
, Definition IV.4.5]

satisfies the property required for Axiom
•
‣
1
without requiring
weak*-continuous
maps
[
Tak79
, Proposition IV.4.7]
. So it is well conceivable that we can define a category of quantum references also with this tensor product. Or we could forgo any topological considerations and try to use the algebraic tensor product, and allow all linear maps (not just bounded ones). The reason why we chose the tensor product of von Neumann algebras is that it satisfies
B(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes B(\mathcal{H}_{B})=B(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
.
This makes the resulting theory much more natural to use:
If we have two references
F,G
, with types
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, then it would be natural that the pair
FG
has type
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. E.g., a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
is something we expect to be able to apply to the reference
FG
. It seems that the tensor product of von Neumann algebras is the only one that gives us this property.
However, we do not exclude that instantiations of the theory of references with other tensor products might work and give us other advantages that we are not currently aware of.

Mixed classical/quantum references.

Another interesting question is to extend the theory of quantum references to mixed classical/quantum references.
For example, we could have a reference that contains a list of qubits, where the length of the list is classical.
Our current formalism does not allow that.
(We can only model references that contain lists of qubits where the length of the list is also in superposition.) In particular, with the current theory, classical references (see
Section 5
) are not a special case of quantum ones.
At a first glance, it would seem that there is already a ready-made solution available for this problem: Mixed classical/quantum systems are oftentimes represented by von Neumann algebras (i.e., certain subsets of the sets of all bounded operators).
For example, a system consisting of
n
qubits would be represented by
B(\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}})
, same as we do in the present paper.
And a system of classical lists of qubits then would be the direct sum of all possible systems of
n
-qubits for
n\geq 0
. That is,
\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}B(\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}})
.
And a classical bit could be
B(\mathbb{C})\oplus B(\mathbb{C})
.
In fact, classical systems are exactly the commutative von Neumann algebras!
Unfortunately, this approach seems unsuitable in our setting.

To see this, consider the special case of classical systems where the program state is purely classical.
I.e., it is modeled by some commutative von Neumann algebra
\mathbf{C}
.
But that means that for any two references
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
, the ranges of
F
and
G
commute trivially.
Hence any two references in a program with classical state would be disjoint.
This shows us that we are on the wrong track.
The intuitive meaning of disjointness is that
F,G
corresponds to different parts of the program state, and this should clearly not be the case for any two references. (In particular,
F
should not be disjoint from itself except in the degenerate case where
F
has unit type.)

Furthermore, modeling classical references as commutative von Neumann algebras also fails to represent the intuitive meaning of a “set of updates”. Updates on a classical reference should in some way correspond to classical functions on some set
X
. In particular, different updates should not always commute. But in a commutative von Neumann algebra, everything commutes.

(Note that we have only illustrated why the approach fails for classical data. But since classical data is a necessary special case of mixed quantum/classical data, this also implies that the approach does not work for mixed data.)

What went wrong? And why is the von Neumann algebra formalism (as sketched above) successful in modeling mixed classical/quantum data in other settings? The reason, as we understand it, is that this von Neumann algebra formalism is based on a very different intuition: Elements of the von Neumann algebra represent
observables
. For example, in
B(\mathcal{H})
, we find the Hermitian operators
a
on
\mathcal{H}
, and each such operator gives rise to a function from quantum states to real numbers, via
\operatorname{tr}a\rho
or
\psi^{\dagger}a\psi
, depending on whether we consider a mixed state
\rho
or a pure state
\psi
. Physically, this corresponds to the expected value of the real-valued measurement described by
a
. And a classical system with values
X
is described by the Neumann algebra consisting of all bounded complex sequences
a_{x}
(
x\in X
). For any classical ‘‘state’’, namely a discrete
18
18

18

We assume
discrete
measure spaces to keep this example simple.
probability distribution
\mu
on
X
, any (real-valued)
a_{x}
also gives rise to a real number via
\sum_{x}\mu(x)a_{x}
, namely the expectation of
a_{x}
where
x
is the state of the system.

So an element of a von Neumann algebra (in this formalism) describes a
measurement
on the system.
I.e., it tells us how to
read
from it. In contrast, in our setting, we also need to know how to
write
to the system (since we describe ‘‘updates’’).
This is basically the same difference as between rvalues and lvalues in classical programming languages. Knowing how to read a reference is not, in general, sufficient for knowing how to write it.
19
19

19

For example, for a classical reference, we can describe how to read it by giving a function
f:M\to X
from program states
M
to reference contents
X
. Yet, from this function
f
, it is not possible to reconstruct how updates on the reference should happen.

So we claim that the fact that our quantum reference category has the
same objects and morphisms as existing work that uses the von Neumann algebra formalism (e.g.,
[
Kor17
,
CW16
,
P“’ec+20
]
to mention just a few) is coincidental and only happens when we consider purely quantum systems. Namely, in our setting, the objects are the linear span of all unitary transformations (a unitary is a natural representation of an “update”), while in the other work, the objects are the linear span of all Hermitian operators (a Hermitian is a natural representation of a real-valued measurement). Those two spans happen to coincide, in both cases we get all bounded operators. However, one should be careful to read too much into this coincidence. In particular, if we leave the purely quantum setting, we do not have any such correspondence anymore. (Consider for example the stark difference of our modeling of classical references (partial functions on sets, see
Section 5
) and the modeling of classical systems via commutative von Neumann algebras (complex sequences).)
20
20

20

One further thing that seems to be coincidence is the use of weak*-continuous unital *-isomorphisms. For example, in
[
P“’ec+20
, Section 5.4]
, a denotational semantics for quantum “values”
v
backed by a memory of qubits is given.
The underlying intuition seems to be that it describes how an observable on
v
is transformed into an observable on the program state.
This denotational semantics
\llbracket v\rrbracket
of
v
is given by a weak*-continuous unital *-isomorphism from the von Neumann algebra
\mathbf{V}
corresponding to
v
to the von Neumann algebra corresponding to a collection of qubits (i.e.,
B(\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}})
),
exactly as in our category
.
Yet we claim that this is another coincidence due to the fact that we consider only pure quantum systems. Namely, in
[
P“’ec+20
]
,
\llbracket v\rrbracket
is not always an injective function. E.g.,
\llbracket\mathbf{left}\dots\rrbracket
(left injection into a sum type) is noninjective. On the other hand, in our setting, noninjective references would make little sense. This would mean that two updates that have different effects on a specific reference can have the same effect on the overall state. So, in fact, one could argue that references in our setting should actually be described as
injective
weak*-continuous unital *-isomorphisms. It just so happens that in our case (where the only von Neumann algebras we consider are the sets of all bounded operators),

injective
weak*-continuous unital *-isomorphisms coincide with
weak*-continuous unital *-isomorphisms (
Lemma 25
).
This is not the case in general!
So the fact that our set of morphisms coincides with those from
[
P“’ec+20
]
is again just an artifact of the fact that we consider only pure quantum systems.
(As a final remark note that the modeling from
[
P“’ec+20
]
also does not give rise to a way of pairing two values in the same way as we do with references. In
[
P“’ec+20
]
, when combining two values into a pair, the two values need to be specified on distinct collections of qubits that then get concatenated. See the fifth rule in Figure 7 in
[
P“’ec+20
]
.
This is more similar to our tensor product of references than to our pairing operation.)

So in light of this, to formalize mixed classical/quantum references, we will need to use a different category than that of von Neumann algebras. At this point, it is not clear yet what this category looks like.

5
Classical references

We come to the classical case. The most obvious approach would be to say that the updates on a reference taking values in
A
are the functions
A\to A
. However, it is not possible to satisfy the axioms of a reference category for this choice.
21
21

21

If updates are the function sets
A\to A
, and the tensor product is defined in the natural way as
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}:=(A\times B)\to(A\times B)
,
(a\otimes b)(x,y):=(a(x),b(y))
, the axioms are contradictory for any definition of pre-references/references:
Let
A:=\{{0,1}\}
.
Let
F_{i}:\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}
for
i=0,1
be defined as
F_{i}(z):=f\circ z\circ g_{i}
where
f(xy):=x0
and
g_{i}(00):=0i
and
g_{i}=00
everywhere else.
By Axioms
•
‣
1
and
•
‣
1
,

F_{i}
are pre-references. Let
z(xy):=yx
. Then
F_{i}(z)(00)=i0
, so
F_{0}\neq F_{1}
.
For any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{A}
, we have

F_{i}(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})(xy)=f\circ(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})(00)
for
xy\neq 00
and
F_{i}(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})(00)=z_{1}(0)0
. Thus
F_{0}(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})=F_{1}(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})
for all
z_{1},z_{2}
.
By Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
, this implies
F_{0}=F_{1}
in contradiction to
F_{0}\neq F_{1}
above.
Instead, we define the updates on
A
as the
partial
functions
A{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}A
.
This choice has the additional advantage that it also directly provides support for partiality in the program semantics; the semantics of a deterministic partial program is often naturally described as a partial function.
(An earlier version
[
Unr21a
]
of this work
formalized classical updates as relations on
A
instead. We believe that our new approach is more natural and easier to use. Both approaches are viable, however.)
In this section, we use the notational
convention that
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\dots
denote the sets of partial functions on
A,B,\dots
.

To define references
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, let us forget for a moment that a reference should map updates to updates.
Instead, we note that a classical reference with values in
A
inside a memory of type
B
naturally allows us to perform two actions:
We can read the value of the reference using a function
g:B\to A
(the
getter
).
And we can set the value of the reference using a function
s:A\times B\to B
(the
setter
).
The getter and setter satisfy some natural conditions: We call
(g,s)

valid
iff for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
b\in B
, we have

b=s(g(b),b)
and
g(s(a,b))=a
and
s(a,s(a^{\prime},b))=s(a,b)
.
This is also known as a lens.
A lens is a natural candidate for formalizing classical references.
We show that this approach indeed fits in our formalism.
A getter/setter pair
(g,s)
naturally gives rise to a function
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
via
F(a)(b):=s(a(g(b)),b)
. (I.e., to apply
a
to the reference, we retrieve its content
g(b)
, apply
a
to it, and set the result as the new content of the reference.) Note that
F(a)
can be a partial function if
a
is, but
F(a)
is total for total
a
. Thus the
references
are the functions
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
such that there exist valid
(g,s)
with
\forall a\in\mathbf{A},b\in B.\ F(a)(b)=s(a(g(b)),b)
.

In order to define pre-references, we somewhat relax these conditions. We cannot define the pre-references
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
as all functions
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
since then Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
would not hold. Instead, we define

pre-references
as the functions
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
such that there exist a total
g:B\to A
and a partial
s:A\times B\rightharpoonup B
with
\forall a\in\mathbf{A},b\in B.\ F(a)(b)=s(a(g(b)),b)
. (Note: we removed the validity requirement, and we allow
s
to be partial. With total
s
, Axiom
•
‣
1
would not hold.)

Finally, we need to define the tensor product of updates.
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
is the set of partial functions on
A\times B
. (This is the only natural choice since we want a pair of references taking values in
A,B
, respectively, to take values in
A\times B
.) Then
(a\otimes b)(x,y):=(a(x),b(y))
, defined iff both
a(x),b(y)
are defined, makes this into a tensor product satisfying all our axioms.

Note that the pairs
FG
in this category are very natural, too: If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
are defined by the getters/setters
(g_{F},s_{F})
and
(g_{G},s_{G})
, respectively, then
FG
is defined
by the getter/setter
(g,s)
with
g(c)=(g_{F}(c),g_{G}(c))
and
s((a,b),c)=s_{F}(a,s_{G}(b,c))
.

With these choices, we get a reference category
\textstyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{classical}}
satisfying all axioms from
Figure 1
and the laws from
Figure 2
follow.

Furthermore, for singleton sets
A
, have a
unit reference

u:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
that is disjoint from all references, and such that setting it has no effect.

We formalized the proofs for this section in Isabelle/HOL (
Section 10
), so we do not give them here.

5.1
Example, specialized

In
Section 3
, we described how the semantics of a small programming language can be based on references.
That example was abstract, assuming a generic reference category.

In the classical setting, there are several possibilities to make it concrete:

Deterministic semantics.

The simplest possibility is to describe a language where the semantics of programs are deterministic.

In this case, the types
T
in the language could correspond to finite sets (e.g.,
\mathtt{bool}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{{0,1}\}
) with
\times
being the Cartesian product and the update monoids
\mathbf{T}
are the partial functions
T\rightharpoonup T
.
The reference category is
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{classical}}
.

This gives us a language where
\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ X
means
X:=U(X)
for a function
U
.
(Of course, in the classical case a notation such as
X:=f(X)
would be more intuitive.)
The semantics of a program on memory of type
T
is described by a partial function
T\rightharpoonup T
.
(Here it comes in handy that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{classical}}
uses
partial
functions in the update monoids since total functions would restrict us to a language where we cannot have non-terminating programs.)

The various programs and example calculations and results then carry over immediately to this concrete setting. (With
U_{\sigma}(x,y):=(y,x)
being a function.)

Probabilistic semantics.

If we want our language to support, e.g., random choices, deterministic semantics are not sufficient.
In this case, we want the semantics to map a program state to a distribution over program states.
That is, it is of type
T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
where
\mathcal{D}_{T}
is the set of functions
f:T\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\sum_{x\in T}f(x)\leq 1
.
For simplicity, we consider only programs where all types are finite (i.e.,
\lvert{T}\rvert<\infty
for all types
T
).

These “probabilistic functions”
f,g:T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
naturally form a monoid with multiplication
(f\cdot g)(x)(z):=\sum_{y}g(x)(y)f(y)(z)
and unit
1\in T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
with
1(x)(x):=1
,
1(x)(y):=0
(
x\neq y
).
This multiplication is the monadic bind and
f\cdot g
intuitively corresponds to the composition of probabilistic functions.

Since the update monoids in the reference category
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{classical}}
consist of partial functions, not probabilistic functions, we cannot use the same mathematical structure for the program semantics and the update monoids.
Instead, we need to make use of the additional flexibility described in
Section 3.3
:

As before, the types
T
in the language could correspond to finite sets, and the update monoids
\mathbf{T}
are the partial functions
T\rightharpoonup T
. The reference category is
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{classical}}
.
But
\mathbf{Sem}_{T}
is now defined as the space of probabilistic functions
T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
.

In particular, the semantics of a program with memory type
M
will be a probabilistic function
M\to\mathcal{D}_{M}
, mapping memories to distributions over memories.

As described in
Section 3.3
, we additionally need to specify:
a monoid homomorphism
\mathbf{Sem}:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{Sem}_{T}
, and a way to apply references to
\mathbf{Sem}_{T}
.
The monoid homomorphism corresponds to interpreting a partial function
f
as a probabilistic function
\mathbf{Sem}(f)
:

\mathbf{Sem}(f)(x)(y):=1
if
f(x)=y
and
:=0
if
f(x)\neq y
.
It is easy to check that this is a monoid homomorphism.

Applying a reference
X
to
\mathbf{Sem}_{T}
is possible, too:
Let
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
denote all functions
T\to\mathbb{R}
(dropping the positivity and the
\leq 1
requirements).
Then the functions
T\to\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
form a real vector space space.
And the probabilistic functions
T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
are a subset of the
T\to\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
.
Furthermore, the functions
\mathbf{Sem}(x\mapsto y)
with
x,y\in T
form a basis of
T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
.
(Here
x\mapsto y
is the partial function defined only at
x
, mapping it to
y
.)
We define
X^{\bullet}
to be the unique linear function on
T\mapsto\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{T}
with
X^{\bullet}(\mathbf{Sem}(x\mapsto y)):=\mathbf{Sem}(X(x\mapsto y))
.
Since
T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
is the convex hull of the
\mathbf{Sem}(x\mapsto y)
, and
\mathbf{Sem}(X(x\mapsto y))\in T\to\mathcal{D}_{T}
,

X^{\bullet}
maps
T\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{T}
to
T\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{T}
.
And we immediately that the required condition
X^{\bullet}(\mathbf{Sem}(f))=\mathbf{Sem}(X(f))
holds for
f
of the form
x\mapsto y
.
We encourage the reader to check that this also holds for all other
f
(because they are a union of functions of the form
x\mapsto y
).

The various programs and example calculations and results then carry over immediately to this concrete setting.

Since we have probabilistic semantics, we can now easily extend the language with, e.g., random bits:
X\leftarrow\mathbf{coin}
assigns a random bit to
X
.
22
22

22

Typing: In the nano language,
X
needs to be a reference
\mathbf{T}_{\mathtt{bool}}\to\mathbf{T}_{M}
.
In the micro language, the following typing rule is added:

\frac{X:\mathbf{T}_{\texttt{bool}}\to\mathbf{T}_{M}\otimes\mathbf{T}_{L}\text{ is a reference}}{T_{M},T_{L},\mathfrak{P}\vdash X\leftarrow\mathbf{coin}}
.

It would have the following semantics:

\llbracket X\leftarrow\mathbf{coin}\rrbracket:=X^{\bullet}(f_{\mathit{coin}})\qquad\text{with}\qquad\forall x,y.\ f_{\mathit{coin}}(x)(y):=\frac{1}{2}.

6
Quantum Hoare logic

To illustrate the use and flexibility of our references, we will now
develop a simple quantum Hoare logic.
Since the main focus is to show the use of quantum variables (and constructions based upon these), the language we analyze will not contain any features that are orthogonal to the question of variables, e.g., loops.
The ideas
described can be easily extended to other Hoare logics that use
subspaces as pre-/postconditions (i.e., von-Neumann-Birkhoff quantum logic
[
BN36
]
;
used, e.g., in
[
Unr19a
,
ZYY19
,
Unr19
,
Li+20
]
). (And Hoare logics
that use operators as pre-/postconditions
[
DP06
,
Fen+07
,
Yin12
,
LU19
,
Bar+19
]

should be even easier to adapt because references
naturally translate operators from variables to overall program
states.)

Language definition.

Our simple language has two
commands: applying a unitary, and branching based on a measurement.
Fix a space
\mathcal{H}_{M}
for the program state.

Statements
are of the form:

P,Q::=\mathbf{skip}\ |\ P;Q\ |\ \mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ F\ |\ \mathbf{if}\ \bigl({{G}={x}}\bigr)\ {P}\ \mathbf{else}\ {Q}

We assume that the following type constraints are satisfied:
U
is a
unitary on some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
;
F
is a reference
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{M}
for the same
\mathcal{H}_{A}
;
x\in B
for some
B
with
\lvert{B}\rvert=2
; and
G
is a reference
\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{M}
where
\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\mathbb{C}^{B}
.
(Here
\mathbf{M}
is fixed throughout and corresponds to the program state.)

We use pure state semantics. That is, given a pure state
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{M}
,
\llbracket P\rrbracket
maps
\psi
to a list of pure states
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{M}
.
The interpretation is that the final state is
\psi_{i}/\lVert{\psi_{i}}\rVert
with probability
\lVert{\psi_{i}}\rVert^{2}
.
Or equivalently that the final state is described by the density operator
\sum_{i}\psi_{i}\psi_{i}^{\dagger}
.

\textstyle\mathbf{skip}
denotes the empty program.
Hence
\llbracket\mathbf{skip}\rrbracket(\psi):=(\psi)
where
(\psi)
denotes a one-element list.

P;Q
the execution of
P
followed by
Q
.
That is, if
\llbracket P\rrbracket(\psi)=(\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n})
, then
\llbracket P;Q\rrbracket(\psi):=\llbracket Q\rrbracket(\psi_{1})\|\dots\|\llbracket Q\rrbracket(\psi_{n})
.
(
\|
is the concatenation of lists.)

The command
\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ F
applies the unitary
U
to the variable
F
.
Recall that, since
F
is a quantum reference,
F(U)
describes the operation applied to the overall program state when we apply
U
to the content of
F
.
Thus the denotational semantics is simply
\llbracket\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ F\rrbracket(\psi):=(F(U)\psi)
.
Note that we do not need to define a statement
\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ F,G
that applies a unitary on two registers since we can get the same effect by just applying
U
to the pair
FG
.
That is, without having any explicit provision for it in the definition of the language, we can write
\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ FG
to apply
U
to more than one variable.

And
\mathbf{if}\ ({{G}={x}})\ {P}\ \mathbf{else}\ {Q}
measures
G
in the computational basis, and if the outcome is
x
, executes
P
, and
Q
otherwise.

The denotation is
\llbracket\mathbf{if}\ ({{G}={x}})\ {P}\ \mathbf{else}\ {Q}\rrbracket(\psi):=\llbracket P\rrbracket(G(\lvert{x}\rangle\langle{x}\rvert)\psi)\ \big\|\ \llbracket Q\rrbracket(G(\lvert{y}\rangle\langle{y}\rvert))
where
y\neq x
.
(Recall that
x\in B
for some
\lvert{B}\rvert=2
, so
y\neq x
is uniquely defined.)
Note that
G(\lvert{x}\rangle\langle{x}\rvert)\psi
is the non-normalized post-measurement state after measuring the content of
G
with outcome
x
.

Hoare judgments.

For subspaces
A,B
of
\mathcal{H}_{M}
and a
program
P
, we write
\{{A}\}{P}\{{B}\}
to mean: for all
\psi\in A
,

\llbracket P\rrbracket(\psi)\subseteq B
.
(Recall that
\llbracket P\rrbracket(\psi)
is the list of all possible final states.)

The following rules follow directly from the semantics:

\displaystyle\frac{\{{A}\}{P_{1}}\{{B}\}\quad\{{B}\}{P_{2}}\{{C}\}}{\{{A}\}{P_{1};P_{2}}\{{C}\}}\quad\textsc{Seq}\qquad\frac{A\subseteq A^{\prime}\quad B^{\prime}\subseteq B\quad\{{A^{\prime}}\}{P}\{{B^{\prime}}\}}{\{{A}\}{P}\{{B}\}}\quad\textsc{Weaken}\qquad\frac{A\subseteq B}{\{{A}\}{\mathbf{skip}}\{{B}\}}\quad\textsc{Skip}

\displaystyle\frac{F(U)\cdot A\subseteq B}{\{{A}\}{\mathbf{apply}\ U\ \mathbf{to}\ F}\{{B}\}}\quad\textsc{Apply}\qquad\frac{\bigl\{{R(\lvert{x}\rangle\langle{x}\rvert)\cdot A}\bigr\}{P}\bigl\{{B}\bigr\}\quad\bigl\{{R(\lvert{y}\rangle\langle{y}\rvert)\cdot A}\bigr\}{Q}\bigl\{{B}\bigr\}\quad x\neq y}{\{{A}\}{\mathbf{if}\ ({{G}={x}})\ {P}\ \mathbf{else}\ {Q}}\{{B}\}}\quad\textsc{If}

Note how in the last two rules, references are used not only in programs, but
also to describe pre-/postconditions. We do not need to introduce any
additional definitions for that since
F(U)
and

G\bigl({P_{S}}\bigr)
already have meaning as operators by
definition of quantum references. (And
\cdot
means the multiplication
of an operator with a subspace.
And
S^{\complement}
is the complement of
S
.)

We can also very easily express the predicate “the content of reference
F
has state
\psi
” (short
F=_{q}\psi
).
Having state
\psi
is represented by the subspace
\operatorname{span}\{{\psi}\}
.
Or equivalently,
\operatorname{im}\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.
And
F
transports this to a subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{M}
.
Namely: for a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{M}
and a state

\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
, we define
F{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\!=_{q}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\!=_{q}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\!=_{q}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\!=_{q}$}}}\psi
as the subspace

\operatorname{im}F(\psi\psi^{\dagger})
of
\mathcal{H}_{M}
.
(Recall the discussion about subspaces in
Section 4
about predicates and references, and see also the more detailed information in
Section 9.2
.)

Finally, we will often need the intersection of subspaces. See
Section 9.2
for facts for reasoning about this in
terms of references and projectors.

In the next section, we show what all of this looks like in a concrete
situation.

6.1
Teleportation

We illustrate the use of quantum references and of our minimal Hoare logic by analyzing quantum teleportation
[
Ben+93
]
.
We chose this example because, while the program is simple, it uses a number of variables that interact nontrivially through unitaries and measurements.
As a quantum circuit, it is illustrated in
Figure 3
left.
The overall effect of
this quantum circuit is to “move” the qubit from wire
X
to wire

\Phi_{2}
, assuming
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
are preinitialized with an EPR
pair
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\beta_{00}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\beta_{00}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\beta_{00}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{00}$}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle
. Note
that there are also two wires
A,B
that represent any additional
state that Alice and Bob may hold.

\beta_{00}
X
A
B
\Phi_{1}
\Phi_{2}
/
/
\mathsf{H}
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.62497pt\kern-4.88191pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.62497pt\kern-4.88191pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.84996pt\kern-4.23051pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.25pt\kern-3.03471pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}
a
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.62497pt\kern-4.88191pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.62497pt\kern-4.88191pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.84996pt\kern-4.23051pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.25pt\kern-3.03471pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\frown}}}
b
\sigma_{x}
if
a=1
\sigma_{z}
if
b=1

\mathsf{Teleport}:=

\mathbf{apply}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \mathbf{to}\ ({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}});

\mathbf{apply}\ \mathsf{H}\ \mathbf{to}\ X;

\mathbf{if}\ ({{\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}}={1}})\ {\{{\mathbf{apply}\ \sigma_{x}\ \mathbf{to}\ \Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}\}}\ \mathbf{else}\ {\mathbf{skip}};

\mathbf{if}\ ({{X}={1}})\ {\{{\mathbf{apply}\ \sigma_{z}\ \mathbf{to}\ \Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}\}}\ \mathbf{else}\ {\mathbf{skip}}

Figure 3:
Teleportation circuit and program.
\textstyle\mathsf{H}
is the Hadamard matrix,
\textstyle\sigma_{x}
is the Pauli-
X
matrix, and
\textstyle\sigma_{z}
is the Pauli-
Z
matrix.

In our simple language, we model this with the program

\mathsf{Teleport}
, see
Figure 3
.
Note
that in this modeling, for the fun of it we decided that instead of
having two qubit variables
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
, we have a single two-qubit
variable
\Phi
, and we access their subparts as

\Phi.\mathsf{Fst},\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}
. We assume that
X,A,B,\Phi
are pairwise
disjoint. Besides that, we make no assumptions about how they are
embedded into the overall program memory.

The claim is that teleportation “moves” a state
\psi
from
X
to

\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}
, if
\Phi
is initialized with an EPR pair. Thus,
with the notation from the previous section, this would be expressed
as

\{{X=_{q}\psi\cap\Phi=_{q}\beta_{00}}\}{\mathsf{Teleport}}\{{\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}=_{q}\psi}\}
. However, this only states that if

X
contains some state
\psi
, not entangled with anything else, then

\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}
will contain it.
It does not cover the case where
X
is entangled with, say, subsystems
A
and
B
in Alice’s and Bob’s labs.
To express a stronger (and harder to analyze in Hoare logic) statement, we can say: If the joint
state of
X,A,B
is
\psi
, then the joint state of

\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A,B
is
\psi
afterwards. In other words, we want to show:

\bigl\{{\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi\cap\Phi=_{q}\beta_{00}}\bigr\}{~\mathsf{Teleport}~}\bigl\{{\bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi}\bigr\}

(1)

We now prove (
1
).
First, we have

{\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi\,\cap\,\Phi=_{q}\beta_{00}}\quad=\quad\underbrace{\Phi\bigl({\beta_{00}\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)}_{=:\,O_{1}}\cdot\underbrace{\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi}_{=:\,\mathord{\spadesuit}}

This follows from the definition of
=_{q}
and the fact that

F(S)\cap\operatorname{im}G(V)=F(P_{S})G(V)
where
P_{S}
is the projector onto
S
(
item ix
).
Next we have

\displaystyle\bigl\{{O_{1}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr\}{~\mathbf{apply}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \mathbf{to}\ ({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}})~}\bigl\{{\underbrace{({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}})(\mathsf{CNOT})\cdot O_{1}}_{=:\,O_{2}}\cdot\,\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\bigl\{{O_{2}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr\}{~\mathbf{apply}\ \mathsf{H}\ \mathbf{to}\ X~}\bigl\{{\underbrace{X(\mathsf{H})\cdot O_{2}}_{=:\,O_{3}}\cdot\,\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr\}

Both judgments follow directly from the Hoare rule
Apply
.

Furthermore, we have
O_{3}=\sum_{a,b}Q_{abab}
where

Q_{aba^{\prime}b^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{2}\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}((\sigma_{z}^{b^{\prime}}\sigma_{x}^{a^{\prime}})^{\dagger})\cdot\bigl({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}\bigr)(U_{\sigma})\cdot\Phi\bigl({\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)
and
\textstyle U_{\sigma}
is the two-qubit swap unitary (
U_{\sigma}\lvert{a,b}\rangle=\lvert{b,a}\rangle
).

This can be shown by rewriting both sides of the equation into the form
X\Phi(\dots)
and then comparing the arguments of
X\Phi
.
That comparison is possible by explicit computation because the arguments are
8\times 8
matrices.
This is automated in Isabelle/HOL.
From
O_{3}=\sum_{a,b}Q_{abab}
we can conclude:

O_{3}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}=\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr).

Here the
\sum
is the sum of closed subspaces, i.e., the least upper bound.
(Applying an operator to a subspace is distributive in the left argument.)

We stress that we could not have directly proven the latter equation by direct computation because the definition of
\mathord{\spadesuit}
involves the infinite-dimensional references
A,B
. Nor could we have directly computed
O_{3}
without rewriting it in the form
({X,\Phi})(\dots)
because
O_{3}
is an infinite-dimensional operator.

Note the interpretation of the postcondition
\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)
:
It means the state is in a superposition of states satisfying
Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}
for different
a,b
.
And each
Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}
means:
The current state is the result of having
\beta_{00}
in reference
\Phi
and
\psi
in
\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)
(as in
\mathord{\spadesuit}
),
then changing
\beta_{00}
into
\lvert{ab}\rangle
(so that
\Phi
contains
\lvert{ab}\rangle
),
Then
X
and
\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}
are swapped, and the inverse of the Pauli matrices
\sigma_{z}^{b}\sigma_{x}^{a}
is applied.
So
except for the application of the Paulis
, we already have the state
\psi
in
\bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)
as desired in the end.
And the content of
\Phi
tells us which Paulis need to be undone.

We next show

\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}{\ \mathbf{if}\ ({{\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}}={1}})\ {\{{\mathbf{apply}\ \sigma_{x}\ \mathbf{to}\ \Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}\}}\ \mathbf{else}\ {\mathbf{skip}}\ }\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}

(2)

That is, we claim that in the state after this program fragment, the superfluous
\sigma_{x}
is not applied anymore, as desired.
(Note the index
0
in
Q_{ab0b}
in the postcondition.)
To show this, by rule
If
, we need to show the following two facts:

\displaystyle\Bigl\{{\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{1}\rangle\langle{1}\rvert)\cdot\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}{\ \mathbf{apply}\ \sigma_{x}\ \mathbf{to}\ \Phi.\mathsf{Snd}\ }\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}

(3)

\displaystyle\Bigl\{{\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert)\cdot\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}{\ \mathbf{skip}\ }\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}

(4)

To show these, we first establish the following auxiliary facts:

\displaystyle\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{a^{\prime}}\rangle\langle{a^{\prime}}\rvert)\cdot Q_{abab}

\displaystyle=\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\,Q_{abab}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\forall aa^{\prime}b

\displaystyle\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}(\sigma_{x})\cdot Q_{ab1b}

\displaystyle=Q_{ab0b}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\forall ab

where
\delta_{aa^{\prime}}=1
if
a=a^{\prime}
, and
0
otherwise.
The first follows by unfolding the definition of
Q_{abab}
, commuting
\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{a^{\prime}}\rangle\langle{a^{\prime}}\rvert)
next to the
\Phi\bigl({\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)
term, and then merging those two terms into
\Phi\bigl({(\lvert{a^{\prime}}\rangle\langle{a^{\prime}}\rvert\otimes 1)\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)=\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\Phi\bigl({\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)
.
The second follows by unfolding the definition of
Q_{abab}
and merging
\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}(\sigma_{x}^{1})
and
\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}((\sigma_{z}^{b}\sigma_{x}^{1})^{\dagger})
, which becomes
\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}((\sigma_{z}^{b}\sigma_{x}^{0})^{\dagger})
.

Now we can prove (
3
): We have

\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}(\sigma_{x})\cdot\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{1}\rangle\langle{1}\rvert)\cdot\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{b}\bigl({Q_{1b0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)\leq\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr).

Here
(*)
follows from the two auxiliary facts.
By rule
Apply
, this implies (
3
).
Similarly, by rule
Skip
, (
4
) is implied by

\Phi.\mathsf{Fst}(\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert)\cdot\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{abab}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{b}\bigl({Q_{0b0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)\leq\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr).

Here
(*)
uses the first auxiliary fact.

So (
3
), (
4
) hold, (
2
) follows.

Quite similarly, we show

\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab0b}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}{\ \mathbf{if}\ ({{X}={1}})\ {\{{\mathbf{apply}\ \sigma_{z}\ \mathbf{to}\ \Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}\}}\ \mathbf{else}\ {\mathbf{skip}}\ }\Bigl\{{\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab00}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)}\Bigr\}.

Finally, we have
Q_{ab00}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}\ \subseteq\ {\bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi}
and thus

\sum_{a,b}\bigl({Q_{ab00}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}}\bigr)\ \subseteq\ {\bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi}
as the following calculation shows:

\displaystyle Q_{ab00}\cdot\mathord{\spadesuit}\

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ ({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}})(U_{\sigma})\cdot\Phi\bigl({\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\cdot\mathcal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle=\ ({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}})(U_{\sigma})\cdot\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\cdot\underbrace{\Phi\bigl({\lvert{a,b}\rangle\beta_{00}^{\dagger}}\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{H}_{M}}_{\subseteq\,\mathcal{H}_{M}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ ({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}})(U_{\sigma})\cdot\bigl({XA,B}\bigr)(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\cdot\mathcal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\cdot\underbrace{({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}})\bigl({U_{\sigma}}\bigr)\cdot\mathcal{H}_{M}}_{\subseteq\,\mathcal{H}_{M}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\cdot\mathcal{H}_{M}=\ {\bigl({({\Phi.\mathsf{Snd},A}),B}\bigr)=_{q}\psi}.

Here
(*)
uses the definitions of
Q_{ab00}
,
\mathord{\spadesuit}
, and
=_{q}
.
The factor
\frac{1}{2}
vanishes because multiplying a subspace with a nonzero scalar has no effect.
And
(**)
follows by bringing both sides into the form
({({X,\Phi.\mathsf{Snd}}),\dots})
and expanding
\sigma=\mathsf{Sd}_{U_{\sigma}}
.

Combining all Hoare judgments, equalities, and inequalities, and using
rules
Seq
and
Weaken
, we get
(
1
) which finishes the analysis of teleportation.

7
Complements

When we think of a reference
F
as region in memory, it makes sense to consider the complement
F^{\complement}
of
F
, namely everything in the memory that is not contained in
F
.
A priori, there is no reason to assume that in a general reference category,
F^{\complement}
is definable in a meaningful way, or that it constitutes a reference.
However, we will see that by requiring some additional axioms in a reference category, we get a well-defined notion of complements.
In particular, quantum references have complements, see
Section 7.1
.
(We suspect classical references have complements, too, but we have not investigated that case.)

Before we formalize complements, let us explain why complements are useful:

•

When formulating predicates referring to states on a complex system (e.g., the predicates that are used in the pre- and postconditions in the quantum Hoare logic in
Section 6
), we can use predicates to refer to the state of “everything else”.
For example, we could state that no part of a quantum memory except for explicitly listed reference
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
is modified by a program by stating that if
({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})^{\complement}
is in state
\psi
before the execution, then
({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})^{\complement}
is in state
\psi
afterwards.

•

Complements will be used numerous times in the constructions and proofs in
Section 9
where we describe how various quantum mechanical concepts interact with references.
For example, when describing the state of a complex system by giving the states of all subsystems (Sections
9.3
and
9.4
), we have to specify the state of
all
references.
If an explicit list of all references is not known, we can use complements to refer to “all other references”.
And both the construction of the partial trace into a reference (
Section 9.6
) as well as the construction how to lift a quantum channel on a reference to a quantum channel on the whole space (
Section 9.5
) use complements under the hood.

•

Last but not least, besides the practical use cases, it is a foundationally interesting and natural property: In a reference category with complements, there is always a meaningful and well-behaved notion of “the rest of the system”.
In particular, this applies to the quantum reference category.

Definitions.

We say references
F,G
are
complements
iff they are disjoint and
FG
is an iso-reference.
We say a reference category
has complements
iff

•

For every reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, there exists an object
\mathbf{C}
and a reference
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
such that
F,G
are complements.
23
23

23

In our Isabelle/HOL formalization (see
Section 10
), we formalize a stronger property (different order of quantifiers):
For every
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
there exists a
\mathbf{C}
such that for every
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, there exists a reference
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
such that
F,G
are complements.
The reason for this is that objects in the reference category are modeled as types in Isabelle/HOL, and that it is not possibly to express the existence of a type
\mathbf{C}
that depends on a value
F
.

•

If
F,G
are complements and
F,H
are complements, then
G,H
are equivalent.

The second condition justifies to talk about “the” complement
\textstyle F^{\complement}
of
F
.
Strictly speaking,
F^{\complement}
is just one of the possible complements of
F
, determined only up to equivalence.
(Recall that intuitively, two references are equivalent when they correspond to the same part of the program memory.)

In a reference category with complements, the laws in
Figure 4
hold. We proved all laws in Isabelle/HOL (see
Section 10
).

Complements.
(
F,G,H
are assumed to be references.)

(xxxiii)

F,G
are complements iff
G,F
are complements.

(xxxiv)

F^{\complement}
is a reference.

(xxxv)

F,F^{\complement}
are complements.

(xxxvi)
Assume
F,G
are complements. Then
G,H
are equivalent iff
F,H
are complements.

(xxxvii)

F,({F^{\complement}})^{\complement}
are equivalent.

(xxxviii)
If
F,G
are disjoint,
F
,
({G,FG^{\complement}})
are complements (and also

G
,
({F,FG^{\complement}})
).

(xxxix)

F.G
and
\bigl({F^{\complement},F.{G^{\complement}}}\bigr)
are complements.
(
F.{G^{\complement}}
is to be read as
F.({G^{\complement}})
.)

(xl)

F\otimes_{\mathbf{r}}G
and
F^{\complement}\otimes_{\mathbf{r}}G^{\complement}
are complements.

Unit references.
(
F
is assumed to be a reference,
U,U^{\prime}
unit references,
I
an iso-reference.)

(xli)

U,F
are disjoint.

(xlii)

F,(U;F)
are equivalent.

(xliii)

F\circ U
is a unit reference.

(xliv)

U\circ I
is a unit reference.

(xlv)

U,U^{\prime}
are equivalent.

(xlvi)
If
U:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C},U^{\prime}:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}
, then
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
are isomorphic.

(xlvii)

\mathit{id}^{\complement}
is a unit reference (with same codomain as
\mathit{id}
).

(xlviii)

U_{\mathbf{A}}:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{A}
is a unit reference (and
\mathbf{I}
does not depend on
\mathbf{A}
).

(xlix)
If
U:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, then
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}\otimes\mathbf{A}
are isomorphic (for every
\mathbf{C}
).

(l)

I
and
U
are complements.

Figure 4:
Additional laws in a reference category with complements.
All laws have the implicit assumption that the types of the references match.

Note that we can easily extend the notion of complements to finite sets of references:
We say
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
are a
partition
iff they are pairwise disjoint and
({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})
is an iso-reference.
(Note that this definition does not depend on the order of the references
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
by Laws
2
,
2
,
2
and the fact that
\sigma,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
are iso-references.
24
24

24

We have not formalized this fact in Isabelle/HOL because it cannot be stated in simple type theory.
)
The intuition of being a partition is that the references
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
cover the whole memory.

Unit references.

One interesting consequence of complements (that could, however, also be easily axiomatized on its own), are
unit references
.
Intuitively, a unit reference is one that corresponds to an empty part of the program memory.
E.g., a classical variable of type
unit
would be a unit reference; writing into it has no effect.
Formally, we say that a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a unit reference iff
F
and
\mathit{id}
are complements.
(Since
\mathit{id}
is a reference that contains the whole memory, this intuitively means that
F
refers to an empty part of the memory.)
Unit references are useful in languages with side-effects (even if the side-effect is just possible non-termination).
E.g., if
e
is an expression with side-effects and unit result type, we can write
()\leftarrow e
to evaluate
e
without storing the result in memory.
(Here
()
denotes a unit reference.)
If unit references did not exist, the language would need a distinct concept for evaluating expressions without storing the result.

It turns out that a reference category with complements has a unit reference
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle U_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle U_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle U_{\mathbf{A}}$}}}:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{A}
for every
\mathbf{A}
(i.e.,
\mathbf{A}
represents the type of the memory).
Roughly speaking,
U_{\mathbf{A}}
is defined as the complement of
\mathit{id}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}
.
This is trivially a unit reference according to our definition.
However, this would lead to a different domain
\mathbf{I}
for every
\mathbf{A}
.
With some extra effort, we can show that all those domains
\mathbf{I}
are isomorphic and then construct a unit reference
U_{\mathbf{A}}
whose domain is independent of
\mathbf{A}
.

However, we also need to show that our definition of unit references “makes sense”.
For example, we want that a unit reference is disjoint from all other references (not just with
\mathit{id}
).
The properties we derive are listed in
Figure 4
; when proving these properties (see our Isabelle formalization,
Section 10
) we heavily use the properties of complements.

Note that for concrete reference categories (such as the quantum reference category), we may prefer not to use the unit reference
U_{\mathbf{A}}
that exists generically but instead define it concretely.
For example, in the quantum setting, we can define a unit reference of type
\mathbb{C}\mapsto\mathbf{A}
, see
Lemma 2
below.
(Here
\mathbb{C}
is slight abuse of notation for the space of linear functions
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
.)
Having
\mathbb{C}
instead of some unspecified
\mathbf{I}
as the domain means that the type of expressions that return unit values is nicer.
However, the laws from
Figure 4
still apply. (And the unit references according to both definitions are equivalent.)

7.1
Quantum references

We show that the quantum reference category
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
has complements. For the finite-dimensional case, we have shown this in Isabelle/HOL.
Here, we show the more general infinite-dimensional case (cf.
Section 4.2
).

Existence.

Fix a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
.
By
Lemma 23
(that was already used in the proof of Axiom
•
‣
1
,
Appendix C
),
there is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
and a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})U^{\dagger}
. Let
G(c):=U(1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes c)U^{\dagger}
.

G
is a reference (using Axiom
•
‣
1
and
Lemma 17
).
Define
I(x):=UxU^{\dagger}
which is a reference (using
Lemma 17
).
We have
F(a)G(c)=U(a\otimes c)U^{\dagger}=G(c)F(a)
since
U
is unitary.
Thus
F,G
are disjoint. Thus the pair
FG
exists, and
FG(a\otimes c)=I(a\otimes c)
.
By Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
, this implies that
FG=I
. Since
I^{-1}(y):=U^{\dagger}yU
is also a reference and the inverse of
I
,
FG=I
is an iso-reference. Thus
F,G
are complements by definition.

Uniqueness.

Assume that
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{D}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}
are complements, and
F
,
H:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
are complements.
By
Lemma 41
(
Appendix D
),
G(\mathbf{B})
is the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
. By the same lemma,
H(\mathbf{C})
is the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
as well.
Thus
G(\mathbf{B})=H(\mathbf{C})
. By
Lemma 42
(
Appendix D
), references with the same range are equivalent.
Thus
G,H
are equivalent.

By definition of “has complements”, we now have:

Theorem 1
.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
has complements.

Hence the laws from
Figure 4
hold for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
.

Reference categories with complements always have unit references.
However, in the quantum reference category, we have a particularly natural unit reference (with a simple and explicitly specified domain):

Lemma 2
.

u:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbf{B}
,
u(c):=c\cdot 1_{\mathbf{B}}
is a unit reference where we identify
B(\mathbb{C})
with
\mathbb{C}
.
25
25

25

Intuitively, an operator in
B(\mathbb{C})
is a complex
1\times 1
-matrix, hence simply a complex number.

Proof.

We have
u(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}
.
By
Lemma 44
(
Appendix D
), this means that
u
is a unit reference.
∎

8
Relationship to optics

In functional programming, a widely used concept are so-called
optics
.
Optics encompass various different methods for accessing subparts of larger data-structures.
The probably best known example of an optic is the lens that we already encountered in
Section 5
.
Other types of optics include
prisms
(giving access to one of the options of a sum-type),
optionals
a.k.a.,
affine traversals
(access to a part of a data-structure that may or may not be there).
Quantum references as described in this paper are also optics in the sense that they are functional objects that provide access to a subpart of the memory.

All this leads to two questions:

•

Can we construct quantum analogoues to other optics besides lenses?
(E.g., to optionals, for a part of a quantum system that may or may not be present.)

•

Might references as we have formalized them actually be a special case of some existing general formalism of optics?

To the first question, we do not have an answer but we believe this to be an interesting question for future research.

Concerning the second question: Riley
[
Ril18
]
presents a general treatment of optics.
They show that many existing kinds of optics are just special cases of their category of optics, for a suitable choice of base category.
(E.g., lenses arise when we use the category
Set
.)
We ask whether we recover our quantum references as a special case if we apply Riley’s definition to the category of Hilbert spaces with bounded operators as morphisms.
(Because our references operate on bounded operators.)
An
optic
from
\mathcal{H}_{A}
to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
in Riley’s sense is a pair
(L,R)
of morphisms (i.e., bounded operators)
L:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
and
R:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
(for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
) modulo the equivalence relation generated by
\bigl({(1\otimes M)L,R}\bigr)\cong\bigl({L,R(1\otimes M)}\bigr)
for arbitrary bounded operators
M
.
26
26

26

This is not the actual
definition
of optics in
[
Ril18
]
, but
[
Ril18
]
shows that this is equivalent to the definition.
We use this formulation because we can present it without introducing a number of category-theoretical concepts.

The intuition is that, if we have data of type
\mathcal{H}_{B}
(e.g., the memory), and we want to access a subpart of type
\mathcal{H}_{A}
(e.g., the reference content), then we apply
L
to split the data into a
\mathcal{H}_{A}
-part (that we want to access) and the rest (type
\mathcal{H}_{C}
). Then we operate on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, and then we apply
R
to put things together again (we get a state in
\mathcal{H}_{B}
again).
That is, like with our references, given an optic
(L,R)
, we can naturally lift a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
to one on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
by
F_{(L,R)}:a\mapsto R(a\otimes 1_{C})L
(we call
F_{(L,R)}
the
lifting function
of the optic).
Obviously, for any bounded operator
M
, the optics
\bigl({(1\otimes M)L,R}\bigr)
and
\bigl({L,R(1\otimes M)}\bigr)
lead to the same lifting function. (The converse is not obvious.)
This justifies the choice of equivalence relation when defining optics.

While the definition of an optics category looks very different from the definition of a reference category, and our definition of quantum references looks very different from this, too, it turns out that the latter are closely connected:
By
Lemma 23
,
F
is a quantum reference iff it can be written as
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{C})U^{\dagger}
for unitary
U
and some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
That is, we can represent
F
as the optic
(U^{\dagger},U)
.
But for optics (in Riley’s sense) and (quantum) references to actually be the same notion, we need to check three things:

(a)

If
(L,R)\cong(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
(i.e., they are the same optic), do they give rise to the same reference (i.e., have the same lifting function)?

(b)

If
(L,R)
and
(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
are the same reference, do we have
(L,R)\cong(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
?

(c)

Is every optic a reference? (I.e., is the lifting function of every optic a reference?)

The answer to (
c
) is trivially no:

(0,0)
leads to the function
F(a)=0
which is not a reference.
However, the latter is not surprising because the category of optics is not intended to be limited to well-behaved objects but simply establishes the type of things we are talking about (similar to our prereferences).
Indeed,
[
Ril18
]
introduces the notion of lawful optics to restrict the categories of optics; these are characterized by two certain properties.
We do not give the exact definition of these properties here as they again involve category-theoretic notions that we have not introduced here.
It suffices to say that they imply and maybe are equivalent to
F(ab)=F(a)F(b)
and
F(1)=1
for the lifting function
F
.
We recognize our Axiom
•
‣
1
here.
But unfortunately,
\bigl({\tiny\begin{pmatrix}1/2\!\!\!\!\\
\!\!&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2\!\!\\
&\!\!1\end{pmatrix}}\bigr)
is a lawful optic from
\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
to
\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
, yet the lifting function has
F\bigl({\tiny\begin{pmatrix}&\!\!i\\
-i\!\!\end{pmatrix}}\bigr)={\tiny\begin{pmatrix}&\!\!\!\!2i\\
-i/2\!\!\end{pmatrix}}
, i.e., it maps a unitary to a non-unitary, and thus is not a reference (nor, it seems, anything sensible from a quantum mechanical point of view).

This issue can be resolved by using the category of Hilbert spaces with unitaries as morphims (instead of bounded operators) as the base category.
The resulting category of optics satisfies conditions (
a
–
c
),
27
27

27

(
a
) is trivial even without assuming lawfulness.
(
c
) follows directly from the fact that lawfulness of
(L,R)
implies that
RL=1
.
(
b
) is shown in
Lemma 40
.

so this leads to a notion of optics that stands in 1-1 correspondence with our references.
But then we live in the category of unitaries, so strictly speaking we cannot apply the lifting functions to other physically meaningful operations (such as projectors or isometries).
To overcome this would have to define the notion of optics with respect to two related categories.
While we do not think that this would lead to problems, to the best of our knowledge this is an extension of optics that has not been studied.

Besides the above challenges, there are two more advantages that references have over optics in Riley’s sense:

•

One of the distinguishing features of references is that they support pairing, i.e., taking two references and see them as one.
For optics, it is unclear how such a notion would be defined (especially in a way that lawfulness would be preserved).

•

To define optics in Riley’s sense, we need category-theoretical constructions that might not be available in some mathematical foundations.
For example, the relation
\cong
above would be defined as the closure of the relation
\sim
defined by “
(L,R)\sim(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
iff there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
and an
M
such that …”.
This seemingly harmless definition quantifies over all Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
(with no upper bound on its cardinality) and would not, for example, even be well-typed in foundations like HOL (underlying Isabelle/HOL).
Stronger foundations (ZFC, calculus of inductive constructions
[
PM93
]
underlying e.g., Coq
[
Tea21
]
and Lean
[
Mou+15
]
, …) may well be able to handle it but we believe that there is an advantage in finding solutions that do not require stronger foundations (both for practical reasons and because the stronger the foundation, the more it is possible that it is unsound).

Of course, Riley’s formalism has advantages, too, that our references do not have.
For example, we have no notion of partial operations (e.g., accessing the fifth element of a quantum list, which may not exist) while in the classical setting, affine traversals (which are covered by Riley’s formalism, too) allow this.

We believe that it would be a valueable avenue for future research to find approaches that combine all the above advantages.

9
Lifting various quantum objects

By definition, a quantum reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
maps a bounded operator on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
(the space corresponding to the part of the memory that the reference occupies) to a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
(the space corresponding to the whole memory).
We already mentioned in
Section 4
that
F
maps unitaries to unitaries.
This allows us to interpret unitary operations on a reference as unitaries on the whole memory, and thus to interpret a quantum circuit containing only unitary operations.
But what about other operations/objects that occur in quantum computation?
Can we interpret a quantum state on a reference as a quantum state on the whole memory?
How about a measurement on a quantum state? A quantum channel?
In this section, we answer those questions.

9.1
Elementary objects

Since unitaries and projectors
A
are bounded operators, a reference
F
lifts them to the whole memory as
F(A)
.
The following lemma tells us that all relevant properties are preserved:

Lemma 3
.

Let
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
be a reference.
Fix
A\in\mathbf{A}
.

(i)

If
A
is a unitary, then
F(A)
is a unitary.

(ii)

If
A
is an isometry, then
F(A)
is an isometry.

(iii)

If
A
is a projector, then
F(A)
is a projector. (By projector, we mean an
orthogonal
projector throughout this paper.)

(iv)

\bigl\lVert{F(A)}\bigr\rVert=\lVert{A}\rVert
.

(v)

If
A
is positive, then
F(A)
is positive.

The proof is given in
Section E.1
.

9.2
Subspaces (quantum predicates)

Subspaces of a Hilbert space are important in quantum mechanics to describe the properties of quantum states (e.g., “state
\psi
is in subspace
S
”).
E.g., in von-Neumann-Birkhoff quantum logic
[
BN36
]
and in many quantum Hoare logics
[
Unr19a
,
ZYY19
,
Unr19
,
Li+20
]
.
Most often, we consider (topologically)
closed subspaces
only.
(Otherwise, the limit of a sequence of quantum states satisfying a property does not necessarily satisfy that property.
In the finite-dimensional case, every subspace is closed
[
Con13
, Proposition 3.3]
, so that requirement is usually not stated explicitly in works considering only finite-dimensional systems.)

Closed subspaces stand in a natural 1-1 correspondence with projectors:
If
S
is a closed subspace, there exists a unique projector
P_{S}
with image
S
(
Lemma 13
),
and for any projector
P
, its image is a closed subspace
[
Con13
, Proposition II.3.2 (b)]
.
Since we already know how to lift projectors through references (
item iii
), it is now easy to lift closed subspaces:

For a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, and a closed subspace
S\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{A}
, we define
\textstyle F({S})
as the image of
F(P_{S})
where
P_{S}
denotes the projector with image
S
.

For non-closed subspaces, we do not know if there is a simple way to lift them through references.
28
28

28

Using the tools from
Section 9.4
, we could define
F({S}):=\{F(\psi)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F^{\complement}(\phi):\psi\in S,\ \phi\in\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\}
where
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
denotes the domain of
F^{\complement}
.
But we have not explored this approach further since we believe that non-closed subspaces are not very relevant in the context of quantum programs.

The following lemma shows that the lifting of subspaces performs as
expected:

Lemma 4
.

Let
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
be a reference.
Let
S,T
be closed subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

(i)

F({S})
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

(ii)

F(S^{\bot})=F(S)^{\bot}
where
\textstyle S^{\bot}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
S
.

(iii)

F\bigl({\{0\}}\bigr)=\{0\}
.

(iv)

F(\mathcal{H}_{A})=F(\mathcal{H}_{B})
.

(v)

F(S)\subseteq F(T)
iff
S\subseteq T
.

(vi)

F(S\cap T)=F(S)\cap F(T)
.

(vii)

F(S+T)=F(S)+F(T)
where
S+T
denotes the closure of
\{\psi+\phi:\psi\in T,\phi\in S\}
, or equivalently the smallest closed subspace containing
S,T
.

(viii)

For a bounded operator
A
,
F(AS)=F(A)F(S)
.
(
AS
means the closed image of
S
under the operator
A
, and analogously
F(A)F(S)
.)

(ix)

Let
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
be another reference and
V
a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{C}
. Assume
F,G
are disjoint.
Then
F(S)\cap G(V)=\operatorname{im}F(P_{S})G(P_{V})=F(P_{S})G(V)
.
(
P_{S},P_{V}
are the projectors onto
S,V
.

F(P_{S})G(V)
is the image of
G(V)
under the operator
F(P_{S})
.)

The proof is given in
Section E.2
.

9.3
Mixed states (density operators)

A
mixed state
(roughly speaking, a probabilistic quantum state) is represented by a density operator.
Formally, a
density operator
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is a positive trace-class operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
of trace
1
.
(A trace-class operator is one for which the trace is defined, see
[
Con00
, Definitions 18.3, 18.10]
.
In the finite-dimensional case, the requirement “trace-class” can be omitted.
We write
\textstyle T(\mathcal{H})
for the space of all trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}
, and
\textstyle\lVert{\cdot}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
for the trace norm.)
And a
subdensity operator
is a positive trace-class operator of trace
\leq 1
.
Every (sub)density operator
\rho
can be written as
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}\psi_{i}\psi_{i}^{\dagger}
with
p_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1
(or
\leq 1
) and
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
;
the intuition is that the state is
\psi_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
.
Since
\rho
is a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, it is tempting to think that we can lift
\rho
to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
as
F(\rho)
.
Unfortunately, this does not work.

F(\rho)
will, in general, not be a density operator.
29
29

29

For example, if
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
F=\mathsf{Fst}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}
,

\rho={\tiny\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}
, then
\rho
is a density operator, but
F(\rho)=\rho\otimes 1_{\mathbf{A}}
has trace
2
and thus is not.

If we think about the situation, this is not surprising:
If we only know what the state of the reference
F
is, we cannot tell what the state of the overall system is since we do not know what the state of the remaining parts of the memory should be.
(Note that if
F
is an
iso-reference
, then
F(\rho)
is a density operator if
\rho
is.)
However, if references
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{n}
“cover the whole memory” and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{n}
are mixed states on those references, then it makes sense to talk about the state of the whole system.
Formally, we do this as follows:
And “
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{n}
cover the whole memory” simply means that
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{n}
are a partition (see
Section 7
).
30
30

30

In cases where
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
are only pairwise disjoint but not necessarily a partition, we can always add
F_{n+1}:=({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})^{\complement}
to turn them into a partition.

By definition, this implies that
({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})
is an iso-reference.
Thus we can lift the states
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3},\dots
to become a state
\rho
on the whole memory defined as
\rho:=({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})\bigl({\rho_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\rho_{n}}\bigr)
.
We write
F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
for this state
\rho
.
(This is a slight abuse of notation, because
\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}
is not actually a binary operation.
The whole term
F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
is defined as a whole.)
The following lemma ensures that this construction is well-defined (in particular, it produces a density operator), and that lifting density operators is compatible with the operations considered in the previous section.

Lemma 5
.

Let
F_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\to\mathbf{B}
, …,
F_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}\to\mathbf{B}
be a partition. Assume that
\rho_{i}
(
i=1,\dots,n
) are trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A_{i}}
.

(i)

If
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
are (sub)density operators,
then
F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
is a (sub)density operator.

(ii)

\operatorname{tr}\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}\cdots\operatorname{tr}\rho_{n}
.
(And in particular,
{F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}
is trace-class.)

(iii)

The lifting of mixed states does not depend on the order of the references and operators.
That is, for a permutation
\pi
on
1,\dots,n
, we have

F_{\pi(1)}(\rho_{\pi(1)})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{\pi(2)}(\rho_{\pi(2)})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{\pi(n)}(\rho_{\pi(n)})=F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.

(iv)

Let
U,V
be bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A_{i}}
.
Then
F_{1}(U\rho_{1}V)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})=F_{1}(U)\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)F_{1}(V)
.
31
31

31

We state this only for the first reference
F_{1}
for simplicity, but by (
iii
), it applies to all other references, too.

(v)

F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
has rank 1 iff all
\rho_{i}
have rank 1.

(vi)

\Phi:\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}\mapsto F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
is bounded linear in each argument (with respect to the trace norm).

(vii)

({F_{1},F_{2}})(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{3}(\rho_{3})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})=F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
31

(viii)

Let
S_{i}
generate
T(\mathcal{H}_{A_{i}})
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
Then
\bigl\{{F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n}):\rho_{i}\in S_{i}}\bigr\}
generates
T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
.
(‘‘Generate’’ refers to the linear span, closed with respect to the trace norm.)
In particular,
for quantum (sub)channels
32
32

32

Readers who wish to recall the precise definition of quantum (sub)channels are directed to
Section 9.5
.

\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
, if

\mathcal{E}\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)=\mathcal{F}\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)

for all
\rho_{i}\in S_{i}
, then
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
.

(ix)

Let
G_{1}:\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{A}_{1}
, …,
G_{m}:\mathbf{C}_{m}\to\mathbf{A}_{1}
be a partition, and
\sigma_{i}
trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}_{C_{i}}
.
Then
F_{1}\Bigl({G_{1}(\sigma_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}G_{m}(\sigma_{m})}\Bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})=F_{1}.G_{1}(\sigma_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{1}.G_{m}(\sigma_{m})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
31

The proof is given in
Section E.3
.

Example.

We illustrate how to use lifted mixed states in a small example, to illustrate how the theory developed in this subsection can be used.
We want to evaluate the following circuit:
\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert

\frac{1}{2}\,1

\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert

\rho_{\textit{rest}}

F
G
H
/
other references
H
H

The bottom wire represents any other quantum references that are possibly present in the system, i.e., the complement
Z:=({FGH})^{\complement}
.
References
F
and
H
are initially in the classical state
\lvert{0}\rangle
(
\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert
as a density operator).
And reference
G
is in the completely mixed state
\frac{1}{2}\,1=\frac{1}{2}\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert+\frac{1}{2}\lvert{1}\rangle\langle{1}\rvert
.
The initial state in this circuit can be expressed as

F\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}G\bigl({\frac{1}{2}\,1}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}H\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})
.
And the circuit consists of the unitaries
F(H)
,
FG(\mathsf{CNOT})
,
G(H)
.
(Which are to be applied from both sides to the current state, like
\rho\mapsto U\rho U^{\dagger}
.)
Thus we can evaluate the circuit as follows:

\displaystyle F\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}G\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\,1}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}H\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\xmapsto{F(H){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(\cdot)}F(H)^{\dagger}}\

\displaystyle F\bigl({H\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert H^{\dagger}}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}G\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\,1}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}H\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\quad=F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}G\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\,1}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}H\bigl({\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}GH\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\,1\otimes\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\xmapsto{GH(\mathsf{CNOT}){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(\cdot)}GH(\mathsf{CNOT})^{\dagger}}\

\displaystyle GH\bigl({\mathsf{CNOT}({\tfrac{1}{2}\,1\otimes\lvert{0}\rangle\langle{0}\rvert})\mathsf{CNOT}^{\dagger}}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\quad=GH\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{00}\rangle\langle{00}\rvert+\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{11}\rangle\langle{11}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\xmapsto{GH(H\otimes 1){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}(\cdot)}GH(H\otimes 1)^{\dagger}}\

\displaystyle GH\bigl({(H\otimes 1)\bigl({\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{00}\rangle\langle{00}\rvert+\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{11}\rangle\langle{11}\rvert}\bigr)(H\otimes 1)^{\dagger}}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}})

\displaystyle\qquad=GH\bigl({{\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{+0}\rangle\langle{+0}\rvert+\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert{-1}\rangle\langle{-1}\rvert}}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F\bigl({\lvert{+}\rangle\langle{+}\rvert}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}Z(\rho_{\mathit{rest}}).

Here
(*)
is by
item vii
, (
iii
) which allow us to arbitrarily reorder and pair references in order to put the references involved in the next gate into the first position.
Each of the
\xmapsto{\dots}
is by
item iv
which applies the current gate to the first tensor factor.
Note that in the third
\xmapsto{\dots}
, we apply
GH(H\otimes 1)
instead of
G(H)
.
This is because we cannot rewrite the current state into the form
G(\dots)\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots
because the state of
GH
cannot be written as a tensor product anymore after the CNOT.
However,
GH(H\otimes 1)=G(H)
by Axiom
•
‣
1
, so we still apply the same gate, only written differently.

So the final state can be written as
\lvert{+}\rangle
in the
F
reference and
\lvert{+0}\rangle
in the
G,H
references.
(Due to the CNOT, we cannot separate the states in those references
G,H
anymore.)

Note that all the reorderings of references necessary for applying the gates are quite straightforward and should be easy to automate in a proof assistant.
(But we have not done so at this point.)

9.4
Pure states

Sometimes, it is simpler to work with pure states than mixed states.
For example, when reasoning about a quantum circuit that contains only unitary operations, keeping track its behavior on pure states may be simpler since the added generality of mixed states might be overkill.
To the best of our knowledge, there are two slightly different definitions of what a pure state on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is:

•

A unit vector
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

•

A unit vector
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
modulo a global phase factor
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
Equivalently: a one-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
Equivalently: a rank-1 density operator
\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.

The second formalization is motivated by the fact that two pure states that differ only by a global phase factor (i.e.,
\exists c\in\mathbb{C}.\ \lvert{c}\rvert=1\land\psi_{1}=c\psi_{2}
) are physically indistinguishable, i.e., there is no sequence of operations and measurements that can distinguish them.
Since the second formalization is equivalent to restricting ourselves to mixed states of rank 1, we can simply use the mechanisms discussed in the previous section to work with these mixed states.

Thus in this section, we will focus on the first formalization, i.e., a state being a unit vector
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
First a word on why this formalization is even important (besides the fact that it is widely used).
After all, if two states differing only in the global phase are physically indistinguishable, why do we even care about the difference between two such states?
The reason (in our opinion) is that one important use case of pure states in quantum computation is to determine the behavior of a unitary by evaluating it on all pure states (or on a basis of pure states).
Namely, if
U_{1}\psi=U_{2}\psi
on all
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
(or on a basis), then
U_{1}=U_{2}
.
We cannot conclude that
U_{1}=U_{2}
if
U_{1}\psi=U_{2}\psi
only holds up to a phase factor.
33
33

33

And similarly, one may ask why one wants to determine a unitary
U
, and not just a unitary up to a global phase factor.
One example would be when considering controlled unitaries: The controlled unitary
CI
corresponding to the identity
I
is the identity.
The controlled unitary
C(-I)
corresponding to the negated identity is the controlled phase gate.
Those are very different even though
I
and
-I
differ only in a phase factor.

So to cover this use case, we need to see how to lift pure states
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
through quantum references.
Unfortunately, while the lifting of all other quantum objects (unitaries, projectors, mixed states, superoperators, measurements) turns out to work very naturally, this is not the case with pure states.

Definition.

Like in the mixed state case (and for the same reasons), we assume a partition
F_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\to\mathbf{B}
, …,
F_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}\to\mathbf{B}
, and pure states
\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}
, …,
\psi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}
on those references.

We define the following auxiliary constructs:
For every
\mathbf{A}
(or equivalently, for every Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
), we fix an arbitrary unit vector
