{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\eta_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\eta_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\eta_{\mathbf{A}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\eta_{\mathbf{A}}$}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
, with the constraint that
\eta_{\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}}=\eta_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\eta_{\mathbf{B}}
.
For every
\mathbf{B}
and every non-zero
b\in\mathbf{B}
, we fix an arbitrary unit vector
\textstyle\xi_{b}
in the span of
b
, with the additional constraint that if
\eta_{\mathbf{B}}
is in the span of
b
, then
\xi_{b}:=\eta_{\mathbf{B}}
.

Then we can lift the states
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
through
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
as
\psi:=\bigl({F_{1}(\psi_{1}\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}^{\dagger})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\psi_{n}\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{n}}^{\dagger})}\bigr)\xi_{b}
with
b:={({F_{1}(\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}^{\dagger})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{n}}\eta_{\mathbf{A}_{n}}^{\dagger})})}
.
(Recall the notation
\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}
from the previous section.)
We write
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
for this state
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
(As for
\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}
, this is a slight abuse of notation because
\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}
is not a binary operation.)

We call a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}

\eta
-regular
iff there exists a
c
such that
(F;F^{\complement})(\eta_{\mathbf{A}}\eta_{\mathbf{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes c)=\eta_{\mathbf{B}}
. (Intuitively,
\eta
-regular references are references that do not involve any “mappings” (see the introduction), e.g., references constructed from pairs,
\mathsf{Fst}
,
\mathsf{Snd}
.)
This is a technical notion needed for stating
Lemma 6
(
vi
) below.

The following lemma shows that this indeed results in a pure state and shows that the approach is compatible with the lifting of unitaries etc.

Lemma 6
.

Assume that
F_{1}:\mathbf{A}_{1}\to\mathbf{B}
, …,
F_{n}:\mathbf{A}_{n}\to\mathbf{B}
form a partition.
Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}
.

(i)

\Bigl\lVert{F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})}\Bigr\rVert=\lVert{\psi_{1}}\rVert\cdots\lVert{\psi_{n}}\rVert
.
In particular, if
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
are pure states, so is
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.

(ii)

The construction does not depend on the order of the references and pure states.
That is, for a permutation
\pi
on
1,\dots,n
, we have

F_{\pi(1)}(\psi_{\pi(1)})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{\pi(2)}(\psi_{\pi(2)})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{\pi(n)}(\psi_{\pi(n)})=F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.

(iii)

\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\mapsto F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
is bounded linear in each argument.

(iv)

({F_{1},F_{2}})(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{3}(\psi_{3})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})=F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
34
34

34

We state this only for the first reference
F_{1}
for simplicity, but by (
ii
), it applies to all other references, too.

(v)

Let
S_{i}
generate
\mathcal{H}_{A_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
(“Generate” refers to the linear span, closed with respect to the Hilbert space norm.)
Then
\bigl\{{F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n}):\psi_{i}\in S_{i}}\bigr\}
generates
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
In particular, for bounded operators
U,V
, if
U\bigl({F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})}\bigr)=V\bigl({F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})}\bigr)
for all
\psi_{i}\in S_{i}
, then
U=V
.

(vi)

Let
G_{1}:\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{A}_{1}
, …,
G_{m}:\mathbf{C}_{m}\to\mathbf{A}_{1}
be a partition.
Assume that
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
are
\eta
-regular.
Then
F_{1}\Bigl({G_{1}(\gamma_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G_{m}(\gamma_{m})}\Bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})=F_{1}.G_{1}(\gamma_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{1}.G_{m}(\gamma_{m})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
34

(vii)

\mathsf{Fst}
,
\mathsf{Snd}
,
\sigma
,
\alpha
,
\alpha^{\prime}
are
\eta
-regular.
If
F,G
are
\eta
-regular and disjoint, then
FG
is
\eta
-regular.
If
F,G
are
\eta
-regular, then
F.G
is
\eta
-regular.
If
F,G
are
\eta
-regular, then
F\otimes_{\mathbf{r}}G
is
\eta
-regular.

(viii)

Let
U
be a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}
.
Then
F_{1}(U\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{2}(\psi_{i})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})=F_{1}(U)\bigl({F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})}\bigr)
.
34

(ix)

Let
\mathord{\lozenge}(\psi):=\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.
Then
F_{1}(\mathord{\lozenge}(\psi_{1}))\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\mathord{\lozenge}(\psi_{n}))=\mathord{\lozenge}\Bigl({F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})}\Bigr)
.

(x)

Let
S
be a closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}
.
Assume
\psi_{2},\dots,\phi_{n}\neq 0
.
Then
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})\in F_{1}({S})
iff
\psi_{1}\in S
.
34

(See
Section 9.2
for the definition of
F_{1}({S})
.)

The proof is given in
Section E.4
.

Limitations of the approach.

In contrast to the lifting of mixed states, there are a number of limitations to this approach of lifting pure states.

First, the resulting definition does depend on the arbitrary choice of vectors
\eta_{\mathbf{A}},\xi_{F}
.
In fact, different choices of
\eta_{\mathbf{A}},\xi_{F}
will map the same pure states on
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
to different states
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
(Although it is easy to verify that different choices of
\eta_{\mathbf{A}},\xi_{F}
will lead to
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
that are equal up to a global phase factor.)
In contrast, the lifting of mixed states (
F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
) does not depend on arbitrary choices.

Second, there are some constraints concerning nested invocations of this construction.
(I.e., when one of the states
\psi_{i}
in
F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
is again of the form
G_{1}(\phi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G_{n}(\phi_{n})
.)
In this case, we can flatten the nested application of the construction only when
F_{1}
is
\eta
-regular (see
item vi
);

\eta
-regularity guarantees that the arbitrarily chosen
\eta_{\mathbf{A}}
are compatible with each other.
In contrast, when lifting mixed states, we can flatten nested applications with no side-conditions (see
item ix
).

Third, depending on the logical foundations we use, it might not even be able to globally fix arbitrary choices
\eta_{\mathbf{A}},\xi_{F}
throughout the category of Hilbert spaces.
(While satisfying the required constraints between them, e.g.,
\eta_{\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}}=\eta_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\eta_{\mathbf{B}}
.)
We might instead use, e.g., the category of Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
with a distinguished unit vector
\eta_{\mathbf{A}}
.
While this seems like a very tiny constraint, it still means that we change our formal foundation just to accommodate the lifting of pure states.
35
35

35

In our Isabelle/HOL formalization, we solve this issue by restricting all of results about the lifting of pure states to Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}
(spaces with a canonical basis indexed by values of type
\alpha
), where
\alpha
is of type class
default
.
The builtin type class
default
applies only to types with a specified default element.
For
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}
, we can then define
\eta_{\mathbf{A}}:=\lvert{\mathtt{default}_{\alpha}}\rangle
where
\mathtt{default}_{\alpha}
is the default value of type
\alpha
.
(And we crucially use the fact that
\mathtt{default}_{\alpha\times\beta}=(\mathtt{default}_{\alpha},\mathtt{default}_{\beta})
.)

Finding a formalization of quantum references that does not pose the above challenges when lifting pure states (while still preserving the other features described in this paper) is an open problem.

Example.

We illustrate how to use lifted pure states in a small example:
We will show that three CNOTs are equivalent to a qubit swap.
(This is near trivial in terms of the matrices but the focus of the example is not to show a complex situation but to explain the basic process.
In situations involving many different gates on different references, the present method would shine more as we would avoid complicated chains of swaps and tensor products with the identity.)
We want to show that the following two circuits evaluate the same unitary:

\hbox to92.65pt{\vbox to41.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-35.78577pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{}}{}{{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 -39.37 L 78.74 -39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In the language of references, we express this as

FG(\mathsf{CNOT})\cdot GF(\mathsf{CNOT})\cdot FG(\mathsf{CNOT})\qquad=\qquad FG(U_{\sigma})

(5)

where
U_{\sigma}
is the qubit swap
U_{\sigma}(\lvert{x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y}\rangle)=\lvert{y}\rangle\otimes\lvert{x}\rangle
.
Note that we did not need to explicitly refer to the “other references” because no operation happens on them.
But when we do wish to refer to them, we can refer to them as
FG^{\complement}
.

There are many ways to show (
5
).
For this example, we choose to show it by evaluating the circuit step by step on an initial state from the computational basis.
That is, we show

\Bigl({FG(\mathsf{CNOT})\cdot GF(\mathsf{CNOT})\cdot FG(\mathsf{CNOT})}\Bigr)\Bigl({F\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)}\Bigr)\\
={}{FG(U_{\sigma})}\ \Bigl({F\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)}\Bigr).

(6)

The left hand side is computed as

\displaystyle F\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}{}FG\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{FG(\mathsf{CNOT})}{}

\displaystyle FG\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y\oplus x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}{}GF\bigl({\lvert{y\oplus x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{GF(\mathsf{CNOT})}{}

\displaystyle GF\bigl({\lvert{y\oplus x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}{}FG\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y\oplus x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{FG(\mathsf{CNOT})}{}

\displaystyle FG\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle\otimes\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}{}F\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr).

Here
(*)
follows by
item iv
.
Note that in a more complex situation with more references, we would only have to pull those references into the first tensor factor that are involved in the operation we are applying
in this computation step
.
All other references in the tensor product can be left untouched (like
FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)
in our example).
And
(**)
follows by
item iv
, (
ii
).
(By first unwrapping the pair and then rewrapping in a different order.)
Each application of the CNOTs is done using
item viii
.
(All these steps are very mechanical and should not be hard to automate.
Our present Isabelle/HOL implementation, for example, proves each of the equalities by a simple tactic invocation.)

And similarly,

\displaystyle F\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)=FG\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle\otimes\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{FG(U_{\sigma})}{}

\displaystyle FG\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle\otimes\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)=F\bigl({\lvert{y}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}G\bigl({\lvert{x}\rangle}\bigr)\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}FG^{\complement}\bigl({\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr).

Thus the lhs and rhs of (
6
) both evaluate to the same state.
Hence we have shown (
6
).

From (
6
), our initial goal (
5
) follows by
item v
.

9.5
Quantum channels

Operations on mixed states are commonly described by
quantum channels
(a.k.a. quantum operation or CPTPM).
A quantum channel is a completely positive trace-preserving map
\mathcal{E}:T(\mathcal{H}_{A})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
.
(In the finite-dimensional case, the set
T(\mathcal{H})
of all trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}
is simply the set of all linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
.)
In particular,
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
is a density operator if
\rho
is.
When we work with subdensity operators, it is more natural to consider
quantum subchannels
.

\mathcal{E}:T(\mathcal{H}_{A})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
is a quantum subchannel iff it is completely positive and
\operatorname{tr}\mathcal{E}(\rho)\leq\operatorname{tr}\rho
for every positive
\rho
.
Quantum (sub)channels have a tensor product compatible with the tensor product of trace-class operators by
Lemma 38
(
Appendix B
).

An alternative definition of quantum channels, which we call Kraus-channels to avoid ambiguity, is the following:

\mathcal{E}:T(\mathcal{H}_{A})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
is a
Kraus-channel
iff there exists a family of bounded operators
M_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{i}M_{i}\rho M_{i}^{\dagger}
and
\sum_{i}M^{\dagger}_{i}M_{i}=I
.
36
36

36

Here (and everywhere else in this paper) infinite sums of operators are supposed to converge with respect to the strong operator topology, SOT
[
Con13
, Definition IX.1.2]
.
Sums can be over arbitrary, not necessarily countable index sets; see, e.g.,
[
Con13
, Definition 4.11]
for a precise definition of such sums.
Note that for sums of
positive
operators, convergence of in the SOT is equivalent to convergence in various other topologies by
Lemma 29
(and for sums of positive trace-class operators in even more topologies by
Lemma 30
).
In particular, the two sums in the definition of Kraus-(sub)channels are with respect to the trace norm and the SOT in
[
Hol11
]
and with respect to the trace norm and the WOT
[
Con13
, Definition IX.1.2]
in
[
Fri19
]
; since they are sums of positive operators the definitions from
[
Hol11
,
Fri19
]
are nonetheless equivalent to ours.

A Kraus-channel is always a quantum channel, and the notions coincide if
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
are separable (i.e., countably dimensional) Hilbert spaces
[
Hol11
, Section 3.1]
.
A Kraus-subchannel is defined in the same way, except with the condition
\sum_{i}M^{\dagger}_{i}M_{i}\leq I
instead of
\sum_{i}M^{\dagger}_{i}M_{i}=I
.
A Kraus-subchannel is always a quantum subchannel, and the notions coincide if
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
are separable Hilbert spaces
[
Fri19
, Theorem 9]
.

In the following, we will only consider quantum channels where the domain and codomain are the same space. (I.e., quantum channels
T(\mathcal{H}_{A})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{A})
).
This is because we will consider quantum channels that operate
on
the content of a reference, not quantum channels between references.
(However, that does not preclude an operation that, e.g., takes data from one reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
and puts the result of the computation into another reference
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
.
This would simply be described as a quantum channel on
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
applied to
FG
.)

Lifting quantum channels.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is an iso-reference, then there is a very natural way of lifting a quantum channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}
.
By
Lemma 26
,
F(a)=UaU^{\dagger}
for some unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
We can thus define
F(\mathcal{E}):\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{B}
as
F(\mathcal{E})(\rho):=U\mathcal{E}(U^{\dagger}\rho U)U^{\dagger}
.
Or equivalently,
F(\mathcal{E}):=F\circ\mathcal{E}\circ F^{-1}
.
(The latter gives us a different interpretation:
An iso-reference is a quantum channel (see
Lemma 7
(
x
) below), so
F(\mathcal{E})
is just the composition of three quantum channels.)

For non-iso-references
F
, this definition does not work.
(
F
is not a quantum channel, nor can it be represented as
UaU^{\dagger}
.)
Fortunately, using complements, we can very easily extend the above idea to arbitrary references.
Let
F^{\complement}:\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{B}
be the complement of
F
.
We can lift
\mathcal{E}
to become a quantum channel on
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
;
the tensor product
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathit{id}
of quantum channels achieves this.
And
({F,F^{\complement}})
is an iso-reference
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{B}
by definition of the complement.
So we can lift
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathit{id}
to a quantum channel on
\mathbf{B}
through
({F,F^{\complement}})
.
Putting this together, we get the following definition:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}}:=({F,F^{\complement}})\circ(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathit{id})\circ({F,F^{\complement}})^{-1}
.
Note that this definition also applies if
\mathcal{E}
is a subchannel.

Note that this construction is only applicable when the domain and codomain of
\mathcal{E}
are the same.
(I.e., we cannot apply it to
\mathcal{E}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
with
\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{B}
.)
This is because
F({\mathcal{E}})
intuitively represents “applying
\mathcal{E}
to
F
”, not “applying
\mathcal{E}
to
F
and storing the result in a different reference”.

The definition uses an arbitrarily chosen complement
F^{\complement}
of
F
(recall that the complement is only unique up to equivalence).
The following lemma shows that the definition does not depend on the choice of complement, and that
F(\mathcal{E})
interacts as expected with other constructions.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{F}
be quantum subchannels.
Let
F,G
be references.

(i)

If
\mathcal{E}
is a quantum (sub)channel, so is
F(\mathcal{E})
.

(ii)

Let
F,G
be complements.
Then
F({\mathcal{E}})=FG\circ(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathit{id})\circ FG^{-1}
.
(This means the choice of complement is irrelevant.)

(iii)

F({\mathcal{E}})\circ F({\mathcal{F}})=F({\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{F}})
.

(iv)

If
F,G
are disjoint, then
FG(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F})=F(\mathcal{E})\circ G(\mathcal{F})=G(\mathcal{F})\circ F(\mathcal{E})
.

(v)

F({\mathit{id}})=\mathit{id}
.

(vi)

For a partition
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
and trace-class operators
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
,
F_{1}\bigl({\mathcal{E}}\bigr)\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)=F_{1}(\mathcal{E}(\rho_{1}))\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{2}(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
37
37

37

We state this only for the first reference
F_{1}
for simplicity, but by
item iii
, it applies to all other references, too.

(vii)

F\bigl({G(\mathcal{E})}\bigr)=(F.G)(\mathcal{E})
.

(viii)

\sigma(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{E}
.

(ix)

\alpha\bigl({\mathcal{E}\otimes(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})}\bigr)=(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{G}
and
\alpha^{\prime}\bigl({(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{G}}\bigr)=\mathcal{E}\otimes(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})
.

(x)

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}
are iso-references, then
F|_{T(\mathcal{H}_{A})}
(
F
restricted to trace-class operators) is a bijective quantum channel, and
(F\otimes_{\mathbf{r}}G)|_{T(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})}=F|_{T(\mathcal{H}_{A})}\otimes G|_{T(\mathcal{H}_{B})}
.

(xi)

For a bounded operator
U
, let
\mathbf{Sem}(U)
denote the map
\rho\mapsto U\rho U^{\dagger}
.
Then
\mathbf{Sem}(F(U))=F(\mathbf{Sem}(U))
.

The proof is given in
Section E.5
.

Lifting Kraus-channels.

A Kraus-channel
\mathcal{E}
can be written as
\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{i}M_{i}\rho M_{i}^{\dagger}
for bounded operators
M_{i}
with
\sum_{i}M^{\dagger}_{i}M_{i}=1
.
It is natural to lift a Kraus channel through the reference
F
using the following definition:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle F(\mathcal{E})$}}}(\rho):=\sum_{i}F(M_{i})\rho F(M_{i})^{\dagger}
.
But since for given
\mathcal{E}
, the choice of the Kraus operators
M_{i}
is not unique, it could be that this definition is not well-defined.
The following lemma shows that this is not the case, and that this definition is compatible with the one above.

Lemma 8
.

Let
F
be a reference.

(i)

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Kraus-(sub)channel, then
F(\mathcal{E})
is a Kraus-(sub)channel.

(ii)

The definition of
F(\mathcal{E})
does not depend on the choice of
M_{i}
in
\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\sum_{i}M_{i}\rho M_{i}^{\dagger}
.

(iii)

For a Kraus-(sub)channel
\mathcal{E}
,
F(\mathcal{E})
as defined for Kraus-(sub)channels, and
F(\mathcal{E})
as defined for quantum (sub)channels, coincide.

The proof is given in
Section E.5
,
\autopageref
page:proof:lemma:lift.kraus.

Note that by (
iii
), the definition
F(\mathcal{E})
for Kraus-channels inherits the laws from
Lemma 7
as well.
Furthermore, (
iii
) gives us a convenient means of computing
F({\mathcal{E}})
for (sub)channels
\mathcal{E}
that are explicitly given in Kraus-operator representation.
E.g., if
\mathcal{E}(\rho)=U\rho U^{\dagger}
for a unitary
U
, then
F({\mathcal{E}})(\rho)=F(U)\rho F(U)^{\dagger}
.

9.6
Partial trace

The partial trace is an operation that maps density operators on a system
C
to density operators on a subsystem
A
.
For example, on a bipartite system
\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
, we define
\operatorname{tr}_{C}:T(\mathcal{H}_{B})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{A})
to be the unique operation such that
\operatorname{tr}_{C}(\rho\otimes\sigma)=\rho\cdot\operatorname{tr}\sigma
.
(We say we “trace out
C
”.)
Note that the commonly used notation
\operatorname{tr}_{C}
indicates which parts of the system
B
we
remove
(namely
C
), not which part we
keep
(namely
A
).
This is notationally somewhat awkward because we need to be always able to list all other parts of the (potentially quite complex) system besides
A
.
Thus, here we use a slightly different notational convention:
Instead of
\operatorname{tr}_{C}
(tracing out
C
), we write
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow A}
(tracing in
A
).

Since in the context of this work, a reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
describes a subsystem of
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, it is natural to want to define
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
, the operation that maps a density operator
\rho\in T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
to
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}\rho\in T(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.
Intuitively,
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}\rho
describes the state of the content of the reference
F
(if we remove the rest of the memory).

To define this, we first define a special case: For Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
, let
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}$}}}:T(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{A})
denote the unique bounded linear map with
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}(\rho\otimes\sigma)=\rho\cdot\operatorname{tr}\sigma
.
This is the operation that traces out everything but the content of reference
\mathsf{Fst}
(the first part of the system), and traces in
\mathsf{Fst}
.
Furthermore, for an iso-reference
I
, we can define
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow I}:=I^{-1}
. (Recall that an iso-reference represents the whole memory, so nothing needs to be traced out; we only need to map the state into the correct representation using
I^{-1}
.)
Note that for any reference
F
, we have that
F=({F,F^{\complement}}).\mathsf{Fst}
(Law
2
).
Thus it makes sense to define
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}\circ\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow({F,F^{\complement}})}
.
Putting all this together, we get the definition:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}$}}}:={\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}}\circ{({F,F^{\complement}})}^{-1}
.
38
38

38

Note that this is compatible with the special case definition of
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}
by
Lemma 9
(
v
) below.

Lemma 9
.

Let
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
and
G
be references.

(i)

\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
is well-defined. (That is, the auxiliary operation
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}
exists and is unique.)

(ii)

\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
is a quantum channel.

(iii)

The definition of
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
does not depend on the choice of complement.
That is, if
F,G
are complements,
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}={\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow\mathsf{Fst}}}\circ{({F,G})}^{-1}
.

(iv)

For iso-references
F
,
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}=F^{-1}|_{T(\mathcal{H}_{A})}
.

(v)

If
F,G
are complements, for all trace-class operators
\rho,\sigma
,
\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}({F,G})(\rho\otimes\sigma)=\rho\cdot\operatorname{tr}\sigma
.

(vi)

For a partition
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
and trace-class operators
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
,

\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F_{i}}\bigl({F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}\bigr)=\rho_{i}\cdot\prod_{j\neq i}\operatorname{tr}\rho_{j}
.

(vii)

\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F.G}=\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow G}\circ\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
.

(viii)

For a quantum subchannel
\mathcal{E}
,
{\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}}\circ F(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}\circ\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
.

(ix)

For a quantum channel
\mathcal{E}
, and disjoint
F,G
,
{\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}}\circ G(\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}
.

The proof is given in
Section E.6
.

Entropy.

The definition of the partial trace is also useful to define information-theoretic properties that refer to subsystems.
For example, we mentioned in the introduction (
\autopageref
page:intro.entropy) that we can use references to define the Shannon entropy
[
Neu32
]
of the content of a reference
F
.
If
H_{\mathit{basic}}(\rho):=\rho\log\rho
(i.e., the entropy of the whole quantum state
\rho
), then we can just define the Shannon entropy
H(F)_{\rho}
of the subsystem
F
(where
\rho
is the state of the whole system) as follows:
H(F)_{\rho}:=H\bigl({\operatorname{tr}_{\downarrow F}(\rho)}\bigr)
.
We can also easily define the conditional Shannon entropy
[
Tom16
, Definition 5.1]
as
H(F|G)_{\rho}:=H\bigl({FG}\bigr)_{\rho}-H(G)_{\rho}
for disjoint
F,G
.

9.7
Measurements

There are different formalisms for defining measurements, in order of increasing generality: complete measurements, projective measurements, POVMs, and generalized measurements.
We discuss each of these in turn.
In the following,
F
will always be a quantum reference
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
.

Projective measurements.

A
projective measurement
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is defined by a family of projectors
M=\{P_{i}\}_{i\in J}
such that
P_{i}P_{j}=0
for
i\neq j
and
\sum_{i}P_{i}=1
.
39
39

39

Convergence of sums is with respect to the SOT, see
footnote 36
for details.

Here
J
is the set of outcomes. (
J
is allowed to be infinite.)
The
probability of outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
\lVert{P_{j}\psi}\rVert^{2}
(
\operatorname{tr}P_{j}\rho P_{j}^{\dagger}
).
The
post-measurement state for outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
P_{j}\psi/\lVert{P_{j}\psi}\rVert
(
P_{j}\rho P_{j}^{\dagger}/trP_{j}\rho P_{j}^{\dagger}
).
The
non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
P_{j}\psi
(
P_{j}\rho P_{j}^{\dagger}
).
Note that from the non-normalized post-measurement state, we can compute the post-measurement state and the outcome probability.
Thus our results below will be stated only for the non-normalized post-measurement state for brevity.

We define
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle F({M})$}}}:=\{F(P_{i})\}_{i\in J}
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
M=\{{P_{i}}\}_{i}
be a projective measurement.
Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
be a partition.

(i)

F(M)
is a projective measurement.

(ii)

For a unitary
U
, let
UM
denote the projective measurement
\{UP_{i}U^{\dagger}\}
.
Then
F(U)\,F(M)=F(UM)=(F.\mathsf{Sd}_{U})(M)
.

(iii)

Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
be vectors.
Let
\psi:=F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
Let
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
M
given
\psi_{1}
.
Let
\psi^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
F_{1}(M)
given
\psi
.
Then
\psi^{\prime}=F(\psi_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F(\psi_{n})
.
40
40

40

We state this only for the first reference
F_{1}
for simplicity, but by
item ii
/
item iii
, it applies to all other references, too.

(iv)

Let
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
be trace-class operators. Let
\rho:=F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
Let
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
M
given
\rho_{1}
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
F_{1}(M)
given
\rho
.
Then
\rho^{\prime}=F(\rho_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\rho_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F(\rho_{n})
.
40

Here (
ii
) describes a common transformation of projective measurements and shows that it is compatible with lifting through references, and with a mapping the reference (replacing
F
by
F.\mathsf{Sd}_{U}
).

The proof is given in
Section E.7
.

Complete measurements.

A complete measurement
M
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is described by an orthonormal basis
M=\{{\psi_{j}}\}_{j\in J}
.
The intuitive interpretation is that we measure which of the states
\psi_{j}
the reference is in.
A complete measurement is a special case of a projective measurement, namely
M:=\{{\psi_{j}\psi_{j}^{\dagger}}\}_{j\in J}
.
Thus a complete measurement can be lifted through a reference simply by interpreting it as a projective measurement and then applying
F(M)
as defined in the previous paragraph.
Note that this means that a complete measurement on
F
does not lead to a complete measurement on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
This is unsurprising: if
\mathcal{H}_{B}
has a higher dimension than
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, then there cannot be an orthonormal basis of the same cardinality
\lvert{J}\rvert
on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
; no complete measurement on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
with outcomes
J
exists.

POVMs.

A
POVM
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is defined by a family of positive bounded operators
M=\{M_{i}\}_{i\in J}
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
such that
\sum_{i}M_{i}=1
.
39

Here
J
is the set of outcomes.
41
41

41

There are more general definitions of POVMs where
J
is a measurable space.
Hence the name POVM which means “positive operator-valued
measure
”.
For example, a measurement of the real-valued position of a particle (with uncountably-many outcomes) cannot be described by a POVM according to the definition we use here.
POVMs as described here describe always lead to discrete probability distributions of outcomes which makes them easier to handle.

(
J
is allowed to be infinite.)
The
probability of outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
\lVert{\sqrt{M_{j}}\psi}\rVert^{2}
(
\operatorname{tr}M_{j}\rho
).
Note that a POVM on its own does not specify the post-measurement-states; the generalized measurements below can be used if post-measurement states are needed.
Note that every projective measurement is also a POVM.

We define
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle F({M})$}}}:=\{F(M_{i})\}_{i\in J}
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
M=\{M_{i}\}_{i}
be a POVM.
Let
F
be a reference.
Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
be a partition.

(i)

F(M)
is a POVM.

(ii)

For a unitary
U
, let
UM
denote the POVM
\{UM_{i}U^{\dagger}\}_{i}
.
Then
F(U)\,{F(M)}=F\bigl({UM}\bigr)=(F.\mathsf{Sd}_{U})(M)
.

(iii)

Let
\psi_{k}
be a vector, and let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
except
\psi_{k}
be unit vectors.
Let
\psi:=F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
Then the probability of outcome
j
using
M
given
\psi_{k}
equals the probability of outcome
j
using
F_{k}(M)
given
\psi
.

(iv)

Let
\rho_{k}
be a trace-class operator, and
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
except
\rho_{k}
be density operators.
Let
\rho:=F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
Then the probability of outcome
j
using
M
given
\rho_{k}
equals the probability of outcome
j
using
F_{k}(M)
given
\rho
.

(v)

Let
\rho
be a trace-class operator (and
\psi
be a vector).
Assume that
M
is also a projective measurement (i.e., all
M_{i}
are projectors).
Then the probability of outcome
j
using
F(M)
given
\rho
or
\psi
(where
M
is interpreted as a POVM)
equals the probability of outcome
j
using
F(M)
given
\rho
or
\psi
(where
M
is interpreted as a projective measurement)

The proof is given in
Section E.7
,
\autopageref
page:proof:povm.

General measurements.

A
general measurement
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is defined by a family of bounded operators
M=\{M_{i}\}_{i\in J}
such that
\sum_{i}M_{i}^{\dagger}M_{i}=1
.
39

Here
J
is the set of outcomes. (
J
is allowed to be infinite.)
The
probability of outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
\lVert{M_{j}\psi}\rVert^{2}
(
\operatorname{tr}M_{j}\rho M_{j}^{\dagger}
).
The
post-measurement state for outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
M_{j}\psi/\lVert{M_{j}\psi}\rVert
(
M_{j}\rho M_{j}^{\dagger}/trM_{j}\rho M_{j}^{\dagger}
).
The
non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j\in J
using
M
given pure state
\psi
(or mixed state
\rho
)
is
M_{j}\psi
(
M_{j}\rho M_{j}^{\dagger}
).
Note that from the non-normalized post-measurement state, we can compute the post-measurement state and the outcome probability.
Thus our results below will be stated only for the non-normalized post-measurement state for brevity.

Note that a projective measurement is a special case of a general measurement (with matching definitions of outcome-probabilities and post-measurement-states).
And note that any general measurement
M
gives rise to a POVM
M^{\prime}
via
M^{\prime}_{i}:=M_{i}^{\dagger}M_{i}
(such that the outcome-probabilities of
M^{\prime}
are the same as those of
M
for the same state).

We define
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle F({M})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle F({M})$}}}:=\{F(M_{i})\}_{i\in J}
.

Lemma 12
.

Let
M=\{M_{i}\}_{i\in J}
be a general measurement.
Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
be a partition.

(i)

F(M)
is a general measurement.

(ii)

Let
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}
be vectors.
Let
\psi:=F_{1}(\psi_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F_{n}(\psi_{n})
.
Let
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
M
given
\psi_{1}
.
Let
\psi^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome
j
using
F(M)
given
\psi
.
Then
\psi^{\prime}=F(\psi_{1}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F(\psi_{2})\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{p}}F(\psi_{n})
.
42
42

42

We state this only for the first reference
F_{1}
for simplicity, but by
item ii
/
item iii
, it applies to all other references, too.

(iii)

Let
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n}
be trace-class operators. Let
\rho:=F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})
.
Let
\rho_{1}^{\prime}
denote the non-normalized post-measurement state for outcome

using
M
given
\rho_{1}
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}

given

(iv)

10
Isabelle formalization

We have formalized a large fraction of the results from this paper in Isabelle/HOL

[
NPW02
,
Nip+21
]
,
namely the everything from Sections
2
–
7
.
The source code of our formalization can be found at
[
Unr21
]
(infinite-dimensional update
[
Unr24b
]
).
In particular, we formalized the quantum references in the more general (and mathematically more involved) infinite-dimensional case.

Note that a large part of the extra formalization effort in the
infinite-dimensional
case lied in the development of the library for tensor products
[
Unr24
]
by the same authors as this paper.

When formalizing, we encountered the following challenge:
In this paper, we structured our results by first stating generic axioms and deriving
generic laws from them (
Section 2
), and then instantiating
these axioms in the quantum and the classical case
(Sections
4
and
5
). Unfortunately,
in the logic HOL, this is not possible.
While HOL (as implemented in Isabelle/HOL) allows us to state and prove facts that are type-polymorphic and thus hold for any type, we cannot state a fact that holds for all type constructors.
But the results from

Section 2
are basically of the form: “for any type constructor
update
(so that
\alpha

update
, when
\alpha
ranges over different types, represents all the objects of the category) satisfying axioms X, the laws Y hold”.
And then we
instantiate that type constructor
update
with the type
constructor of linear maps (quantum case) or of partial functions (classical
case). Therefore, we cannot express this generally in Isabelle/HOL.

We used the following workaround: We have a theory
Axioms

that states the general axioms for a type constructor

update
,
43
43

43

Restricted to a type class
domain
.

and then we derive the laws generically from these axioms in the
theory
Laws
. Then we instantiate these axioms in the theories

Axioms_Quantum
and
Axioms_Classical
. By
instantiating, we mean that we write an independent theory (that does not import
Axioms
) that has a proven lemma for each axiom, but
with the type constructor
update
replaced by the appropriate type constructor for the quantum/classical setting.
(That is,
cblinfun
, the type of complex bounded linear functions, and

map
, the type of partial functions.) Then we use a Python script to
copy the theory
Laws
into
Laws_Quantum
and

Laws_Classical
, importing
Axioms_Quantum
and

Axioms_Classical
instead of
Axiom
. The script then
substitutes
update
by the appropriate type constructor (and does potential other replacements of constant or lemma names, as desired) based on the specification given in the theory headers.
All proofs from

Laws
then automatically carry over, and improvements in
Laws
are
automatically adapted to
Laws_Quantum
and

Laws_Classical
. This constitutes a poor man’s substitute for
higher-order polymorphism.

Theory

Purpose

Misc

187

Various lemmas that are missing in other libraries

Tensor_Product_Matrices

200

Theorems for translating finite-dimensional tensor products to explicit matrix representation (for explicit computations)

Some useful facts about quantum mechanics (unrelated to references)

for the quantum case

for the quantum case

for the classical case

for the classical case

Total:

4583

Figure 5:
Isabelle/HOL theories
[
Unr21
]
. Lines of code are without blanks or comments.

The results from
Section 2
are therefore proven in the
theories
Axioms
and
Laws
.

The results from
Section 4
(quantum references) are in the
theories
Axioms_Quantum
and
Quantum_Extra
.

Axioms_Quantum
instantiates the axioms from
Axioms
.
Specifically, we use the complex bounded operator library

[
CU21
]
as the mathematical foundation. It defines
the type
(\alpha,\beta)

cblinfun
(short:

\alpha{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\Rightarrow_{\mathit{CL}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathit{CL}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathit{CL}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathit{CL}}$}}}\beta
) as the type of bounded linear
44
44

44

In the
finite dimensional case that we consider here, bounded linear is the
same as linear.
functions between complex vector spaces

\alpha,\beta
. And the type
\alpha

ell2
of square-summable
functions
\alpha\to\mathbb{C}
.
45
45

45

For finite types
\alpha
, as
in our case, this is just
\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}
.
The objects of the
category (the “updates”) are then

\alpha\ \mathtt{update}:=({\alpha\ \mathtt{ell2}\Rightarrow_{\mathit{CL}}\alpha\ \mathtt{ell2}})
(for finite
\alpha
), i.e., the linear functions

\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}
. Based on this formalization, the tensor
product can be defined very nicely, because the tensor product of

\alpha\ \mathtt{update}
and
\beta\ \mathtt{update}
is simply

(\alpha\times\beta)\ \mathtt{update}
. Using the existing
infrastructure from the bounded operator library, and our own
formalization of finite dimensional tensor products (theory

Finite_Tensor_Product
, the bounded operator library does
not have a tensor product), defining the required constants (i.e.,
predicates that specify what the pre-references and references are) and
proving the axioms is then very easy. (Just 744 lines of code, see

Figure 3
.)

All the laws specific to the quantum setting mentioned in

Section 4
are proven in theory

Quantum_Extra
. Again, the number of lines is very small
(270) showing that our formalism is indeed well-suited for
machine-checked formalization.

Finally, the results from
Section 5
are formalized in
the theories
Axioms_Classical
and
Classical_Extra
,
with
\alpha\ \mathtt{update}
instantiated as
(\alpha,\alpha)\ \mathtt{map}=(\alpha\Rightarrow\alpha\ \mathtt{option})
,
the type of partial functions on
\alpha
.

Examples.

The Hoare logic from
Section 6
is formalized in the theory

QHoare
. Again, using references turns out to be very easy, the
(admittedly simple) Hoare logic only needs 56 lines of
formalization, including the derivation rules. And the analysis of
the teleportation protocol from
Section 6.1
is presented in

Teleport
, in 225 lines. The structure of this file is as
follows: First we define a “locale” axiomatizing the references

X,A,B,\Phi
.
46
46

46

In Isabelle, a locale
[
Bal14
,
Bal10
]
is a kind
of package for definitions and lemmas based on some constants and
axioms such that the constant and axioms (the references and their
mutual compatibility in our case) can be instantiated later.
In
this locale, we define teleportation and prove the Hoare judgment
(
1
).

Additionally, we instantiate this locale with some concrete choices for the references.
Namely
A
and
B
are 64 and
1000.000 qubit references, respectively. And instead of a two qubit reference
\Phi
, we have two references
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
, and the
references are explicit parts of a quantum memory that has the tensor structure
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathit{specific}}:=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{2}}
.
This demonstrates how easy it is to reuse a theorem stated in terms of
references in a different context that makes specific assumptions about
the memory layout. Had we proven Hoare judgment for some specific
layout (e.g.,

\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
) and by
explicitly tensoring operators with the identity when applying them to
specific references, we would probably have to redo the proof (although
without any relevant changes) if we wanted to reuse the result in a
different context. (Note that we even chose not to put
\Phi_{1}
and

\Phi_{2}
next to each other in
\mathcal{H}_{M,\mathit{specific}}
, even
though
\Phi
was treated as a single reference in the proof of the
Hoare judgment. This did not lead to any difficulties.)

Complements.

We also develop the theory of reference categories with complements.
Analogous to the theory
Axioms
, we have a theory
Axioms_Complements
that is instantiated in the quantum case by
Axioms_Complements_Quantum
.
The additional laws that hold in a reference theory with complements (
Figure 4
) are proven generically in
Laws_Complements
; our Python script then creates the quantum instantiation automatically (
Laws_Complements_Quantum
).

We note that showing that the quantum reference theory has complements is mathematically considerably more involved than proving the rest of the quantum reference axioms.
While the rest (
Axioms_Quantum
; 744 lines of code) is mostly an application of standard facts about linear algebra, in
Axioms_Complements_Quantum
we need to actually do some real proofs.
Consequently, the latter theory is more complex (816 lines of code).

We have additionally implemented one of the sections about lifting quantum objects, namely the lifting of pure states (
Section 9.4
).
We chose this one for two reasons:
First, it is a non-trivial application of the theory of complements.
Second, it is one of the most complicated constructions in
Section 9
.
(Recall the various helper constructs such as
\eta_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\xi_{b}
.)
We wanted to make sure that that construction is amenable to formalization.
The answer is positive,
Pure_States
contains both a formalization of the lemmas in
Section 9.4
as well as a formalization of the example from that section (triple CNOT).

When formalizing the existence of complements, we encountered one interesting challenge:
The axiom of the existence of complements (
\autopageref
page:ex.compl) stipulates that for any reference
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, there exists a
\mathbf{C}
and a complement
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
of
F
.
However, in our formalization, the objects
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}
of the category are represented by types
\alpha\ \mathtt{update},\beta\ \mathtt{update},\gamma\ \mathtt{update}
.
So the existence axiom requires that for every
F
, there is a type
\gamma
with certain properties.
But this cannot be expressed in HOL!
(It would require dependent types.)
Instead, we use the following workaround for
finite-dimensional
references:
We require that for any
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
, there exists a
\mathbf{C}
such that for any
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, we have a complement
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
.
This can actually be formalized because now the type
\gamma
depends only on other types
\alpha,\beta
.
Concretely, in Isabelle/HOL, we formalize this by requiring the definition of a type constructor
(
\alpha,\beta
) complement_domain
that we use for
\gamma
, and then require the axiom

\mathtt{reference}\ F\implies\exists G::(\alpha,\beta)\ \mathtt{complement\_domain}\ \mathtt{update}\Rightarrow\beta\ \mathtt{update}.\ \ \texttt{complements}\ F\ G

for
F::\alpha\ \mathtt{update}\Rightarrow\beta\ \mathtt{update}
.
This works well with the rest of our formalization.
However, it makes the proof of existence in the quantum reference category harder:
We need to show that
\mathbf{C}
only depends on
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
, not on
F
.
This, in turn, boils down to showing that the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{C}
depends only on the dimensions of
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
(In fact,
\dim\mathcal{H}_{C}=\frac{\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}}{\dim\mathcal{H}_{A}}
.)
This extra nontrivial proof would not be necessary if
\mathbf{C}
could depend on
F
.

This also shows a problem that we get when formalizing the infinite-dimensional setting:
In that case
\dim\mathcal{H}_{C}=\frac{\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}}{\dim\mathcal{H}_{A}}
does not hold, and
\mathcal{H}_{C}
does not depend solely on
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

Thus we cannot instantiate our axioms for complements in the case of infinite-dimensional quantum references
47
47

47

Proof: Let
\mathcal{H}_{A}
be a countably infinite-dimensional space,
\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\mathcal{H}_{A}
. Let
U
be a unitary from
\mathcal{H}_{B}
to
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n}
(exists for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
).
F:=\mathsf{Sd}_{U}.\mathsf{Fst}
.
Then
\mathcal{H}_{C}
needs to be isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, but there is no way to recover
n
just from knowing
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
(or infinite classical references)!

Fortunately, there is an alternative, somewhat more complicated to use, solution that also works in the infinite-dimensional case:
It involves a poor man’s substitute for dependent types available in Isabelle/HOL:
The
Types_To_Set
extension
[
KP16
]
roughly speaking allows us to locally (within a proof) assume the existence of a type
\gamma
that is isomorphic to some set
S_{\gamma}
that is defined only within this proof context.
(Here isomorphic simply means that there is a bijection between them.)
This can be used for our purposes as follows:
Instead of stating something like “for all types
\alpha
,
\beta
and all references
F:\alpha\ \mathtt{update}\to\beta\ \mathtt{update}
, there exists a type
\gamma
and a complement
G:\gamma\ \mathtt{update}\to\beta\ \mathtt{update}
of
F
” (which cannot be formulated in Isabelle/HOL), we can formulate a lemma that says

\begin{gathered}\text{for all types $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, and all $F:\alpha\ \mathtt{update}\to\beta\ \mathtt{update}$, if $\gamma$ is isomorphic to the set $\mathtt{cdc}\ F$,}\\
\text{then there is a complement $G:\gamma\ \mathtt{update}\to\beta\ \mathtt{update}$ of $F$.}\end{gathered}

(7)

Here
\mathtt{cdc}\ F
is a set that we construct so that it has the desired cardinality (which may depend on
F
);
after proving (
7
) we never need to use the definition of
cdc
again.

Without the
Types_To_Set
mechanism, such a lemma is relatively useless because we cannot construct a suitable type
\gamma
to satisfy the premise of the lemma, unless
F
is globally fixed.
But with
Types_To_Set
, it becomes possible to use it:
If, within a proof, we need a complement of a given register
F
, we locally define the type
\gamma
, and then, within the scope of that definition, have access to a complement of
F
by (
7
).
Once we exit that scope, the complement is not accessible (or even typable) anymore, but any consequences from its existence persist beyond the local scope.

Using
Types_To_Set
is quite complicated (and low-level), but the process is much eased by another as yet unpublished Isabelle-library
With_Type
.

Conclusion.

Overall, we believe that this formalization shows that our approach is
well-suited for handling quantum references in a
theorem prover, and that the references are easy to handle and lead to
low overhead or boilerplate proof code. We believe the same would hold for implementations in other theorem provers than Isabelle/HOL.

11
Conclusion

We have shown how to formalize both classical and quantum references (as
instantiations of a generic theory of references), and how to use them on
the example of a quantum Hoare logic. We have formalized a large part of our results in
Isabelle/HOL (for the quantum case only in finite dimensions) and shown that our theory is easy to use in a formal setting.
Some open research questions include:

•

How do we model references that contain mixed quantum/classical data?
(E.g., classical lists of qubits.)
See
Section 4.3
.

•

Are there other interesting theories of references besides
classical or quantum ones? Or are there variants of the quantum or
classical ones with different nice properties?

•

Completing the formalization: All the results about lifting quantum objects from
Section 9
, currently we only cover lifting of pure states.
Additional automation such as tactics, simplification procedures, etc. for easier reasoning about references. (E.g., all proof steps in the teleportation example,
Section 6.1
, should ideally be solved by a single proof methods invocation.)

Acknowledgments.

We thank the anonymous PlanQC reviewers for useful feedback and pointers. This work was funded by the ERC consolidator grant CerQuS (819317), by the PRG team grant “Secure Quantum Technology” (PRG946) from the Estonian Research Council, and by the Estonian Centre of Exellence in IT (EXCITE) funded by the ERDF.

Appendix A
Proofs of generic laws about references

A.1
Proof of
Lemma 1

Assume
F,G
to be disjoint references. The existence of a reference
FG
as in
Definition 3
follows immediately from Axiom
•
‣
1
.
It remains to show that it is unique, i.e., if
\forall a,b.\ X(a\otimes b)=F(a)\cdot G(b)
for some reference
X
, then
X=FG
.
Since both
FG
and
X
are references, they are also pre-references (Axiom
•
‣
1
).
Furthermore,
X(a\otimes b)=F(a)\cdot G(b)=FG(a\otimes b)
for all
a,b
, so
X=FG
(Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
).

A.2
Proofs for
Figure 2

We show all the laws in

.
The proofs are not fully in the the same order as the laws in the figure because some laws are used in the proofs of earlier ones and therefore need to be shown first.

Proof of
footnote 9
.

Let
M,N
be separating sets for
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
. Let
\mathit{MN}:=\{{a\otimes b:a\in M,b\in N}\}
.
We want to show that
\mathit{MN}
is a separating set for
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.
For this, fix some pre-references
F,G:\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
with
\forall x\in\mathit{MN}.\ F(x)=G(x)
.

Let
F_{a}(b):=F(a\otimes b)
.
Then
F_{a}=F\circ\bigl({\lambda x.\ x\cdot(a\otimes 1)}\bigr)\circ(\lambda x.\ 1\otimes x)
by Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
.
Thus
F_{a}
is a pre-register since it is a composition of pre-registers (Axioms
•
‣
1
,
•
‣
1
,
•
‣
1
,
•
‣
1
).

Let
F_{b}(a):=F(a\otimes b)
,
G_{a}(b):=G(a\otimes b)
, and
G_{b}(a):=G(a\otimes b)
.
Then
F_{b},G_{a},G_{b}
are also pre-references.

Since
\forall x\in\mathit{MN}.\ F(x)=G(x)
, we have
F(a\otimes b)=G(a\otimes b)
for all
a\in N,b\in M
.
Hence
F_{a}(b)=G_{a}(b)
for all
a\in N,b\in M
.
Since
M
is separating, it follows that
F_{a}=G_{a}
for all
a\in N
.

Thus
F(a\otimes b)=G(a\otimes b)
for all
a\in N,b\in\mathbf{B}
.
Hence
F_{b}(a)=G_{b}(a)
for all
a\in N,b\in\mathbf{B}
.
Since
N
is separating, it follows that
F_{b}=G_{b}
for all
b\in\mathbf{B}
.

Thus
F(a\otimes b)=G(a\otimes b)
for all
a\in\mathbf{A},b\in\mathbf{B}
.
By Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
, it follows that
F=G
.

Thus
\mathit{MN}
is a separating set.

This shows the fact from
footnote 9
.

Appendix B
Miscellaneous facts about infinite-dimensional quantum mechanics

In this section, we give a number of lemmas related to (infinite-dimensional) quantum references (i.e., to operator theory) that are used throughout the paper.
Many of them are likely known facts but we did not find citable references.
We recall our convention that
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\dots
always denote
B(\mathcal{H}_{A}),B(\mathcal{H}_{B}),\dots
, the spaces of bounded operators.
Whenever we say reference/pre-reference we mean quantum reference/pre-reference.

Lemma 13
.

For every closed subspace
S
of
\mathcal{H}
, there exists exactly one projector
P_{S}
with image
S
.

Proof.

For the existence see
[
Con13
, comment after Definition II.3.1]
.
48
48

48

We define a projector as a bounded operator with
P^{2}=P
and
P=P^{\dagger}
.

[
Con13
]
gives a different definition of what they call a “projection” but it is equivalent by
[
Con13
, Proposition II.3.3 (d)]
.

Assume there are two projectors
P,Q
with image
S
.
Then
\operatorname{im}Q\subseteq\operatorname{im}P
, so by
[
Con13
, Exercise II.3.6]
,
PQ=Q
.
And
\operatorname{im}P\subseteq\operatorname{im}Q
, so by
[
Con13
, Exercise II.3.6]
,
PQ=P
.
Thus
P=Q
.
∎

Lemma 14
.

The tensor product
S\otimes T
of closed subspaces
S,T
of Hilbert spaces is defined as the closed span of
\{{s\otimes t:s\in S,t\in T}\}
. For a bounded operator
A
between Hilbert spaces, let
AS
denote the closure of the image of
S
under
A
.

Then
AS\otimes BT=(A\otimes B)(T\otimes S)
.

Proof.

In this proof, let
\overline{X}
denote the closure of
X
.
For an operator
A
and a subspace
S
, let
A\cdot S
denotes the image of
S
under
A
, and
AS:=\overline{A\cdot S}
.
Let
f(x,y):=x\otimes y
and
g(x):=(A\otimes B)x
.

\displaystyle AS\otimes BT

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny def}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{\operatorname{span}f(AS\times BT)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny def}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{\operatorname{span}f(\overline{A\cdot S}\times\overline{B\cdot T})}=\overline{\operatorname{span}f(\overline{A\cdot S\times B\cdot T})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{\operatorname{span}\overline{f\bigl({A\cdot S\times B\cdot T}\bigr)}}=\overline{\operatorname{span}{f\bigl({A\cdot S\times B\cdot T}\bigr)}}=\overline{\operatorname{span}{g\bigl({f({S\times T})}\bigr)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{g\bigl({{\operatorname{span}{f({S\times T})}}}\bigr)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny def}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{g\bigl({S\otimes T}\bigr)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny def}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{(A\otimes B)\cdot({S\otimes T})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny def}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}{(A\otimes B)({S\otimes T})}.

Here
(*)
uses that
f
is continuous.
And
(**)
uses that
g
is linear.
∎

Lemma 15
.

Fix a bounded operator
t
.

(i)

t
is trace-class iff
\lvert{t}\rvert
is trace-class.

(ii)

If
t
is trace-class,
\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}\lvert{t}\rvert

(iii)

If
t
is positive trace-class,
\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}t
.

(iv)

If
t
is trace-class and
s\leq t
is a positive operator, then
s
is trace-class.

(v)

If
t
is trace-class,
\lVert{t}\rVert\leq 2\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.

Proof.

We show (
i
).
By definition of trace-class
[
Con00
, Definition 18.3]
,
t
is trace-class iff
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert e<\infty
for some orthonormal basis
E
.
Since
\lvert{t}\rvert=\bigl\lvert{\lvert{t}\rvert}\bigr\rvert
, this holds iff
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\bigl\lvert{\lvert{t}\rvert}\bigr\rvert e<\infty
which by definition holds iff
\lvert{t}\rvert
is trace-class.

We show (
ii
).
By definition
[
Con00
, text after Definition 18.3]
,
\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert e
for some orthonormal basis
E
, and by definition
[
Con00
, Definition 18.10]
,
\operatorname{tr}t=\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}te
for all
t
, and in particular
\operatorname{tr}\lvert{t}\rvert=\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert e
. Thus
\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}\lvert{t}\rvert
.

We show (
iii
). For positive
t
, we have
\lvert{t}\rvert=t
. Thus
\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{lemma:traceclass.abs:norm}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\lvert{t}\rvert=\operatorname{tr}t
.

We show (
iv
).
By definition
[
Con00
, Definition 18.3]
,
t
is trace-class means that
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert e
converges.
Since
s
is positive and
t\geq s
,
t
is positive. Thus

e^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert e=e^{\dagger}te\geq e^{\dagger}se=e^{\dagger}\lvert{s}\rvert e\geq 0.

Hence
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{s}\rvert e
converges as well.
By definition, this means that
s
is trace-class.

We show (
v
).
Fix positive a trace-class
t
. Then there is a bounded operator
a
with
a^{\dagger}a=t
.
Fix an arbitrary vector
\psi
with
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1
. Fix an orthonormal basis
E
with
\psi\in E
.
Then

\lVert{a\psi}\rVert^{2}=\psi^{\dagger}a^{\dagger}a\psi=\psi^{\dagger}t\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}te\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}t.

(9)

Here
(*)
follows since
t
is positive and hence
e^{\dagger}te\geq 0
for all
e
.
And
(**)
is by definition of the trace
[
Con00
, Definition 18.10]
.

Since this holds for any unit vector
\psi
, we have (with
\psi
ranging over unit vectors):

\lVert{t}\rVert=\lVert{a^{\dagger}a}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{a}\rVert^{2}=\sup_{\psi}\lVert{a\psi}\rVert^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:norm.apsi}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\operatorname{tr}t.

(10)

Here
(*)
holds by
[
Con13
, Proposition II.2.7]
.
So (
10
) holds for any positive trace-class
t
.

Fix a Hermitian trace-class
t
. By
[
Con00
, Proposition 3.2]
,
t=t_{+}-t_{-}
where
t_{+}
and
t_{-}
are the “positive and negative part” of
t
, and
t_{+},t_{-}
are positive.
Furthermore,
\lvert{t}\rvert=t_{+}+t_{-}
by
[
Con00
, Exercise 3.4]
.
Since
\lvert{t}\rvert
is trace-class by (
i
), and
t_{+},t_{i}\leq\lvert{t}\rvert
, we have that
t_{+},t_{-}
are trace-class by (
iv
).
Then

\lVert{t}\rVert=\lVert{t_{+}-t_{-}}\rVert\leq\lVert{t_{+}}\rVert+\lVert{t_{-}}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:norm.pos}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\operatorname{tr}t_{+}+\operatorname{tr}t_{-}=\operatorname{tr}\lvert{t}\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{lemma:traceclass.abs:norm}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}.

(11)

This holds for any Hermitian trace-class
t
.

Fix a trace-class
t
. Then
t=t_{h}+it_{a}
where
t_{h}:=(t+t^{\dagger})/2
and
t_{a}:=(t-t^{\dagger})/2i
.
Both
t_{h},t_{a}
are easily verified to be Hermitian.
Thus

\displaystyle\lVert{t}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert{t_{h}+it_{a}}\rVert\leq\lVert{t_{h}}\rVert+\lVert{t_{a}}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:norm.herm}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\lVert{t_{h}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\lVert{t_{a}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{t/2+t^{\dagger}/2}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\lVert{t/2-t^{\dagger}/2i}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}/2+\lVert{t^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}/2+\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}/2+\lVert{t^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}/2\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}2\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}.

Here
(*)
follows since
\lVert{t^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
by
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (f)]
.
Thus holds for any trace-class
t
and thus shows (
v
).
∎

Lemma 16

(Circularity of the trace)
.

Let
a=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
where
a_{i}
are bounded operators (not necessarily square).
Let
b=b_{1}\cdots b_{m}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}
where the
b_{i}
are bounded operators (not necessarily square), but one of them is a (square) trace-class operator.
49
49

49

A
square
is an operator with equal domain and co-domain.
E.g.,
x:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}
is square and
y:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
with
\mathcal{H}_{A}\neq\mathcal{H}_{B}
is not.

(i)

If
b
(as defined above) is square and
u
is an isometry (not necessarily square), then
b
and
ubu^{\dagger}
are trace-class and
\operatorname{tr}ubu^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}b
and
\lVert{ubu^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.

(ii)

The square operators
ab
and
ba
are trace-class and
\operatorname{tr}ab=\operatorname{tr}ba
.

Note that, e.g.,
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (e)]
states the same as
item ii
but it only applies when the involved operators are square.

Proof.

First, we show that for any isometry
u:\mathcal{H}_{t}\to\mathcal{H}_{u}
, and any
t\in B(\mathcal{H}_{t})
,

utu^{\dagger}\text{ is trace-class}\iff t\text{ is trace-class}.

(12)

Note that
\lvert{utu^{\dagger}}\rvert=u\lvert{t}\rvert u^{\dagger}
.
Fix an orthonormal basis
T
of
\mathcal{H}_{t}
.
Then
uT
is orthonormal and can be extended to an orthonormal basis
V\supseteq uT
.
Then for
\psi\in V\setminus uT
,
u^{\dagger}\psi=0
and thus
\psi^{\dagger}u\lvert{t}\rvert u^{\dagger}\psi=0
.

By definition of trace-class
[
Con00
, Definition 18.3]
,
utu^{\dagger}
is trace-class iff
\sum_{\psi\in V}\psi^{\dagger}\lvert{utu^{\dagger}}\rvert\psi<\infty
iff
\sum_{\psi\in V}\psi^{\dagger}u\lvert{t}\rvert u^{\dagger}\psi<\infty

iff
\sum_{\psi\in uT}\psi^{\dagger}u\lvert{t}\rvert u^{\dagger}\psi<\infty

iff
\sum_{\phi\in T}(u\phi)^{\dagger}u\lvert{t}\rvert u^{\dagger}(u\phi)<\infty
(because
u
is a bijection between
T
and
uT
)
iff
\sum_{\phi\in T}\phi^{\dagger}\lvert{t}\rvert\phi<\infty
(because
u^{\dagger}u=1
)
iff
t
is trace-class (by definition of trace-class).

Next, we show any square operator
e:\mathcal{H}_{e}\to\mathcal{H}_{e}
that is the product of bounded operators and at least one trace-class operator, we have that

e
is trace-class.

(13)

We can write
e
as
e=ctd
for
t\in T(\mathcal{H}_{t})
and bounded
c:\mathcal{H}_{t}\to\mathcal{H}_{e}
and
d:\mathcal{H}_{e}\to\mathcal{H}_{t}
(for some Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{t}
).
Let
U_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{t}\to\mathcal{H}_{t}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{e}
and
U_{e}:\mathcal{H}_{e}\to\mathcal{H}_{t}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{e}
be arbitrary isometries (e.g., the canonical embeddings).
Then
U_{e}ctdU_{e}^{\dagger}=U_{e}cU_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{t}tU_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{t}dU_{e}^{\dagger}
. The right hand side is a product of square operators, and
U_{t}tU_{t}^{\dagger}
is trace-class by (
12
).
Thus their product
U_{e}ctdU_{e}^{\dagger}
is trace-class
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (a)]
.
By (
12
), this implies that
e=ctd
is trace-class. This shows (
13
).

We show (
i
).
We have that
b
and
ubu^{\dagger}
are trace-class by (
13
) (with
e:=b
and
e:=ubu^{\dagger}
, respectively).
Since
b
is square,
b:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
u:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{u}
for some
\mathcal{H}_{u}
.
Fix an orthonormal basis
A
of
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
Then
uA
is orthonormal and can be extended to an orthonormal basis
T\supseteq uA
.
By definition of the trace
[
Con00
, Definition 18.10]
,
\operatorname{tr}b=\sum_{\psi\in A}\psi^{\dagger}b\psi
and
\operatorname{tr}ubu^{\dagger}=\sum_{\psi\in T}\psi^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}\psi
.
Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}ubu^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\psi\in uA}\psi^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}\psi+\sum_{\hskip-28.45274pt\psi\in T\setminus uA\hskip-28.45274pt}\psi^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{\psi\in uA}\psi^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{\phi\in A}(u\phi)^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}(u\phi)=\sum_{\phi\in A}\phi^{\dagger}u^{\dagger}ubu^{\dagger}u\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$\scriptscriptstyle(\!*\!*\!*\!)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{\phi\in A}\phi^{\dagger}b\phi=\operatorname{tr}b.

Here
(*)
follows because
\psi\in T\setminus uA
is orthogonal to the image of
u
and thus
u^{\dagger}\psi=0
.
And
(**)
follows because
u
is injective and thus a bijection between
A
and
uA
.
And
(*{*}*)
follows because
u
is an isometry and thus
u^{\dagger}u=1
.

Finally, we show that
\lVert{ubu^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.
Note that
\lvert{ubu^{\dagger}}\rvert=u\lvert{b}\rvert u^{\dagger}
.
Since
b
is trace-class,
\lvert{b}\rvert
is trace-class (
item i
).
Thus we can apply the part of (
i
) that we have shown so far, namely that
\operatorname{tr}ubu^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}b
, to
\lvert{b}\rvert
instead of
b
.
That is, we have
\operatorname{tr}u\lvert{b}\rvert u^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}\lvert{b}\rvert
.
Thus
\lVert{ubu^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\lvert{ubu^{\dagger}}\rvert=\operatorname{tr}u\lvert{b}\rvert u^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}\lvert{b}\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
where
(*)
is by
item ii
.

This shows (
i
).

We show (
ii
).
The fact that
ab
and
ba
are trace-class follows from (
13
) because
ab
and
ba
are square.
Let
U_{a}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{B}
and
U_{b}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{B}
be arbitrary isometries (e.g., the canonical embeddings).
Then we have

\operatorname{tr}ab\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{lemma:circ.trace:ubu}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}U_{b}abU_{b}^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}U_{b}aU_{a}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{a}bU_{b}^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}U_{a}bU_{b}^{\dagger}\cdot U_{b}aU_{a}^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}U_{a}baU_{a}^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{lemma:circ.trace:ubu}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}ba.

Here the
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{lemma:circ.trace:ubu}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}
use (
i
) with
b:=ab
and
b:=ba
, respectively.
And
(*)
is since
U_{a},U_{b}
are isometries.
And
(**)
follows since
U_{a}bU_{b}^{\dagger}
and
U_{b}aU_{a}^{\dagger}
are both bounded square operators, and
U_{a}bU_{b}^{\dagger}
is trace-class by (
13
), and thus we can apply the circularity of the trace for square operators
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.10 (e)]
.

This shows (
ii
).
∎

Lemma 17
.

x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
,
x\mapsto UxU^{\dagger}
,

x\mapsto xy
, and
y\mapsto xy
are all weak*-continuous and bounded.
(Not linear though in the case of
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
.)
Here
x,y\in\mathbf{A}
are bounded operators and
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
is unitary.

Proof.

First, we show the auxiliary fact that
\operatorname{tr}x^{\dagger}=\overline{\operatorname{tr}x}
.
(
\textstyle\overline{c}
is the complex conjugate of
c
.)
By definition of the trace
[
Con00
, Definition 18.10]
,
\operatorname{tr}x^{\dagger}=\sum_{e}e^{\dagger}x^{\dagger}e=\sum_{e}\overline{e^{\dagger}xe}=\overline{\sum_{e}e^{\dagger}xe}=\overline{\operatorname{tr}x}
where
e
ranges over an orthonormal basis. Thus
\operatorname{tr}x^{\dagger}=\overline{\operatorname{tr}x}
.

We show that
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous and bounded.
It is bounded because
\lVert{x^{\dagger}}\rVert=\lVert{x}\rVert

[
Con13
, Proposition 2.7]
. We show weak*-continuity: Consider a net
x_{i}
that weak*-converges to
x
. Then
\operatorname{tr}ax_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}ax
for all trace-class
a
.
Then for all trace-class
b
,
\operatorname{tr}b(x_{i}^{\dagger}-x^{\dagger})=\overline{\operatorname{tr}(x_{i}-x)b^{\dagger}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{\operatorname{tr}b^{\dagger}(x_{i}-x)}\to 0
.
Here
(*)
is the circularity of the trace
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (e)]
. And
\to 0
follows since
\operatorname{tr}ax_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}ax
holds in particular with
a:=b^{\dagger}
.
Thus
x_{i}^{\dagger}\to x^{\dagger}
in the weak*-topology.
So
x_{i}\to x
implies
x_{i}^{\dagger}\to x^{\dagger}
for every net
x_{i}
. Hence
x\mapsto x^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous.

We show that
x\mapsto UxU^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous and normal.
It is a *-isomorphism.
Thus is it completely positive
[
Con00
, Example 34.3 (a)]
.
Thus it is bounded
[
Con00
, Proposition 33.4]
.
And by
[
Con00
, Proposition 46.6]
, *-isomorphisms between von Neumann algebras are weak*-continuous, hence
x\mapsto UxU^{\dagger}
is weak*-continuous.

Let
F(y):=xy
. To show that
F
is weak*-continuous, we need
to show that for any net
y_{i}
, if
y_{i}
weak*-converges to
y
,
then
F(y_{i})
weak*-converges to
F(y)
. Assume
y_{i}

weak*-converges to
y
. By definition of the weak*-topology,

\operatorname{tr}ay_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}ay
for any trace-class operator
a
. Thus

\operatorname{tr}bF(y_{i})=\operatorname{tr}(bx)y_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}(bx)y=\operatorname{tr}bF(y)
for any trace-class
operator
b
. (Since
bx
is trace-class if
b
is trace-class and
x
is bounded
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (a)]
.)
Thus
F(y_{i})
weak*-converges to
F(y)
.
Hence
F
is weak*-continuous.
Weak*-continuity of
F^{\prime}(x):=xy
is proven analogously, using additionally that
\operatorname{tr}ax_{i}=\operatorname{tr}x_{i}a
by the
circularity of the trace.

F,F^{\prime}
are
\lVert{a}\rVert
-bounded because
\lVert{ax}\rVert,\lVert{xa}\rVert\leq\lVert{a}\rVert\lVert{x}\rVert
for bounded operators
a,x
.
Hence
F,F^{\prime}
are weak*-continuous and bounded.
∎

Lemma 18
.

For bounded operators
a
,
b
, we have
\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert=\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert
.
In particular, if
a,b
are positive, so is
a\otimes b
.

Proof.

We first show the “in particular” part. If
a
and
b
are positive, by definition, there are operators
\hat{a},\hat{b}
such that
\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}=a
,
\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}=b
.
Then
(\hat{a}\otimes\hat{b})^{\dagger}(\hat{a}\otimes\hat{b})=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\otimes\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}=a\otimes b
.
(That
\cdot^{\dagger}
and multiplication commute with
\otimes
is shown in
[
Tak79
, Equation IV.1 (5), (6)]
.)
Thus
a\otimes b
is positive.

We now show
\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert=\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert
. By definition,
\lvert{a}\rvert
is the unique positive operator with
\lvert{a}\rvert^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert=a^{\dagger}a
. Analogously for
\lvert{b}\rvert
and
\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert
. Then
\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert
is positive by the “in particular” part. Furthermore
(\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert)^{\dagger}(\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert)=\lvert{a}\rvert^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert^{\dagger}\lvert{b}\rvert=a^{\dagger}a\otimes b^{\dagger}b=(a\otimes b)^{\dagger}(a\otimes b)
. Thus

\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert
is
\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert
.
∎

Lemma 19
.

The
algebraic tensor product

\mathbf{A}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}$}}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.
(
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is defined as the subspace of
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
spanned by the operators
a\otimes b
for
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
b\in\mathbf{B}
.)

Proof.

The
spatial tensor product

\mathbf{A}{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}$}}}\mathbf{B}
is defined as the norm-closure of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
Thus
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is norm-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
. Since the weak*-topology is coarser than the norm-topology,

\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is also weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
.

The tensor product
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
of von Neumann algebras is defined as the smallest von Neumann algebra containing
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}

[
Tak79
, Definition IV.1.3]
.
By definition of von Neumann algebras
[
Con00
, Definition 13.1]
), this is the SOT-closure (strong operator topology) of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
Since the norm-topology is finer than the SOT, the SOT-closure of the norm-closure of a set is the SOT-closure. Thus
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
is also the SOT-closure of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
.

We will now show that
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
:
Fix
x\in\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
. W.l.o.g.
\lVert{x}\rVert\leq 1
.
Then
x
is in the intersection of the unit ball
S
with SOT-closure of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
.
By the Kaplansky Density Theorem
[
Con00
, Theorem 44.1 (a)]
, and since
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
is a C*-algebra, this is the same as the SOT-closure of
S\cap(\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B})
.
S\cap(\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B})
is a convex subset of
B(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
, so its SOT-closure equals its WOT-closure
[
Con00
, Corollary 8.2]
. So
x
is in the WOT-closure
of
S\cap(\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B})
.
Thus there is a net
x_{i}
with
x_{i}\in S\cap(\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B})
that WOT-converges to
x
.
Since
x_{i}\in S\cap(\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B})
is a bounded subset of
B(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
,
the WOT and the weak*-topology coincide on it
[
Con00
, Proposition 20.1 (b)]
).
So
x_{i}
weak*-converges to
x
. So
x
is in the weak*-closure of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
. Since this holds for any
x
,
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.

So
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
