\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{sp}}\mathbf{B}
which is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
. So
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Let
U_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})
be the unitary with
U_{\alpha}\bigl({(\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi}\bigr)=\psi\otimes(\phi\otimes\xi)
.
(This is the canonical isomorphism between those spaces.)
Fix
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
,
\phi\!\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
,
\psi\!\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
,
\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
Assume that
\psi,\xi\neq 0
and
\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\psi\otimes\phi\!\xi)
.
Then there exists a
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
\psi\!\phi=\psi\otimes\phi
and
\phi\!\xi=\phi\otimes\xi
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\psi\otimes\phi\!\xi)=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes 1)(\psi\otimes\phi\!\xi)=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes(1\otimes 1))(\psi\otimes\phi\!\xi)

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes(1\otimes 1))U_{\alpha}(\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi)=((\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes 1)\otimes 1)(\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi)=(\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes 1)\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi.

Since
\xi\neq 0
, this implies
\psi\!\phi=(\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes 1)\psi\!\phi
.
Thus
\psi\!\phi
is in the image of the projector
\psi\psi^{\dagger}\otimes 1
.
Hence
\psi\!\phi
is of the form
\psi\!\phi=\psi\otimes\phi
for some
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
as desired.

Then

\displaystyle\psi\otimes\phi\!\xi=U_{\alpha}(\psi\!\phi\otimes\xi)=U_{\alpha}((\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi)=\psi\otimes(\phi\otimes\xi).

Since
\psi\neq 0
, this implies
\phi\!\xi=\phi\otimes\xi
are desired.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Let
U_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})
be the unitary with
U_{\alpha}((\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi)=\psi\otimes(\phi\otimes\xi)
.
(This is the canonical isomorphism between those spaces.)
Fix
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
b\!c\in\mathbf{B}\otimes\mathbf{C}
,
a\!b\in\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
,
c\in\mathbf{C}
.
Assume that
a,c\neq 0
and
a\!b\otimes c=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(a\otimes b\!c)U_{\alpha}
.
Then there exists some
b\in\mathbf{B}
such that
a\!b=a\otimes b
and
b\!c=b\otimes c
.

Proof.

Let
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}
be some vector with
c\xi_{0}\neq 0
.
Let
\psi_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
be some vector with
a\psi_{0}\neq 0
.

For any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A},\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
, we have

a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes c\xi_{0}=(a\!b\otimes c)((\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi_{0})=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(a\otimes b\!c)U_{\alpha}((\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi_{0})=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(a\psi\otimes b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi_{0})).

By
Lemma 20
(with
\psi\!\phi:=a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)
,
\xi:=c\xi_{0}
,
\phi\!\xi:=b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi_{0})
,
\psi:=a\psi
), this implies that there exists an
\eta
with
a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes\eta
.
(In the case
a\psi=0
, the lemma does not apply, but in this case
a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=0
and thus
a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes\eta
holds for any
\eta
.)

Thus we can fix some
\eta_{\psi\phi}
such that
a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes\eta_{\psi\phi}
for all
\psi,\phi
.
And analogously some
\theta_{\phi\xi}
such that
b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi)=\theta_{\phi\xi}\otimes c\xi
for all
\phi,\xi
.

Let
b(\phi):=\eta_{\psi_{0}\phi}
.

Note that we have
a\!b(\psi_{0}\otimes\phi)=a\psi_{0}\otimes b(\phi)
which determines
b(\phi)
uniquely since
a\psi_{0}\neq 0
.
Furthermore,
a\!b(\psi_{0}\otimes(s\phi+\phi^{\prime}))=a\psi_{0}\otimes(sb(\phi)+b(\phi^{\prime}))
for all
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
.
Thus
b(s\phi+\phi^{\prime})=sb(\phi)+b(\phi^{\prime})
.
Thus
b
is linear.
Then for all
\phi
,

\lVert{b(\phi)}\rVert=\frac{\lVert{a\psi_{0}\otimes b(\phi)}\rVert}{\lVert{a\psi_{0}}\rVert}=\frac{\lVert{a\!b(\psi_{0}\otimes\phi)}\rVert}{\lVert{a\psi_{0}}\rVert}\leq\frac{\lVert{a\!b}\rVert\cdot\lVert{\psi_{0}\otimes\phi}\rVert}{\lVert{a\psi_{0}}\rVert}=\frac{\lVert{a\!b}\rVert\cdot\lVert{\psi_{0}}\rVert}{\lVert{a\psi_{0}}\rVert}\cdot\lVert{\phi}\rVert.

Thus
b
is bounded.
Since
b
is a bounded operator, we will from now on write
b\phi
instead of
b(\phi)
.

For any
\psi,\phi,\xi
, we have

(a\psi\otimes\eta_{\psi\phi})\otimes c\xi=(a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi))\otimes c\xi=(a\!b\otimes c)((\psi\otimes\phi)\otimes\xi)\\
=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(a\psi\otimes b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi))=U_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(a\psi\otimes(\theta_{\phi\xi}\otimes c\xi))=(a\psi\otimes\theta_{\xi\phi})\otimes c\xi.

Thus whenever
a\phi,c\xi\neq 0
, we have
\eta_{\psi\phi}=\theta_{\phi\xi}
.

For
\psi,\phi
with
a\psi\neq 0
, we then have

a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes\eta_{\psi\phi}=a\psi\otimes\theta_{\phi\xi_{0}}=a\psi\otimes\eta_{\psi_{0}\phi}=a\psi\otimes b\phi=(a\otimes b)(\psi\otimes\phi).

And for
\psi,\phi
with
a\psi=0
, we have

a\!b(\psi\otimes\phi)=a\psi\otimes\eta_{\psi\phi}=0=a\psi\otimes b\phi=(a\otimes b)(\psi\otimes\phi).

as well. Thus
a\!b=a\otimes b
on all
\psi\otimes\phi
. Thus
a\!b=a\otimes b
as desired.

For
\phi,\xi
with
c\xi\neq 0
, we then have

b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi)=\theta_{\phi\xi}\otimes c\xi=\eta_{\psi_{0}\phi}\otimes c\xi=b\phi\otimes c\xi=(b\otimes c)(\phi\otimes\xi).

And for
\phi,\xi
with
c\xi=0
, we have

b\!c(\phi\otimes\xi)=\theta_{\phi\xi}\otimes c\xi=0=b\phi\otimes c\xi=(b\otimes c)(\phi\otimes\xi).

as well. Thus
b\!c=b\otimes c
on all
\phi\otimes\xi
. Thus
b\!c=b\otimes c
as desired.
∎

Lemma 22
.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a reference, then
F
is linear, bounded, continuous, weak*-continuous, unital, completely positive, a *-homomorphism, and normal.

Proof.

By definition,
F
is weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphisms.
A *-homomorphism is linear by definition.
By
[
Con00
, Example 34.3 (a)]
*-homomorphisms are completely positive.
Thus
F
is completely positive.
Positive linear maps are bounded by
[
Con00
, Proposition 33.4]
.
Thus
F
is bounded.
Bounded linear maps are continuous, so
F
is continuous.

[
Tak79
, Definition III.2.15]
defines a normal map as a continuous weak*-continuous linear map.
[
Con00
, Definition 46.1]
defines a normal map differently;
by
[
Con00
, Theorem 46.4]
their definition of normal is equivalent to a positive weak*-continuous linear map.
Since we already showed that
F
is continuous, weak*-continuous, and positive,
F
is normal according to both definitions.
∎

Lemma 23
.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a reference, then there exists a
Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
and a unitary

U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})U^{\dagger}
for all
a\in\mathbf{A}
.
50
50

50

The converse follows from
Lemma 17
together with Axiom
•
‣
1
that we prove later in
Appendix C
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 22
,
F
is a normal *-homomorphism.

[
Tak79
, Theorem IV.5.5]
states that a normal homomorphism
F
between von Neumann algebras
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
(and in particular our
F
) is of the form
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}})_{e^{\prime}}U^{\dagger}
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{D}
and some projection
e^{\prime}\in\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{D}
and some unitary
U
.
Here
\textstyle\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
denotes the
commutant
of
\mathbf{A}
, i.e., the set
of all bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
that commute with all
operators in
\mathbf{A}
. And
\textstyle(x)_{e^{\prime}}
(the

induction map
) denotes the operator
x
,
restricted in input and output to the image
\mathcal{H}_{e}
of the
projection
e^{\prime}

[
Tak79
, Definition II.3.11]
. (I.e.,

(x)_{e^{\prime}}
is a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{e}
. Note that

\mathcal{H}_{e}
is a Hilbert space since it is a closed subspace of

\mathcal{H}_{D}
.)

Since in our case,
\mathbf{A}
is the set of
all
bounded
operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, we have
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}1
, the set of all multiples of the identity. Thus
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{D}
. Since
x\mapsto 1\otimes x
is an isomorphism
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
and thus surjective onto
\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{D}
, we have that
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{D}
implies that
e^{\prime}
is of the form
1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes e_{0}^{\prime}
for some
e_{0}^{\prime}
. Thus
\mathcal{H}_{e}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
where we define
\mathcal{H}_{C}
as the image of
e_{0}
. So
(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}})_{e^{\prime}}
is the restriction of
a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}}
to
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
, i.e.,
a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}}
. Thus

F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})U^{\dagger}
as desired.
∎

Lemma 24
.

If
F
is a quantum reference, then
\lVert{F(a)}\rVert=\lVert{a}\rVert
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 23
,
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}
for unitary
U
.
Thus
\lVert{F(a)}\rVert=\lVert{U(a\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{a\otimes 1}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{a}\rVert\cdot\lVert{1}\rVert=\lVert{a}\rVert
.
Here
(*)
follows because
U
is unitary and
(**)
follows by
[
Tak79
, Equation IV.1 (7)]
.
∎

Lemma 25
.

If
F
is a reference, then
F
is injective.

Proof.

If
F(a)=0
, then
\lVert{a}\rVert=\lVert{F(a)}\rVert=0
by
Lemma 24
.
Thus
a=0
.
Since
F
is linear, this implies that
F
is injective.

∎

Lemma 26
.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is an iso-reference, then there exists a unitary

U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

F(a)=UaU^{\dagger}
for all
a\in\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 23
,
F(a)=V(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})V^{\dagger}
for some
\mathbf{C}
and unitary
V
.
Fix some arbitrary unit vector
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{C}
.

The function
x\mapsto VxV^{\dagger}
is bijective (with inverse
b\mapsto V^{\dagger}bV
), and
F
is bijective, hence
a\mapsto a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}}
is bijective as a function
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{C}
, too.
Then exists an
a_{*}\in\mathbf{A}
such that
a_{*}\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}}=1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.
Since the rhs is nonzero,
a_{*}
is nonzero.
Then there exists an
\alpha\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
such that
\alpha^{\dagger}a_{*}\alpha\neq 0

[
Con13
, Proposition II.2.15]
.
Let
c:={\alpha^{\dagger}\alpha/\alpha^{\dagger}a_{*}\alpha}\neq 0
.

For all
\gamma\in\mathcal{H}_{C}
:

\gamma^{\dagger}1_{\mathbf{C}}\gamma=\frac{(\alpha\otimes\gamma)^{\dagger}(a_{*}\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})(\alpha\otimes\gamma)}{\alpha^{\dagger}a_{*}\alpha}=\frac{(\alpha\otimes\gamma)^{\dagger}(1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\psi\psi^{\dagger})(\alpha\otimes\gamma)}{\alpha^{\dagger}a_{*}\alpha}=\frac{\alpha^{\dagger}\alpha\cdot\gamma^{\dagger}\psi\psi^{\dagger}\gamma}{\alpha^{\dagger}a_{*}\alpha}=\gamma^{\dagger}(c\psi\psi^{\dagger})\gamma.

By
[
Con13
, Proposition II.2.15]
, this implies
1_{\mathbf{C}}=c\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.
And
c=\psi^{\dagger}(c\psi\psi^{\dagger})\psi=\psi^{\dagger}1_{\mathbf{C}}\psi=1
.
Hence
1_{\mathbf{C}}=\psi\psi^{\dagger}
.

Then
W:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
,
Wx:=x\otimes\psi
is an isometry.
And we have

VWaW^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}=V(a\otimes\psi\psi^{\dagger})V^{\dagger}=V(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})V^{\dagger}=F(a).

With
U:=VW
, we have
F(a)=UaU^{\dagger}
.
And since
F
is surjective, so is
U
.
And since
V
is unitary and
W
an isometry,
U
is an isometry.
A surjective isometry is unitary, so
U
is unitary.
∎

Lemma 27
.

For references
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}
, there exists a reference
T:\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}\otimes\mathbf{D}
with
T(a\otimes b)=F(a)\otimes G(b)
for all
a,b
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 22
,
F,G
are both completely positive weak*-continuous maps.
By
[
Tak79
, Proposition IV.5.13]
, there is a weak*-continuous completely positive linear map
T:\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}\otimes\mathbf{D}
such that
T(a\otimes b)=F(a)\otimes G(b)
for all
a,b
.
(Note that
[
Tak79
]
uses the word
\sigma
-weak for weak*.)

For
x=a\otimes b
, we have
T(x^{\dagger})=T(x)^{\dagger}
because
F,G
preserve adjoints by definition of references.
Since
T(\cdot^{\dagger})
,
T(\cdot)^{\dagger}
are antilinear, this implies
T(x^{\dagger})=T(x)^{\dagger}
for
x\in\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
Since
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
by
Lemma 19

and
T
and
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
are weak*-continuous (
Lemma 17
), this implies
T(x^{\dagger})=T(x)^{\dagger}
on
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.

For
x=a\otimes b
,
y=c\otimes d
,
T(xy)=T(x)T(y)
because
F,G
are multiplicative.
By linearity,
T(xy)=T(x)T(y)
for
x,y\in\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
For fixed
x\in\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
,
y\mapsto T(xy)
and
y\mapsto T(x)T(y)
are weak*-continuous (
Lemma 17
, together with weak*-continuity of
T
), and they coincide on
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
Since
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
, they coincide on
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
as well.
Thus, for fixed
y\in\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
,
x\mapsto T(xy)
and
x\mapsto T(x)T(y)
coincide on
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
.
And they are weak*-continuous by
Lemma 17
, together with weak*-continuity of
T
.
Thus by weak*-density of
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
, they coincide on

\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
. Hence
T(xy)=T(x)T(y)
for all
x,y
. Thus
T
is multiplicative.

We have
T(1)=T(1\otimes 1)=F(1)\otimes G(1)=1\otimes 1=1
. Thus
T
is unital.

Altogether,
T
is a reference.
∎

Lemma 28
.

The norm is monotonous on the set of positive bounded operators.
The trace norm is monotonous on the set of positive trace-class operators.

Proof.

For positive bounded
a
, we have

\lVert{a}\rVert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{\sqrt{a}}\rVert^{2}=\sup_{\psi}\lVert{\sqrt{a}\psi}\rVert^{2}=\sup_{\psi}\psi^{\dagger}\sqrt{a}^{\dagger}\sqrt{a}\psi=\sup_{\psi}\psi^{\dagger}a\psi.

Here
(*)
uses
[
Con13
, Proposition II.2.7]
.
And the suprema range over unit vectors
\psi
.
Let
a\leq b
be positive bounded operators.
Then

\lVert{b}\rVert=\sup_{\psi}(\psi^{*}a\psi+\psi^{*}(b-a)\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\geq}}\sup_{\psi}\psi^{*}a\psi=\lVert{a}\rVert.

Here
(*)
uses that
\psi^{*}(b-a)\psi\geq 0
since
b-a
is positive.
So the norm is monotonous on the set of positive bounded operators.

For positive trace-class
a
,
\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}a
(
item iii
).
Let
a\leq b
be positive trace-class operators.
Then
\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}(a+(b-a))=\operatorname{tr}a+\operatorname{tr}(b-a)=\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\lVert{b-a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\geq\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.
Here we use that
b-a
is positive since
a\leq b
.
So the trace norm is monotonous on the set of positive trace-class operators.
∎

Lemma 29
.

Let
a_{i}
be an increasing net of positive bounded operators. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

{\lVert{a_{i}}\rVert}
is upper-bounded.

(ii)

a_{i}
has a supremum in the set of bounded operators.

(iii)

a_{i}
converges in the weak operator topology (WOT).

(iv)

a_{i}
converges in the strong operator topology (SOT).

(v)

a_{i}
converges in the weak*-topology.

In cases (
ii
)–(
v
), the supremum and the limits coincide.

Note that “
a_{i}
converges in the norm topology” is not equivalent to statements (
i
)–(
v
).
For example, consider the operators
a_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\lvert{j}\rangle\langle{j}\rvert
over the Hilbert space
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
. We have
a_{i}\to 1
in the topologies mentioned in the theorem, but
a_{i}\to 1
does not hold with respect to the norm-topology because
\lVert{a_{i}-1}\rVert=1\not\to 0
.

Proof.

By
[
Con00
, Proposition 43.1]
, (
i
) implies (
ii
), (
iii
), (
iv
), (
v
), and the supremum and the three limits coincide.

The SOT and weak*-topology are both finer than the WOT. (See
[
Tak79
, overview after Definition II.2.3]
. The weak*-topology is called
\sigma
-weak there.) Hence (
iv
), (
v
) each imply (
iii
).

We now show that (
iii
) implies (
ii
).
Assume
a_{i}\to a
in the WOT.
By definition of the WOT, this means
x^{\dagger}a_{i}x\to x^{\dagger}ax\in\mathbb{C}
for all vectors
x
.
Furthermore,
x^{\dagger}a_{i}x
is increasing since
a_{i}
is increasing.
Thus
x^{\dagger}a_{i}x\leq x^{\dagger}ax
for all
i
and
x
.
It follows that
x^{\dagger}ax=\lim x^{\dagger}a_{i}x=\sup x^{\dagger}a_{i}x
.
And
x^{\dagger}a_{i}x\leq x^{\dagger}ax
also implies
a_{i}\leq a

[
Con00
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Fix some
b\leq a
such that
b\geq a_{i}
for all
i
.
Then
x^{\dagger}bx\geq x^{\dagger}a_{i}x
for all
i
.
Since
x^{\dagger}ax=\sup x^{\dagger}a_{i}x
, this implies
x^{\dagger}bx\geq x^{\dagger}ax
.
And from
b\leq a
, we get
x^{\dagger}bx\leq x^{\dagger}ax
.
Thus
x^{\dagger}bx=x^{\dagger}ax
.
Thus
b=a
by
[
Con13
, Proposition II.2.15]
.
This holds for any
b\leq a
with
b\geq a_{i}
for all
i
, hence
a=\sup a_{i}
.
This shows that (
iii
) implies (
ii
).

We now show that (
ii
) implies (
i
).
Let
a
be the supremum that exists by (
ii
).
Then
a\geq a_{i}
for all
i
.
Then by
Lemma 28
,
\lVert{a}\rVert\geq\lVert{a_{i}}\rVert
for all
i
.
Thus
\lVert{a_{i}}\rVert
is bounded.

Thus we have the following implications:
(
i
)
(
ii
)
(
iii
)
(
iv
)
(
v
)

Hence all five statements are equivalent.
And if any of them holds, (
i
) holds and the supremum and the three limits coincide as mentioned in the beginning of the proof.
∎

Lemma 30
.

Let
a_{i}
be an increasing net of positive trace-class operators. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{tr}a_{i}
(or equivalently
\lVert{a_{i}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
) is upper-bounded.

(ii)

a_{i}
has a supremum in the set of trace-class operators.

(iii)

a_{i}
converges to a trace-class operator in the WOT.

(iv)

a_{i}
converges to a trace-class operator in the SOT.

(v)

a_{i}
converges to a trace-class operator in the weak*-topology.

(vi)

a_{i}
converges to a trace-class operator w.r.t. the trace norm.

(vii)

a_{i}
converges to a trace-class operator w.r.t. the operator norm.

In cases (
ii
)–(
vii
), the supremum and the limits coincide.

Proof.

The “or equivalently
\lVert{a_{i}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
” part in (
i
) follows by
item iii
.

Statements (
ii
),(
iii
),(
iv
),(
v
) are equivalent by
Lemma 29
(and the supremum/limits are the same in those four statements).

Since the trace norm is monotonous (
Lemma 28
), (
ii
)
implies (
i
).

We next show that (
i
) implies (
vi
).
By
[
Con00
, Theorem 19.1]
, the space of trace-class operators is isometrically isomorphic to the dual of the space of compact operators.
The dual of a normed space is a Banach space
[
Con13
, Proposition III.5.4]
, hence the dual of the space of compact operators is a Banach space.
Thus the space of trace-class operators is isometrically isomorphic to a Banach space and thus it is a Banach space.
Since
\operatorname{tr}
is a positive linear functional
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (c)]
,
\operatorname{tr}
is monotonous.
By assumption
a_{i}
is increasing, so
\operatorname{tr}a_{i}
is increasing, too.
By (
i
),
\operatorname{tr}a_{i}
is upper-bounded.
Hence
\operatorname{tr}a_{i}
converges and thus is a Cauchy net.
Fix some
\varepsilon>0
.
For sufficiently large
i,j
we have
\lvert{\operatorname{tr}a_{j}-\operatorname{tr}a_{i}}\rvert\leq\varepsilon/2
because
\operatorname{tr}a_{i}
is a Cauchy net.
Then, for sufficiently large
i,j
, and with some
s\geq i,j
(
s
exists because
a_{i}
is a net), we have

\lVert{a_{i}-a_{j}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\lVert{a_{s}-a_{i}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\lVert{a_{s}-a_{j}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lvert{\operatorname{tr}a_{s}-\operatorname{tr}a_{i}}\rvert+\lvert{\operatorname{tr}a_{s}-\operatorname{tr}a_{j}}\rvert\leq\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon.

Here
(*)
is by
item iii
and using that
a_{s}-a_{i}
,
a_{s}-a_{j}
are positive because
a_{i}
is an increasing net and
s\geq i,j
.
Since
a_{i}
is increasing,
\lVert{a_{j}-a_{i}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\operatorname{tr}(a_{j}-a_{i})=\lvert{\operatorname{tr}a_{j}-\operatorname{tr}a_{j}}\rvert\leq\varepsilon
.
Thus
a_{i}
is a Cauchy net as well (with respect to the trace norm).
Since the trace-class operators are a Banach space (and thus complete),
a_{i}
then converges to some trace-class operator.
This shows that (
vi
) is implied.

We show that (
vi
) implies (
vii
).
For all trace-class
t
,
\lVert{t}\rVert\leq 2\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.
Hence convergence with respect to the trace norm implies convergence with respect to the norm.
Thus (
vi
) implies (
vii
), and the limit is the same in both cases.

We show that (
vii
) implies (
iv
).
The norm-topology is finer than the SOT.
(See
[
Tak79
, overview after Definition II.2.3]
. The norm-topology is called uniform there.)
Hence convergence with respect to the norm topology implies convergence with respect to the SOT.
Thus (
vii
) implies (
iv
),
and the limit is the same in both cases.

Thus we have the following implications:
(
i
)
(
ii
)
(
iii
)
(
iv
)
(
v
)
(
vi
)
(
vii
)

Hence all statements are equivalent.

And as mentioned in the beginning of the proof,
the supremum/limit in (
ii
), (
iii
),
(
iv
), (
v
) is the same.
And we showed that the limits in (
vi
) and (
vii
) are the same.
And that the limits in (
vii
) and (
iv
) are the same.
Thus the supremum and the limits are the same in cases (
ii
)–(
vii
).
∎

Lemma 31
.

For a quantum reference
F
and positive
a_{i}
,
F\bigl({\sum_{i}a_{i}}\bigr)=\sum_{i}F(a_{i})
where the left sum converges iff the right sum converges.
(Convergence of sums is with respect to the strong operator topology, SOT.)

Proof.

Let
s_{M}:=\sum_{i\in M}a_{i}
for finite sets
M
.
Since
a_{i}
are positive,
s_{M}
are positive.
Furthermore,
s_{M}
is an increasing net (with respect to the set-inclusion ordering of finite sets
M
).

F
is positive by
Lemma 22
.
Thus
F(s_{M})
are positive.
Since
F
is positive, it is monotonous.
Thus
F(s_{M})
is an increasing net.

By
Lemma 24
,
\lVert{s_{M}}\rVert=\lVert{F(s_{M})}\rVert
.
Thus
\lVert{s_{M}}\rVert
is bounded iff
\lVert{F(s_{M})}\rVert
is bounded.
By
Lemma 29
, this means that
s_{M}
converges in the SOT iff
F(s_{M})
converges in the SOT.

If
s_{M},F(s_{M})
converge in the SOT, then they converge in the weak*-topology to the same limits by
Lemma 29
.
Since
F
is weak*-continuous,
F(\lim_{M}s_{M})=\lim_{M}F(s_{M})
in the weak*-topology.
By
Lemma 29
, this also implies
F(\lim_{M}s_{M})=\lim_{M}F(s_{M})
in the SOT.

Then we have

F\Bigl({\sum_{i}a_{i}}\Bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}F\bigl({\lim_{M}s_{M}}\bigr)=\lim_{M}F(s_{M})=\lim_{M}\sum_{i\in M}F(a_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{i}F(a_{i}).

Here each equality means that the limits/sums on the lhs converge iff they ones on the rhs converge. All limits/sums in the SOT.
And
(*)
is the definition of infinite sums
[
Con13
, Definition 4.11]
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

If
a,b
are trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
, respectively, then
a\otimes b
is trace-class and
\operatorname{tr}a\otimes b=\operatorname{tr}a\cdot\operatorname{tr}b

and
\lVert{a\otimes b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\cdot\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
. (Here
\lVert{\cdot}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
is the trace norm.)

Proof.

By definition
[
Con00
, Definition 18.3]
,
a
is trace-class iff
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert e
converges for some orthonormal basis
E
. And
\sum_{f\in F}f^{\dagger}\lvert{b}\rvert f
converges for some orthonormal basis
F
. With
G:=\{e\otimes f:e\in E,f\in F\}
, we have that

\sum_{g\in G}g^{\dagger}\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert g=\sum_{e\in E,f\in F}(e^{\dagger}\otimes f^{\dagger})\lvert{a\otimes b}\rvert(e\otimes f)\ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny Lem.\ref{lemma:tensor.abs}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{e\in E,f\in F}(e^{\dagger}\otimes f^{\dagger})(\lvert{a}\rvert\otimes\lvert{b}\rvert)(e\otimes f)\\
=\sum_{e\in E,f\in F}e^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert e\cdot f^{\dagger}\lvert{b}\rvert f=\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert e\cdot\sum_{f\in F}\cdot f^{\dagger}\lvert{b}\rvert f

converges. Thus
a\otimes b
is trace-class.

Since
\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}\lvert{a}\rvert e=\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
by definition of the trace norm
[
Con00
, after Definition 18.3]
, and analogously for
\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
and
\lVert{a\otimes b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
, this equality also implies
\lVert{a\otimes b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\lVert{a}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\cdot\lVert{b}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.

By definition of the trace
[
Con00
, Definition 18.10]
,

\operatorname{tr}a=\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}ae
and

\operatorname{tr}b=\sum_{f\in F}f^{\dagger}bf

and

\operatorname{tr}(a\otimes b)=\sum_{g\in G}g^{\dagger}(a\otimes b)g
.
(The definition of the trace is independent of the chosen basis
E,F,G
.
And the sums converge.)

Thus

\operatorname{tr}(a\otimes b)=\sum_{g\in G}g^{\dagger}(a\otimes b)g=\sum_{e\in E,f\in F}(e^{\dagger}\otimes f^{\dagger})(a\otimes{b})(e\otimes f)\\
=\sum_{e\in E,f\in F}e^{\dagger}ae\cdot f^{\dagger}bf\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sum_{e\in E}e^{\dagger}ae\cdot\sum_{f\in F}f^{\dagger}bf=\operatorname{tr}a\cdot\operatorname{tr}b.

For
(*)
note that the sums are over the complex numbers.
∎

Lemma 33
.

If
S_{1},S_{2}
generate
T(\mathcal{H}_{1}),T(\mathcal{H}_{2})
, respectively (with respect to the trace norm), then
\{\rho\otimes\sigma:\rho\in S_{1},\sigma\in S_{2}\}
generates
T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
.

Proof.

In slight overloading of notation, for sets of trace-class operators
X,Y
, we write
X\otimes Y
for
\{x\otimes y:x\in X,y\in Y\}
. Furthermore, let
\overline{X}
denote the topological closure of
X
with respect to the trace norm.
With that notation, the conclusion of the lemma becomes
T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\subseteq\overline{\operatorname{span}(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})}
.

Consider first a rank-1 operator
t
on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
Then
t
can be written as
t=\psi\phi^{\dagger}
for
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}

[
Con13
, Exercise II.4.8]
.
We have that
\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}
for
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{1},\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
And analogously
\phi=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}
for
\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{1},\phi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
(Convergence of the sums is with respect to the usual topology of the Hilbert space.)
We have

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert{\sum\nolimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq n\\
j\leq m\end{subarray}}\psi_{i}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}{\phi_{j}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\psi\phi^{\dagger}}\Bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\lVert{\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}\bigr)\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi}\bigr)^{\dagger}+\psi({\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi})^{\dagger}+\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}\bigr)\phi^{\dagger}}\Bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\lVert{\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}\bigr)\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi}\bigr)^{\dagger}}\Bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\Bigl\lVert{\psi({\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi})^{\dagger}}\Bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\Bigl\lVert{\bigl({\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}\bigr)\phi^{\dagger}}\Bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\Bigl\lVert{{\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}}\Bigr\rVert}_{{}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0}\cdot\underbrace{\Bigl\lVert{{{\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi}}}\Bigr\rVert}_{{}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0}+\bigl\lVert{\psi}\bigr\rVert\cdot\underbrace{\Bigl\lVert{{{\sum\nolimits_{j\leq m}\phi_{j}\otimes\phi_{j}^{\prime}-\phi}}}\Bigr\rVert}_{{}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0}+\underbrace{\Bigl\lVert{{\sum\nolimits_{i\leq n}\psi_{i}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}-\psi}}\Bigr\rVert}_{{}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0}\cdot\lVert{\phi}\rVert

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{n,m\to\infty}0.

Then
\psi\phi^{\dagger}=\sum_{ij=1}^{\infty}\psi_{i}\phi_{j}^{\dagger}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}{\phi_{j}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}
(convergence with respect to the trace norm).
Since
\psi_{i}\phi_{i}^{\dagger}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}{\phi_{i}^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\in T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})
by definition, this implies that
\psi\phi^{\dagger}\in\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}\bigr)}
.
Thus all rank-1 operators on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
are in
\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}\bigr)}
.
Since finite rank operators are linear combinations of rank-1 operators, they are in
\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}\bigr)}
as well.
And since finite rank operators are dense in the space
{T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})}
of all trace-class operators
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (d)]
, we have

{T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})}\subseteq\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}\bigr)}.

(14)

Furthermore

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({T(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\overline{\operatorname{span}\bigl({\overline{\operatorname{span}S_{1}}\otimes\overline{\operatorname{span}S_{2}}}\bigr)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\subseteq}}\overline{\operatorname{span}\overline{\bigl({\operatorname{span}S_{1}\otimes\overline{\operatorname{span}S_{2}}}\bigr)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\subseteq}}\overline{\operatorname{span}\overline{\overline{\bigl({\operatorname{span}S_{1}\otimes\operatorname{span}S_{2}}\bigr)}}}=\overline{\operatorname{span}{{\bigl({\operatorname{span}S_{1}\otimes\operatorname{span}S_{2}}\bigr)}}}=\overline{\operatorname{span}{{\bigl({S_{1}\otimes S_{2}}\bigr)}}}.

Here
(*)
follows because by assumption
S_{i}
generates
T(\mathcal{H}_{i})
, i.e.,
T(\mathcal{H}_{i})=\overline{\operatorname{span}S_{i}}
.
And
(**)
follows because
\overline{X}\otimes Y\subseteq\overline{X\otimes Y}
(and
X\otimes\overline{Y}\subseteq\overline{X\otimes Y}
) which in turns follows from the trace norm continuity of
x\mapsto x\otimes y
which in turn follows because
x\mapsto x\otimes y
is bounded by
Lemma 32
.

With (
14
) this implies
T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\subseteq\overline{\operatorname{span}(S_{1}\otimes S_{2})}
and thus proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 34
.

If
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
is an isometry,
\mathcal{E}:T(\mathcal{H}_{A})\to T(\mathcal{H}_{B})
,
\rho\mapsto U\rho U^{\dagger}
is a quantum channel (i.e., a completely positive trace-preserving linear map).

Proof.

\mathcal{E}
is obviously linear.
Since
U
is an isometry,
\mathcal{E}(\rho\sigma)=U\rho\sigma U^{\dagger}=U\rho U^{\dagger}U\sigma U^{\dagger}=\mathcal{E}(\rho)\mathcal{E}(\sigma)
, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}
is multiplicative.
Furthermore
\mathcal{E}(\rho^{\dagger})=U\rho^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}=\bigl({U\rho U^{\dagger}}\bigr)^{\dagger}=\mathcal{E}(\rho)^{\dagger}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}
preserves adjoints.
That is,
\mathcal{E}
is a *-homomorphism.
By
[
Con00
, Example 34.3 (a)]
this implies that
\mathcal{E}
is completely positive.

Furthermore,

\operatorname{tr}\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\operatorname{tr}U\rho U^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\rho U^{\dagger}U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\rho I=\operatorname{tr}\rho.

Here
(*)
follows by the circularity of the trace
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (e)]
(
item ii
).
And
(**)
follows since
U
is an isometry.
Thus
\mathcal{E}
is trace-preserving.
∎

Lemma 35

(Schrödinger-Heisenberg dual)
.

Consider the following relation between functions
f:T(\mathcal{H})\to T(\mathcal{K})
and
g:B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H})
:

\operatorname{tr}f(t)a=\operatorname{tr}g(a)t\qquad\text{for all $t\in T(\mathcal{H})$, $a\in B(\mathcal{K})$}\qquad\qquad(*)

(i)

The relation
(*)
is biunique.
I.e., for every
f
there is at most one
g
, and for every
g
there is at most one
f
satisfying
(*)
.

(ii)

Bounded linear maps
f:T(\mathcal{H})\to T(\mathcal{K})
stand in
1-1 correspondence to
weak*-continuous bounded linear maps
g:B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H})
via the relation
(*)
.
(I.e., for every such
f
there exists exactly one such
g
and vice versa.)

(iii)

And
f
is positive iff
g
is positive.
(Here and in the following statements, assume
f,g
stand in relation
(*)
.)

(iv)

And
f
is completely positive iff
g
is completely positive.

(v)

And
f
is trace-preserving iff
g
is unital.

(vi)

And
f
is trace-reducing (
\operatorname{tr}f(t)\leq\operatorname{tr}t
for positive
t
) iff
g
is subunital.

Proof.

​​
51
51

51

Thanks to Robert Furber for helpful pointers on how to prove this
[
Fur21a
]
.

We first show (
i
).

An operator
b\in B(\mathcal{H})
is uniquely determined by the function
\phi,\psi\mapsto\phi^{*}b\psi
. We have

\phi^{*}b\psi=\operatorname{tr}\phi^{*}b\psi=\operatorname{tr}b\psi\phi^{*}
where the last equality is the circularity of the trace (
item ii
).
Since
\psi\phi^{*}
is trace-class, this means that
b
is uniquely determined by
\operatorname{tr}bt
for all
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
.
Thus for fixed
f
,
g(a)
is uniquely determined for all
a
by
(*)
.
Similarly, for fixed
g
,
f(t)
is uniquely determined for all
t
by
(*)
.

This shows (
i
).

We show (
ii
).

We need some observations first:

We have a duality
\langle B(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H})\rangle

with canonical bilinear form
(a,t)\mapsto\operatorname{tr}at
with
a\in B(\mathcal{H})
,
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
.
By definition of a duality
[
Sch71
, beginning of section IV.1]
, this means that
(a,t)\mapsto\operatorname{tr}at
is bilinear and satisfies
(\forall a.\operatorname{tr}at=0)\Longrightarrow t=0
and

(\forall t.\operatorname{tr}at=0)\Longrightarrow a=0
.
Bilinearity follows from the linearity of the trace, and the other two properties were shown in
the proof of (
i
).
Analogously we have a duality
\langle B(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K})\rangle
.

For a duality
\langle X,Y\rangle
, let
\sigma(X,Y)
denote the coarsest topology on
X
such that
x\mapsto\langle x,y\rangle
is continuous for all
y\in Y

[
Sch71
, before IV.1.2]
.
Here
\langle x,y\rangle
is the canonical bilinear form of
\langle X,Y\rangle
.

By definition of the weak*-topology
[
Con00
, beginning of Section 20]
,
\sigma(B(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K}))
and
\sigma(B(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H}))
are the weak*-topology on
B(\mathcal{K})
,
B(\mathcal{H})
, respectively.

Now we proceed to the actual proof of (
ii
).

First, assume that
f:T(\mathcal{H})\to T(\mathcal{K})
is a bounded linear map. We want to show that there exists a weak*-continuous bounded linear map
g:B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H})
that stands in relation
(*)
with
f
.
(Uniqueness of
g
already follows from (
i
).)

By
[
Sch71
, discussion before IV.2.4]
, any continuous (i.e., bounded)
f:T(\mathcal{H})\to T(\mathcal{K})
is continuous for
\sigma(T(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H})^{\prime})
and
\sigma(T(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K})^{\prime})
where
X^{\prime}
denotes the dual of
X
. (I.e., the space of continuous linear functionals on
X
.)
Furthermore,
B(\mathcal{H})
is isometrically isomorphic to the dual of
T(\mathcal{H})
if we identify
b\in B(\mathcal{H})
with
t\in T\mapsto\operatorname{tr}bt

[
Con00
, Theorem 19.2]
.
Thus
\sigma(T(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H})^{\prime})
is the same as
\sigma(T(\mathcal{H}),B(\mathcal{H}))
.
Analogously,
\sigma(T(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K})^{\prime})
is the same as
\sigma(T(\mathcal{K}),B(\mathcal{K}))
.
Thus
f
is continuous for
\sigma(T(\mathcal{H}),B(\mathcal{H}))
and
\sigma(T(\mathcal{K}),B(\mathcal{K}))
.

By
[
Sch71
, IV.2.1]
, this implies that there exists a
g:B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H})
(the
adjoint
) that is continuous for
\sigma(B(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K}))
and
\sigma(B(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H}))
.
This
g
is furthermore linear and satisfies
\operatorname{tr}af(t)=\operatorname{tr}g(a)t
for all
a,t
because it is the restriction of the
algebraic adjoint
to a smaller domain, and the algebraic adjoint has those properties
[
Sch71
, beginning of section IV.2]
.
So
g
is weak*-continuous and linear.

We next show that
g
is bounded.
Fix
a\in B(\mathcal{K})
. Since
f
is bounded, there exists a
c
such that

\lVert{f(t)}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq c\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
for all
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
.
We have

\displaystyle\lVert{g(a)}\rVert

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi,\phi}\bigl\lvert{\phi^{\dagger}g(a)\psi}\bigr\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\sup_{\psi,\phi}\bigl\lvert{\operatorname{tr}g(a)\psi\phi^{\dagger}}\bigr\rvert=\sup_{\psi,\phi}\bigl\lvert{\operatorname{tr}af(\psi\phi^{\dagger})}\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\sup_{\psi,\phi}\lVert{a}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lVert{f(\psi\phi^{\dagger})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\sup_{\psi,\phi}\lVert{a}\rVert\cdot c\underbrace{\lVert{\psi\phi^{\dagger}}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}}_{=1}=c\lVert{a}\rVert.

Here the suprema range over unit vectors
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{H}
.
And
(*)
uses the circularity of the trace,
item ii
.
And
(**)
follows by
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (e)]
.
Thus
g
is bounded.

Hence there exists a weak*-continuous bounded linear map
g
that stands in relation
(*)
with
f
.

Next, assume that
g:B(\mathcal{K})\to B(\mathcal{H})
is a weak*-continuous bounded linear map. We want to show that there exists a bounded linear map
f:T(\mathcal{H})\to T(\mathcal{K})
that stands in relation
(*)
with
g
.
Since
g
is weak*-continuous,
g
is continuous for
\sigma(B(\mathcal{K}),T(\mathcal{K}))
and
\sigma(B(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{H}))
.
By
[
Sch71
, IV.2.1]
, this implies that there exists an
f
that is continuous for
\sigma(T(\mathcal{H}),B(\mathcal{H}))
and
\sigma(T(\mathcal{K}),B(\mathcal{K}))
satisfying
\operatorname{tr}af(t)=\operatorname{tr}g(a)t
for all
a,t
.
This
f
is furthermore linear and satisfies
\operatorname{tr}af(t)=\operatorname{tr}g(t)
for all
a,t
because it is the restriction of the
algebraic adjoint
to a smaller domain, and the algebraic adjoint has those properties
[
Sch71
, beginning of section IV.2]
.

We show that
f
is
bounded. Fix
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
. By the polar decomposition
[
Con00
, Theorem 3.9]
,
there is a partial isometry
u\in B(\mathcal{K})
such that
f(t)=u\lvert{f(t)}\rvert
.
Furthermore,
u^{\dagger}u
is the projection on the closure of the range of
\lvert{f(t)}\rvert
.
(Shown in the proof of
[
Con00
, Theorem 3.9]
, noting that
U=W=u
and
P=\lvert{A}\rvert=\lvert{f(t)}\rvert
there.)
Thus
u^{\dagger}f(t)=u^{\dagger}u\lvert{f(t)}\rvert=\lvert{f(t)}\rvert
.
Then

\displaystyle\lVert{f(t)}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\lVert{u\lvert{f(t)}\rvert}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lVert{\lvert{f(t)}\rvert}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lvert{\operatorname{tr}{\lvert{f(t)}\rvert}}\bigr\rvert=\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lvert{\operatorname{tr}{u^{\dagger}f(t)}}\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle=\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lvert{\operatorname{tr}{g(u^{\dagger})t}}\bigr\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$\scriptscriptstyle(\!*\!*\!*\!)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\bigl\lVert{g(u^{\dagger})}\bigr\rVert\cdot\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\lVert{u}\rVert\cdot\lVert{g}\rVert\lVert{u^{\dagger}}\rVert\cdot\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$\scriptscriptstyle(\!*\!*\!*\!*\!)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\lVert{g}\rVert\cdot\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}.

Here
(*)
follows by
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (g)]
.
And
(**)
by
item iii
.
And
(*{*}*)
by
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (e)]
.
And the operator norm
\lVert{g}\rVert
exists because
g
is bounded by assumption.
And
(*{*}{*}*)
uses that
\lVert{u}\rVert,\lVert{u^{\dagger}}\rVert\leq 1
since
u
is a partial isometry.
Thus
f
is bounded.

Hence there exists a bounded linear map
f
that stands in relation
(*)
with
g
.

This shows (
ii
)

We now show (
iii
).

We need the auxiliary fact that
\operatorname{tr}at\geq 0
for positive
a\in B(\mathcal{H})
and positive
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
.
Since
a
is positive, by definition of positivity,
a=b^{\dagger}b
for some
b\in B(\mathcal{H})

[
Con00
, Theorem 3.4 (c)]
.
Then
\operatorname{tr}at=\operatorname{tr}btb^{\dagger}\geq 0
where the
=
follows from the circularity of the trace (
item ii
),
and
\geq
follows since
btb^{\dagger}
is positive and thus has positive trace
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (c)]
.

Assume that
f
is positive. We show that
g
is positive. Fix a positive
a\in B(\mathcal{K})
.
For every
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,
\psi\psi^{\dagger}
is positive and trace-class. Thus
f(\psi\psi^{\dagger})
is positive.
Then
\operatorname{tr}af(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\geq 0
. Then
\psi^{\dagger}g(a)\psi=\operatorname{tr}g(a)\psi\psi^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}af(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\geq 0
.
Here the first equality uses the circularity of the trace (
item ii
) and
\geq
uses the auxiliary fact.
Since
\psi^{\dagger}g(a)\psi\geq 0
for all
\psi
,
g(a)
is positive
[
Con00
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Since this holds for every positive
a
,
g
is positive.

Assume that
g
is positive. We show that
f
is positive. Fix a positive
t\in T(\mathcal{H})
.
For every
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,
\psi\psi^{\dagger}
is positive and bounded. Thus
g(\psi\psi^{\dagger})
is positive.
Then
\operatorname{tr}g(\psi\psi^{\dagger})t\geq 0
. Then
\psi^{\dagger}f(t)\psi=\operatorname{tr}\psi\psi^{\dagger}f(t)=\operatorname{tr}g(\psi\psi^{\dagger})t\geq 0
.
Here the first equality uses the circularity of the trace (
item ii
).
Since
\psi^{\dagger}f(t)\psi\geq 0
for all
\psi
,
f(t)
is positive. Since this holds for every positive
t
,
f
is positive.

Thus
f
is positive iff
g
is.
This shows (
iii
).

We show (
iv
). Fix
f,g
standing in relation
(*)
.

Let
M_{n}(S)
denote the set of
n\times n
matrices with elements in

S
where
S=T(\mathcal{H}),T(\mathcal{K}),B(\mathcal{H}),B(\mathcal{K})
, together with the usual matrix
addition and multiplication.
For a linear map
f:S\to S^{\prime}
, let
f^{(n)}:M_{n}(S)\to M_{n}(S^{\prime})

be the component-wise application of
f
.

By definition
[
Con00
, Definition 34.2]
,
f
is completely
positive iff
f^{(n)}
is positive for all
n\geq 1
.

Note that
M_{n}(B(\mathcal{H}))
are the bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}:=\mathcal{H}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{H}
, and analogously for
M_{n}(B(\mathcal{K}))
.
And
M_{n}(T(\mathcal{H})),M_{n}(T(\mathcal{K}))
are the trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}^{(n)},\mathcal{K}^{(n)}
.

Since
f,g
stand in correspondence
(*)
,
f^{(n)},g^{(n)}
stand in correspondence
(*)
as well.
(Note that
\operatorname{tr}t=\sum_{i}\operatorname{tr}t_{ii}
for
t\in M_{n}(T(\mathcal{H}))
.)
Then
f
is completely positive iff
f^{(n)}
is positive for all
n\geq 1

iff
g^{(n)}
is positive for all
n\geq 1
(by (
iii
) applied to
f^{(n)},g^{(n)}
) iff
g
is completely positive.

This shows (
iv
).

We show (
v
).
f
is trace-preserving iff
\operatorname{tr}f(t)=\operatorname{tr}t
for all
t

iff
\operatorname{tr}f(t)1=\operatorname{tr}t
for all
t
iff
\operatorname{tr}tg(1)=\operatorname{tr}t
for all
t
(by relation
(*)
) iff
g(1)=1
(i.e.,
g
is unital). The
\Rightarrow
direction in the last “iff” follows since

\operatorname{tr}ta
determines
a
(see the proof of (
i
)).
This shows (
v
).

We show (
vi
).

Assume that
f
is trace-reducing.
Then for all
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,

\psi^{\dagger}(1-g(1))\psi=\operatorname{tr}\psi^{\dagger}\psi-\operatorname{tr}g(1)\psi\psi^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}\psi^{\dagger}\psi-\operatorname{tr}1f(\psi\psi^{\dagger})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\geq}}0.

Here
(*)
holds because
f
is trace-reducing and
\psi\psi^{\dagger}
positive.
Since this holds for all
\psi
, we have that
{1-g(1)}
is positive
[
Con00
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Thus
g(1)\leq 1
. Hence
g
is subunital.

Now assume that
g
is subunital.
Then for positive
\rho\in T(\mathcal{H})
,

1-g(1)
is positive and thus
\sqrt{\rho}\bigl({1-g(1)}\bigr)\rho
is positive. Then

\operatorname{tr}\rho-\operatorname{tr}f(\rho)=\operatorname{tr}\rho-\operatorname{tr}f(\rho)1=\operatorname{tr}\rho-\operatorname{tr}\rho g(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\sqrt{\rho}\sqrt{\rho}-\operatorname{tr}\sqrt{\rho}g(1)\sqrt{\rho}=\operatorname{tr}\sqrt{\rho}\bigl({1-g(1)}\bigr)\sqrt{\rho}\geq 0.

Here
(*)
uses the circularity of the trace (
item ii
).
Hence
\operatorname{tr}f(\rho)\leq\operatorname{tr}\rho
.
So
f
is trace-reducing.

This shows (
vi
).
∎

Lemma 36
.

Any quantum subchannel
\mathcal{E}
is a bounded linear map (with respect to the trace norm).
In particular, continuous with respect to the trace norm.

Proof.

Fix a Hermitian trace-class
t
. By
[
Con00
, Proposition 3.2]
,
t=t_{+}-t_{-}
where
t_{+}
and
t_{-}
are the “positive and negative part” of
t
, and
t_{+},t_{-}
are positive.
Furthermore,
\lvert{t}\rvert=t_{+}+t_{-}
by
[
Con00
, Exercise 3.4]
.
Since
\lvert{t}\rvert
is trace-class (
item i
), and
t_{+},t_{i}\leq\lvert{t}\rvert
, we have that
t_{+},t_{-}
are trace-class by
item iv
.
Then

\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t)}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}=\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{+})-\mathcal{E}(t_{-})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{+})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{-})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}{\mathcal{E}(t_{+})}+\operatorname{tr}{\mathcal{E}(t_{-})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\operatorname{tr}t_{+}+\operatorname{tr}t_{-}=\operatorname{tr}t.

Here
(*)
follows with
item iii
since
\mathcal{E}
is positive and thus
\mathcal{E}(t_{+}),\mathcal{E}(t_{-})
are positive.
And
(**)
follows since
\mathcal{E}
is trace-reducing.
Thus for Hermitian
t
,
\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t)}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
.

Fix an arbitrary (not necessarily Hermitian) trace-class
t
. Then
t=t_{h}+it_{a}
where
t_{h}:=(t+t^{\dagger})/2
and
t_{a}:=(t-t^{\dagger})/2i
.
Both
t_{h},t_{a}
are easily verified to be Hermitian.
Thus

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t)}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{h})+i\mathcal{E}(t_{a})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{h})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\bigl\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t_{a})}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\leq}}\bigl\lVert{t_{h}}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\bigl\lVert{t_{a}}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\lVert{t/2+t^{\dagger}/2}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\bigl\lVert{t/2i-t^{\dagger}/2i}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl\lVert{t}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl\lVert{t^{\dagger}}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl\lVert{t}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl\lVert{t^{\dagger}}\bigr\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}2\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}.

Here
(*)
uses that we showed
\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t)}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
for Hermitian
t
above.
And
(**)
is by
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (f)]
.

Thus
\lVert{\mathcal{E}(t)}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}\leq\lVert{t}\rVert_{\operatorname{tr}}
for all trace-class
t
, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}
is bounded with respect to the trace-class.
A bounded linear map is continuous.
∎

Lemma 37
.

If
g,h:T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\to T(\mathcal{K})
are quantum subchannels with
f(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})=g(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})
for all trace-class
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, then
f=g
.

If
g,h:T\bigl({(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3}}\bigr)\to T(\mathcal{K})
are quantum subchannels with
f\bigl({(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\otimes\rho_{3}}\bigr)=g\bigl({(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\otimes\rho_{3}}\bigr)
for all trace-class
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
, then
f=g
.

If
g,h:T\bigl({\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3})}\bigr)\to T(\mathcal{K})
are quantum subchannels with
f\bigl({\rho_{1}\otimes(\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3})}\bigr)=g\bigl({\rho_{1}\otimes(\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3})}\bigr)
for all trace-class
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
, then
f=g
.

Proof.

By
Lemma 33
, the
\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}
(the
(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\otimes\rho_{3}
, the
\rho_{1}\otimes(\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3})
) generate the whole space
T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})
(
T\bigl({(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3}}\bigr)
,
T\bigl({\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3})}\bigr)
).
Furthermore,
g,h
are continuous linear maps (w.r.t. the trace norm) by
Lemma 36
.
Thus
g=h
.
∎

Lemma 38
.

Fix completely positive
f_{i},g_{i}:T(\mathcal{H}_{i})\to T(\mathcal{K}_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

(i)

There exists a
completely positive
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}:T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\to T(\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2})
such that

(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})=f_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes f_{2}(\rho_{2})
.

(ii)

If
f_{1},f_{2}
are trace-reducing, then
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
as defined in (
i
) is unique.

(iii)

If
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}
are trace-reducing, then
(g_{1}\otimes g_{2})\circ(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})=(g_{1}\circ f_{1})\otimes(g_{2}\circ f_{2})
.

(iv)

If
f_{1},f_{2}
are trace-preserving, then
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
is.
(I.e., the tensor product of quantum channels exists and is a quantum channel.)

(v)

If
f_{1},f_{2}
are trace-reducing (i.e., for positive
\rho
,
\operatorname{tr}f_{i}(\rho)\leq\operatorname{tr}\rho
), then
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
is.
(I.e., the tensor product of quantum subchannels exists and is a quantum subchannel.)

(vi)

\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\otimes\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}=\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})}
.

Proof.

​​
52
52

52

Thanks to Robert Furber for helpful pointers on how to prove this
[
Fur21
]
.

We first show (
i
).

By
item ii
, there are weak*-continuous completely positive
g_{1},g_{2}:B(\mathcal{K}_{i})\to B(\mathcal{H}_{i})
such that
\operatorname{tr}af_{i}(t)=\operatorname{tr}g_{i}(a)t
for all
a\in B(\mathcal{K}_{i})
,
t\in T(\mathcal{H}_{i})
.
By
[
Tak79
, Proposition IV.5.13]
, there is a weak*-continuous completely positive
\theta:B(\mathcal{K}_{1})\otimes B(\mathcal{K}_{2})\to B(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes B(\mathcal{H}_{2})

such that
\theta(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})=g_{1}(a_{1})\otimes g_{2}(a_{2})
for all
a_{1},a_{2}
.
Note that
B(\mathcal{K}_{1})\otimes B(\mathcal{K}_{2})=B(\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2})
and

B(\mathcal{H}_{1})\otimes B(\mathcal{H}_{2})=B(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})

[
Tak79
, (10) after Proposition IV.1.6]
.
(This is an equality of spaces, not just an isomorphism.)
Positive maps are bounded
[
Con00
, Proposition 33.4]
.
Thus
\theta
is weak*-continuous bounded completely positive.
Hence by
item ii
, there exists a completely positive
\theta^{\prime}:T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})\to T(\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2})
such that
\operatorname{tr}\theta^{\prime}(t)a=\operatorname{tr}t\theta(a)
for all
t,a
.

Fix
\rho_{1}\in T(\mathcal{H}_{1})
,
\rho_{2}\in T(\mathcal{H}_{2})
.

\psi_{1},\phi_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
,
\psi_{2},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}
.
Define
a_{1}:=\psi_{1}\phi_{1}^{\dagger}\in B(\mathcal{K}_{1})
and
a_{2}:=\psi_{2}\phi_{2}^{\dagger}\in B(\mathcal{K}_{1})
.
Then

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\dagger}\theta^{\prime}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\theta^{\prime}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}\theta^{\prime}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})=\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})\theta(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}g_{1}(a_{1})\otimes\rho_{2}g_{2}(a_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}g_{1}(a_{1})\cdot\operatorname{tr}\rho_{2}g_{2}(a_{2})=\operatorname{tr}f_{1}(\rho_{1})a_{1}\cdot\operatorname{tr}f_{2}(\rho_{2})a_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}f_{1}(\rho_{1})a_{1}\otimes f_{2}(\rho_{2})a_{2}=\operatorname{tr}\bigl({f_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes f_{2}(\rho_{2})}\bigr)(a_{1}\otimes a_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\dagger}(f_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes f_{2}(\rho_{2}))(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}).

Here
(*)
uses the circularity of the trace and
(**)
uses
Lemma 32
.

Since the vectors
\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
span
\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2}
, and so do the vectors
\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{1}
,
we have that

\forall\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}.\ (\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\dagger}u(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})=(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})^{\dagger}u^{\prime}(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})

implies
u=u^{\prime}
. Thus
\theta^{\prime}(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})=f_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes f_{2}(\rho_{2})
.

Thus with
(f_{1}\otimes f_{2}):=\theta^{\prime}
, this shows
(
i
).

Next we show (
iii
).
Since
g_{1}\otimes g_{2}
and
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
are completely positive, so is
(g_{1}\otimes g_{2})\circ(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})
.
For trace-class
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, we have
(g_{1}\otimes g_{2})\circ(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})=g_{1}\circ f_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes g_{2}\circ f_{2}(\rho_{2})
.
By (
ii
),
{(g_{1}\circ f_{1})\otimes(g_{2}\circ f_{2})}
is the only completely positive map with that property.
Thus
(g_{1}\circ f_{1})\otimes(g_{2}\circ f_{2})=(g_{1}\circ f_{1})\otimes(g_{2}\circ f_{2})
.
This shows (
iii
).

Next we show (
iv
).
Assume that
f_{1},f_{2}
are trace-preserving. By
item v
,
g_{1},g_{2}
as defined above in the proof of (
i
) are unital.
Then
\theta
as defined above is unital:
\theta(1)=\theta(1\otimes 1)=g_{1}(1)\otimes g_{2}(1)=1\otimes 1=1
.
Thus by
item v
,
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}=\theta^{\prime}
is trace-preserving.
This shows (
iv
).

Next we show (
v
).
Assume that
f_{1},f_{2}
are trace-reducing.
Then
g_{1},g_{2}
as defined above in the proof of (
i
) are subunital by
item vi
.
Then
\theta
as defined above is subunital:

1-\theta(1)=1-\theta(1\otimes 1)=1\otimes 1-g_{1}(1)\otimes g_{2}(1)\\
=\Bigl({(1-g_{1}(1))\otimes(1-g_{1}(1))}\Bigr)+\Bigl({(1-g_{1}(1))\otimes g_{2}(1)}\Bigr)+\Bigl({g_{1}(1)\otimes(1-g_{1}(1))}\Bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\geq}}0

Here
(*)
follows by
Lemma 18
and the fact that
g_{1},g_{2}
are subunital and positive, so
1-g_{1}(1)
,
1-g_{2}(1)
,
g_{1}(1)
,
g_{2}(1)
are all positive.
Then
(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})=\theta^{\prime}
is trace-reducing by
item vi
.
This shows (
v
).

We show (
vi
).
By (
ii
) and
iv
,
\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\otimes\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}
is the unique completely positive trace-preserving map that maps
\rho\otimes\sigma
to
\rho\otimes\sigma
.
Since
\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})}
is completely positive trace-preserving and maps
\rho\otimes\sigma
to
\rho\otimes\sigma
, we have
\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1})}\otimes\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{2})}=\mathit{id}_{T(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})}
.
∎

Lemma 39
.

Assume that
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a quantum reference, and
x_{i}
is a net of bounded operators, and
x
is a bounded operator.
Then
x_{i}\to x
iff
F(x_{i})\to F(x)
(both limits are in the weak*-topology).

Proof.

The direction “if
x_{i}\to x
then
F(x_{i})\to F(x)
” follows since
F
is weak*-continuous.

To show the direction “if
F(x_{i})\to F(x)
then
x_{i}\to x
”, we need to show
x_{i}\to x
in the weak*-topology.
By
Lemma 23
,
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})U^{\dagger}
for some
\mathbf{C}
and some unitary
U
.
Let
\delta\in T(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})
be an arbitrary operator of trace 1 (e.g.,
\delta:=\psi\psi^{\dagger}
with
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1
).
For any
t
, let
u_{t}:=U(t\otimes\delta)U^{\dagger}
.
Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}tx

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}tx\cdot\operatorname{tr}\delta 1_{\mathbf{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}tx\otimes\delta 1_{\mathbf{C}}=\operatorname{tr}(t\otimes\delta)(x\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}U^{\dagger}u_{t}U(x\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}u_{t}U(x\otimes 1_{\mathbf{C}})U^{\dagger}=\operatorname{tr}u_{t}F(x).

Here
(*)
is by
Lemma 32
.
And
(**)
is by the circularity of the trace (
item ii
).

Analogously,
\operatorname{tr}tx_{i}=\operatorname{tr}u_{t}F(x_{i})
. And
u_{t}
is trace-class
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (a)]
.
Since
F(x_{i})\to F(x)
by assumption, for every
t
,
\operatorname{tr}u_{t}F(x_{i})\to\operatorname{tr}u_{t}F(x)
by definition of the weak*-topology
[
Con00
, beginning of Section 20]
.
Thus
\operatorname{tr}tx_{i}\to\operatorname{tr}tx
.
Since this holds for all
t
,
x_{i}\to x
.
∎

Lemma 40
.

Let
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
,
V:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
,

\hat{U}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
,
\hat{V}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}
be unitaries.
Assume that the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{C}
is at least as large as that of
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}
.
(Otherwise apply the lemma the other way around.)

Let
F(a):=U(a\otimes 1)V
and
\hat{F}(a):=\hat{U}(a\otimes 1)\hat{V}
. Assume
F=\hat{F}
.
Then there is a unitary
M
such that
V=(1\otimes M)\hat{V}
and
\hat{U}=U(1\otimes M)
.
(That is,
(V,U)\cong(\hat{V},\hat{U})
for the relation
\cong
from
\autopageref
page:def:opticequiv.)

Proof.

Since
F=\hat{F}
, we have for all bounded operators
a\in\mathbf{A}
that
U(a\otimes 1)V=\hat{U}(a\otimes 1)\hat{V}
.
With
a=1
,
UV=\hat{U}\hat{V}
.
Since
\hat{V},U
are unitaries, this implies
V\hat{V}^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}\hat{U}=:W
. Then
W:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
is unitary as well.
Let
Z
be an arbitrary isometry
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}\to\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
(It exists because the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{C}
is at least as large as that of
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{C}}
.)
So

W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})(a\otimes 1)=W(a\otimes 1)(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})=U^{\dagger}\hat{U}(a\otimes 1)\hat{V}\hat{V}^{\dagger}(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})\\
=U^{\dagger}U(a\otimes 1)V\hat{V}^{\dagger}(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})=(a\otimes 1)W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger}).

(15)

So
W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})
commutes with all
a\otimes 1
.
By
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
,
a\mapsto a\otimes 1
is an isomorphism and thus surjective onto
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
.
(Here
\mathbb{C}1
is the set of the multiples of the identity.)
Thus
W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})
commutes with all of
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
, i.e.,
W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})\in(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1)^{\prime}
, the commutant of
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
.
By
[
Tak79
, Proposition IV.1.6 (i)]
,
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1)^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{C}
.
So
W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})\in\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{C}
.
By
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
,
c\mapsto 1\otimes c
is an isomorphism and thus surjective onto
\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{C}
.
Thus all elements of
\mathbb{C}1\otimes\mathbf{C}
are of the form
1\otimes c
.
Hence we can find some
N\in\mathbf{C}
such that
W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})=1\otimes N
.
With
M:=NZ
, we have
W=W(1\otimes Z^{\dagger})(1\otimes Z)=(1\otimes N)(1\otimes Z)=1\otimes M
.

We have
1\otimes M^{\dagger}M=W^{\dagger}W=1=1\otimes 1
.
Since
c\mapsto 1\otimes c
is an isomorphism and thus injective, this implies
M^{\dagger}M=1
.
Analogously
MM^{\dagger}=1
.
So
M
is unitary.

Finally, we have

V=V\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{V}=W\hat{V}=(1\otimes M)\hat{V}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{U}=UU^{\dagger}\hat{U}=UW=U(1\otimes M)

(16)

as desired.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of axioms for infinite-dimensional quantum references

We show that the axioms from

Figure 1
hold for the quantum reference category
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
as defined in
Section 4.2
.

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Immediate from the definition of the objects in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
(i.e., the spaces of bounded operators).

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Immediate from the definition of pre-references (i.e., weak*-continuous bounded linear operators).

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Immediate by
Lemma 17
and the definition of pre-references.

Axioms
–
‣
•
‣
1
.

The fact that a bounded operator
a\otimes b
exists is shown in
[
Tak79
, discussion preceding Definition IV.1.3]
.

Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
.

Fix pre-references
F,G
such that
F(a\otimes b)=G(a\otimes b)
for all
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
b\in\mathbf{B}
.
The algebraic tensor product
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
(see
Lemma 19
) is spanned by the operators
a\otimes b
for
a\in\mathbf{A}
,
b\in\mathbf{B}
.
Since
F,G
are linear, this implies that
F=G
on
\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
. By
Lemma 19
,

\mathbf{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{alg}}\mathbf{B}
is weak*-dense in
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
.
Since
F,G
are weak*-continuous, and
F=G
on a weak*-dense subset, we have
F=G
.

Axiom
–
‣
•
‣
1
.

Shown in
[
Tak79
, (5) after Definition IV.1.2]
.

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

References are weak*-continuous bounded linear maps by
Lemma 22
, and thus pre-references.

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Immediate from the definition of references (weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphisms).

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

A direct consequence of the fact that that references are unital and *-homomorphisms.

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Let
F(x):=x\otimes 1_{\mathbf{B}}
for
x\in\mathbf{A}
.
By
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
,
F
is an isomorphism
from
\mathbf{A}
onto the von Neumann algebra
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
where
\textstyle\mathbb{C}1
is the set of all multiples of the identity.
F
also preserves adjoints and is multiplicative, so
F
is a
*
-isomorphism.
By
[
Con00
, Proposition 46.6]
, any
*
-isomorphism is normal, i.e., weak*-continuous
[
Con00
, Theorem 46.4 (c)]
. Thus
F
is weak*-continuous as a map

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
, and thus also as a map
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\supseteq\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1
.
F(1)=1\otimes 1=1
, hence
F
is also unital. Thus
F
is a reference.

That
x\mapsto 1\otimes x
is a reference is shown analogously.

Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Fix references
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
,
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
with commuting ranges.
By
Lemma 23
,
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}})U^{\dagger}
and
G(b)=V(b\otimes 1_{\mathbf{E}})V^{\dagger}
for some Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{D},\mathcal{H}_{E}
and unitaries
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{D}\to\mathcal{H}_{C}
,
V:\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}\to\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
By
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
, the range of
a\mapsto a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}}
is
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}}
, and the range of
b\mapsto b\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{E}}
is
\mathbf{B}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{E}}
.
(
\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{E}}
is the set of multiples of the identity in
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{E}
.)
Thus the ranges of
F,G
are
F(\mathbf{A})=U(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}})U^{\dagger}
and
G(\mathbf{B})=V(\mathbf{B}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{E}})V^{\dagger}
.

F(\mathbf{A})
,
G(\mathbf{B})
commute by assumption.
Hence
U(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}})U^{\dagger}
and
G(\mathbf{B})
commute.
Thus
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}}
and
U^{\dagger}G(\mathbf{B})U
commute.
Thus
U^{\dagger}G(\mathbf{B})U\subseteq({\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1})^{\prime}
where
\textstyle X^{\prime}
denotes the commutant of
X
.
By
[
Tak79
, Theorem IV.5.9]
,
({\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}}})^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes(\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{D}})^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}
.
So the range of
\tilde{G}(b):=U^{\dagger}G(b)U
is
\tilde{G}(\mathbf{B})=U^{\dagger}G(\mathbf{B})U\subseteq\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}
, and
\tilde{G}
is a weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphism (because
G
and
x\mapsto U^{\dagger}xU
are, using
Lemma 17
).
Then let
\hat{G}:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}
be result of restricting the codomain
\tilde{G}
to
\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}
.
Then
\hat{G}
is still a weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphism.
Let
\iota(d):=1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes d
for
d\in\mathbf{D}
.
By
[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
,
\iota:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}
is an isomorphism of von Neumann algebras.
It also preserves adjoints, so it is a *-isomorphisms.
Thus
\iota^{-1}:\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}
is a
*
-isomorphism, too. And thus by
[
Con00
, Proposition 46.6]
,
\iota^{-1}
is normal and thus a weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphism.
Then
G_{1}:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}:=\iota^{-1}\circ\hat{G}
is a weak*-continuous unital *-homomorphism, hence a reference.
Since
\mathit{id}_{\mathbf{A}},G_{1}
are references, by
Lemma 27
, there is a reference
T:\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{D}
such that
T(a\otimes b)=a\otimes G_{1}(b)
for all
a\in\mathbf{A},b\in\mathbf{B}
.
Let
FG(x):=UT(x)U^{\dagger}
. Then
FG
is a reference because it is the composition of the references
T
and
x\mapsto UxU^{\dagger}
.

We have

FG(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{B}})=U\bigl({a\otimes G_{1}(1_{\mathbf{B}})}\bigr)U^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}U(a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{D}})U^{\dagger}=F(a)

where
(*)
uses that
G_{1}
is a reference and thus unital.
Moreover

FG(1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes b)=U\bigl({1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes G_{1}(b)}\bigr)U^{\dagger}=U\bigl({\iota(G_{1}(b))}\bigr)U^{\dagger}=U\bigl({\hat{G}(b)}\bigr)U^{\dagger}=U\bigl({U^{\dagger}G(b)U}\bigr)U^{\dagger}=G(b).

Since
FG
is a reference,

FG(a\otimes b)=FG\bigl({(a\otimes 1)\cdot(1\otimes b)}\bigr)=FG(a\otimes 1)\cdot FG(1\otimes b)=F(a)G(b).

Thus there exists a reference
FG
with the property required by Axiom
•
‣
1
.

Appendix D
Proofs for complements in the quantum setting

The lemmas below assume the quantum reference category (finite- or infinite-dimensional).
reference/pre-reference means quantum reference/pre-reference.

Lemma 41
.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
and
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
are complements,
then
G(\mathbf{B})
is the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
.
(The set of all operators that commute with all of
F(\mathbf{A})
.)

Proof.

Throughout this proof, let
\textstyle X^{\prime}
denote the commutant of
X
.
For any iso-reference
H:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{E}
,

\displaystyle x\in H(\mathbf{D})^{\prime}

\displaystyle\iff\forall e\in H(\mathbf{D}).\ xe=ex\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\iff}}\forall e\in H(\mathbf{D}).\ H^{-1}(x)H^{-1}(e)=H^{-1}(e)H^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle\iff\forall d\in\mathbf{D}.\ H^{-1}(x)d=dH^{-1}(x)\iff H^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\iff x\in H(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})

(17)

Here we repeatedly use that
H
is bijective as an iso-reference, and in
(*)
we also use the multiplicativity of
H^{-1}
.

Let
\textstyle\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}
denote the multiples of the identity, i.e.,
\{\alpha 1_{\mathbf{B}}:\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\}
.
The map
\pi:x\mapsto x\otimes 1
is an isomorphism of von Neumann algebras from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1

[
Tak79
, Corollary IV.1.5]
.
Thus
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}=\pi(\mathbf{A})=\{a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{B}}:a\in\mathbf{A}\}
.
Analogously,
\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{B}=\{1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes b:b\in\mathbf{B}\}
.

Then

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{B})^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\Bigl({FG\bigl({\{{a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{B}}:a\in\mathbf{A}}\}}\bigr)}\Bigr)^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:commutant.exchange}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}FG\bigl({\{{a\otimes 1_{\mathbf{B}}:a\in\mathbf{A}}\}^{\prime}}\bigr)=FG\bigl({({\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}})^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}FG{({\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}})}=FG{({\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathbf{B}})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:commutant.exchange}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}FG\bigl({\{{1_{\mathbf{A}}\otimes b:b\in\mathbf{B}}\}}\bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}G(\mathbf{B}).

Here
(*)
follows from Axioms
•
‣
1
and
•
‣
1
.
And
(**)
is by
[
Tak79
, Proposition IV.1.6 (ii)]
(with the left/right side of the tensor product exchanged).
∎

Lemma 42
.

If
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
and
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
are references and
F(\mathbf{A})=G(\mathbf{B})
, then
F,G
are equivalent.

Proof.

Since
F,G
are quantum references, they are injective (
Lemma 25
).
Let
I:=F^{-1}\circ G
.
This is well-defined because
F(\mathbf{A})=G(\mathbf{B})
, so
F^{-1}
is defined on the range of
G
.
It is elementary to check that
I
is linear, multiplicative, unital and preserves adjoints since
F
and
G
are and do.
We show that
I
is weak*-continuous:
Fix a net
x_{i}
of bounded operators and a bounded operator
x
such that
x_{i}\to x
(w.r.t. the weak*-topology).
Then
G(x_{i})\to G(x)
by
Lemma 39
.
So
F(F^{-1}(G(x_{i})))=G(x_{i})\to G(x)=F(F^{-1}(G(x)))
.
Since
F
is a reference, by
Lemma 39
,
F^{-1}(G(x_{i}))\to F^{-1}(G(x))
.
Thus
I(x_{i})\to I(x)
.
Since this holds for any net
x_{i}
,
I
is weak*-continuous.
Thus
I
is a reference.

Analogously, we have that
J:=G^{-1}\circ F
is a reference. Then
I\circ J=F^{-1}\circ G\circ G^{-1}\circ F=\mathit{id}
and analogously
J\circ I=\mathit{id}
. Thus
I
is an iso-reference.

Finally,
F\circ I=F\circ F^{-1}\circ G=G
. Thus
F
and
G
are equivalent.
∎

Note that only the lemmas above are used in the proof of
Theorem 1
in
Section 7.1
.
Thus in the proofs of the following lemmas we can already assume that
Theorem 1
holds, i.e., that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathit{quantum}}
has complements.

Lemma 43
.

The converse of
Lemma 41
holds.
That is, for references
F:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
and
G:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
, if
G(\mathbf{B})
is the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
, then
F
and
G
are complements.

Proof.

By Laws
4
,
4
,
F^{\complement},F
are complements.
Let
\mathbf{D}
denote the domain of
F^{\complement}
.
Then by
Lemma 41
,
F^{\complement}(\mathbf{D})
is the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
.
Since
G(\mathbf{B})
is also the commutant of
F(\mathbf{A})
by assumption,
F^{\complement}(\mathbf{D})=G(\mathbf{B})
.
By
Lemma 42
, this implies that
F^{\complement}
and
G
are equivalent.
Since
F,F^{\complement}
are complements, with Law
4
this implies that
F,G
are complements.
∎

Lemma 44
.

U:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a unit reference iff
U(\mathbf{A})=\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}
(the set of scalar multiples of
1_{\mathbf{B}}
).

Proof.

By definition,
U:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
is a unit reference iff
U
and
\mathit{id}:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{B}
are complements.
By Lemmas
41
and
43
, this holds iff
U(\mathbf{A})
is the commutant of
\mathit{id}(\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{B}
.
Since the commutant of
\mathbf{B}
is
\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}
, this holds iff
U(\mathbf{A})=\mathbb{C}1_{\mathbf{B}}
.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for lifting of quantum objects

E.1
Elementary objects,
Lemma 3

Proof.

We show (
ii
): If
A
is an isometry, then
A^{\dagger}A=1
by definition. Since
F
is a reference, this implies that
F(A)^{\dagger}F(A)=F(A^{\dagger}A)=F(1)=1
. Thus
F(A)
is an isometry.

We show (
i
): If
A
is unitary, then
A^{\dagger}A=1
and
AA^{\dagger}=1
by definition. Analogous to (
ii
), this implies that
F(A)^{\dagger}F(A)=1
and
F(A)F(A)^{\dagger}=1
. Thus
F(A)
is unitary.

We show (
iii
): If
A
is a projector, then
A^{\dagger}=A
and
A^{2}=A
.
Thus
F(A)^{\dagger}=F(A^{\dagger})=F(A)
and
F(A)^{2}=F(A^{2})=F(A)
. Thus
F(A)
is a projector.

We show (
iv
): By
Lemma 24
.

We show (
v
): If
A
is positive, there exists a
B
with
A=B^{\dagger}B
. Since
F
is a register,
F(A)=F(B^{\dagger}B)=F(B^{\dagger})F(B)=F(B)^{\dagger}F(B)\geq 0
. So
F(A)
is positive.
∎

E.2
Subspaces,
Lemma 4

Proof.

We show (
i
).
By
[
Con13
, comment after Definition II.3.1]
, the projector
P_{S}
onto
S
exists.
By
item iii
,
F(P_{S})
is a projector.
By
[
Con13
, Proposition II.3.2 (b)]
the image of a project is a closed subspace.
Thus
F({S})
, the image of
F(P_{S})
, is a closed subspace.

This shows (
i
).

We show (
ii
).
For any closed subspace
X
, if
P_{X}
denotes the projector onto
X
, then
P_{X^{\bot}}=1-P_{X}
.
(Proof:
1-P_{X}
is a projector. Thus
\operatorname{im}(1-P_{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ker P_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}({\operatorname{im}P_{X}})^{\bot}=X^{\bot}

where
(*)
follows by
[
Con13
, Proposition II.3.2 (b)]
and
(**)
by
[
Con13
, Definition II.3.1]
.
Thus
1-P_{X}
is the unique projector
P_{X^{\bot}}
onto
X^{\bot}
.)
Thus
F(S^{\bot})=\operatorname{im}F(P_{S^{\bot}})=\operatorname{im}F(1-P_{S})=\operatorname{im}1-F(P_{S})=(\operatorname{im}F(P_{S}))^{\bot}=F(P_{S})^{\bot}
.

This shows (
ii
).

We show (
iii
).
The projector
P_{\{{0}\}}
onto
\{0\}
is
0
. Thus
F\bigl({\{{0}\}}\bigr)=\operatorname{im}F(P_{\{{0}\}})=\operatorname{im}F(0)=\operatorname{im}0=\{{0}\}
.

This shows (
iii
).

We show (
iv
).
The projector
P_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{A}
is
1
. Thus
F(\mathcal{H}_{A})=\operatorname{im}F(P_{\mathcal{H}_{A}})=\operatorname{im}F(1)=\operatorname{im}1=\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

This shows (
iv
).

We show (
v
).

We have
F(S)\subseteq F(T)
iff
\operatorname{im}F(P_{S})\subseteq\operatorname{im}F(P_{T})
iff
F(P_{S})F(P_{T})=F(P_{S})

[
Con13
, Exercise II.3.6]
iff
F(P_{S}P_{T})=F(P_{S})
iff
P_{S}P_{T}=P_{S}
(
Lemma 25
) iff
S\subseteq T

[
Con13
, Exercise II.3.6]
.

This shows (
v
).

We show (
vi
).
Since
S\cap T
is the largest closed subspace contained in both
S,T
, (
vi
) can be equivalently stated as:
F
preserves greatest lower bounds (in the lattice of subspaces).
And this follows by (
v
).

This shows (
vi
).

We show (
vii
).
The fact that
S+T
is the smallest closed subspace containing
S,T
follows because it is a closed subspace (since
+
by our definition includes taking the closure of a subspace), and it is the smallest closed subspace because it is the closure of the smallest subspace containing
S,T
.

Then
F(S+T)=F(S)+F(T)
can be equivalently stated as:
F
preserves least upper bounds (in the lattice of subspaces).
And this follows by (
v
).

This shows (
vii
).

We show (
viii
).
By
Lemma 23
, there exists a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{C}
and a unitary
U:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
F(a)=U(a\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}
for all
a
.

Let
P_{S}
denote the projector onto
S
, and
P_{AS}
the projector onto
AS
. (Exists by
Lemma 13
.)

When writing
XYS
for operators
X,Y
and a subspace
A
, we mean
X(YS)
, i.e., the closure of the image of
X
of the closure of the image of
Y
of
S

(and not the closure of the image of
XY
of
S
).

Then

F(A)F(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}F(A)F(P_{S})\mathcal{H}_{B}=U(A\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}U(P_{S}\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}\mathcal{H}_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}U(A\otimes 1)(P_{S}\otimes 1)(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})

Here
(*)
is by definition of
F(S)
(application of a reference to a closed subspace).
And
(**)
since
U
is unitary, and thus
U^{\dagger}U=1
and
U^{\dagger}\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
And further

\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}U(AP_{S}\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})=U(AS\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})=U(P_{AS}\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}U(P_{AS}\otimes 1)(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C})

Here
(*)
follows from
Lemma 14
. (
S\otimes T
for subspaces
S,T
is defined as the closed span of
\{s\otimes t:s\in S,t\in T\}
).
And further

\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}U(P_{AS}\otimes 1)U^{\dagger}\mathcal{H}_{B}=F(P_{AS})\mathcal{H}_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}F(AS).

Here
(*)
since
U
is unitary, and thus
U^{\dagger}\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{C}
.
And
(**)
is by definition of
F(AS)
(application of a reference to a closed subspace).

Altogether
F(A)F(S)=F(AS)
, as claimed.
This shows (
viii
).

We show (
ix
).
Note that for commuting projectors,
\operatorname{im}PQ\subseteq\operatorname{im}P
,
\operatorname{im}PQ=\operatorname{im}QP\subseteq\operatorname{im}Q
.
And for
h\in\operatorname{im}P\cap\operatorname{im}Q
,
PQh=Ph=h
, hence
h\in\operatorname{im}PQ
.
It follows that

\operatorname{im}P\cap\operatorname{im}Q=\operatorname{im}PQ\qquad\text{for commuting projectors }P,Q

(18)

We have

F(S)\cap G(V)=\operatorname{im}F(P_{S})\cap\operatorname{im}G(P_{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{im}F(P_{S})G(P_{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}F(P_{S})\operatorname{im}G(P_{V})=F(P_{S})G(V).

Here
(*)
follows from the (
18
) and the fact that
F(P_{S}),G(P_{V})
commute because
F,G
are disjoint.
And
(**)
is simply a special case of the fact that
\operatorname{im}f\circ g=f(\operatorname{im}g)
for arbitrary functions
f,g
.
∎

E.3
Mixed states,
Lemma 5

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we abbreviate
\rho^{*}:=\rho_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\rho_{n}
and
F^{*}:=({F_{1},\dots,F_{n}})
. Note that
F^{*}
is an iso-reference since
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
is a partition by assumption.
And
{F_{1}(\rho_{1})\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}\dots\otimes_{\mathfrak{m}}F_{n}(\rho_{n})}=F^{*}(\rho^{*})
by definition.
Since
F^{*}
is an iso-reference, by
Lemma 26
,
F^{*}(\rho^{*})=U\rho^{*}U^{\dagger}
for some unitary
U
that we fix throughout the proof as well.

We show (
ii
).
By
Lemma 32
,
\rho^{*}
is a trace-class operator and
\operatorname{tr}\rho^{*}=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}\cdots\operatorname{tr}\rho_{n}
.
By
[
Con00
, Theorem 18.11 (a)]
, this implies that
F^{*}(\rho^{*})=U\rho^{*}U^{\dagger}
is trace-class, and
\operatorname{tr}F^{*}(\rho^{*})=\operatorname{tr}U\rho^{*}U^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\operatorname{tr}\rho^{*}=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}\cdots\operatorname{tr}\rho_{n}
.
(Here
(*)
is by
item i
.)
